Summary

“I think you should be my caddie,” Lena says, on her sixth day in Midvale. She realizes maybe her phrasing isn’t entirely clear. “What I mean is, I want you to be my caddie.”

“I am your caddie,” Kara says, then looks up from where she’s been reading the grass, a teasing smile on her lips. “Right now, even.”

or

Lena finds her new caddie on a golf course in the middle of nowhere, and then, she finally starts winning trophies.

Notes

Golf AU has been spoiling around in my drafts for years and finally she arrives to the light of day. A couple big important notes:

1) Yes, women's golf is now very famous and cool and there's no misogyny anymore and maybe world peace. In this fic, the association is called the WPGA, but in real life it's the LPGA. It's just that I hate the term ladies. Please go watch any and all LPGA events and support women.
2) You do not need to understand golf but I do think maybe you should check it out - if you ignore the old white men it isn't so bad. I promise.

This fic's beta is lynnearlington, who is as perfect and wonderful and fastidious as ever. This fic's playlist can be found right here.

like a river connects to the ocean

by mooosicaldreamz

https://archiveofourown.org/works/48474565

    Chapter 1: rip me out again

    WASHINGTON, UNITED STATES - MIDVALE MUNICIPAL GOLF COURSE

    “Maybe you should swing the three,” comes a voice from behind her. Lena is in the middle of squaring up to the ball with her driver. It’s cool and misty on Midvale’s well-loved municipal golf course. Well-loved means there are divots tracked all over the forward tee box, and there’s a kid sprinting up along his dad’s golf cart on the next hole over. It’s the exact kind of out-and-away place that Lena had used to avoid like the plague. Her mother’s voice in her ear: you’ll only be better if you challenge yourself, Lena. 

    For the last several months, it was the only kind of course she could convince herself to play - one where no one would take a second look at her.

    She turns back and looks at the woman behind her. The caddie is tall, blonde, limbs long, and settled a solid eight feet back. She’s wearing the club’s red polo with navy golf pants and a matching navy Ping hat, her hands in her pockets. The bib is an obnoxious red thing with a palm tree on it.

    Why this course even has a caddie program is beyond Lena - maybe gainful employment for area teens? All the same, carrying and managing her bag was not her favorite thing, so any course with caddies immediately raised her interest. None of them had bothered giving advice before, though.

    “I’m sorry?” Lena asks, stepping out of her stance and eyeing the woman. The caddie cocks her head and then looks past Lena down the fairway.

    “Try your three,” the woman says. “You look like you’re gonna bomb this ball into the lake with that driver.”

    “What’s the average drive on this hole?” Lena asks. The caddie shrugs, her hands still tucked into her pockets.

    “About 220,” the caddie says.

    “My average drive is 250, and considering the pin is at 310, I think I’ll be alright,” Lena says. “So the driver would, in fact, be satisfactory.”

    The caddie finally withdraws her hands from her pockets and raises them up, shaking her head.

    “Of course,” she says. “I’m sorry. You know best.”

    Lena considers it settled. She lines up again with the ball, takes one glance toward the pin, and swings.

    She’s been playing golf since she was five years old, and her swing has changed plenty over time. But it's a rote physical memory at this point - the ping of the club on the ball is beautiful, and the ball soars while she holds her pose. She watches as it pushes on toward the hole, and then, eventually -

    The caddie is already fishing out a new ball from her bib pocket and extending it into Lena’s field of vision when the ball splashes dramatically into the water. 

    “Breakfast ball?” the caddie asks, her voice gentle. Lena takes the ball and turns to eye the caddie, who doesn’t quite cow under her glare as Jack might’ve. “It’s another term for mulligan?”

    Lena glares even harder.

    “My three wood, if you’d please?” Lena says, waiting for it to arrive in her hand before she lines up, and swings. It hits the green with a solid thunk, a bit of grass flying up after it. The caddie doesn’t say anything except to take the three wood back and hoist the bag up onto her shoulders. 

    -

    On the tenth hole, the caddie speaks again, after almost an hour of simply handing Lena clubs and Lena accepting them without so much as a thank you . Her shots have been the kind of perfectly fine that she’s started to find absolutely boring in this interminable six months away.

    “It breaks left,” the caddie comments softly when she hands over Lena’s putter. Lena sees fit to ignore her. Sure, the girl had been able to interpret correctly what Lena’s swings would be like in terms of distance, but Lena made her money on greens. She had made millions, in fact. Mixed in with a fair bit of stubbornness, Lena had resolved after the first hole to ignore any and all advice.

    The putt hooks the lip of the hole and curls left. She nearly smashes her putter into the springy grass beneath her feet, but tries to reign it in, tries to think of those absurd breathing exercises that her therapist wants her to do whenever she thinks about destroying something. Create, Lena. When it hurts, create. Whatever the hell that means.

    The caddie says nothing when Lena taps it in. When they climb into the cart, she nearly screams when she sees the putt count the caddie’s been keeping on the back of the scorecard, clipped to the wheel.

    -

    Her caddie is gorgeous. Lena knew this the moment she set eyes on the girl; Lena is not blind and she is also very gay. And even though her caddie is dressed in as unrevealing an outfit as a female caddie could possibly wear, the drunk men on the sixteenth make sure to loudly bet on whoever could bag the hot blonde.

    Lena is idling next to their golf cart and considering snapping one of their cheap clubs when the caddie leans into her field of vision and looks her in the face.

    “Are you okay?” she asks. Lena looks back at her. The girl doesn’t look like she’s making fun or like she’s dying to provoke a reaction from the Lena Luthor - though surely the caddie must know who she is. She looks, genuinely, as though she is concerned for Lena in this moment. Lena draws her posture up accordingly, tilting her chin upward, giving a short nod. This does not seem to satisfy the caddie one bit, because she steps in Lena’s view, blocking her from seeing those awful boys’ attempt to drive. She’s closer than anyone has been in months, it feels like.

    “Do you need to take a break?” the caddie says, nodding to a small shed about fifty yards away, tucked into the trees along the side of the hole. It has a prominent sign stating employees only. 

    “I’m fine,” Lena says, and she can feel her teeth grind as she says it. “Please move.”

    “Hey, miss, would you mind?” one of the golfers calls, and Lena watches as a golf ball hits the back of the caddie’s white tennis shoes and rolls sideways. The caddie glances down at it with a roll of her eyes, her hands still placed protectively on Lena’s golf bag. Giggles are rising up from the men assembled in the tee box. “Think you might need to bend over to grab that.”

    Lena takes the club in her hand, her driver, and reaches out on a one-handed, lefty swing to smack the ball the twenty yards between them. It thunks loudly into one of the men’s shins, and he howls.

    “Okay,” the caddie says, hoisting Lena’s golf bag up onto her shoulder and reaching for Lena’s elbow. “Come on.”

    “What do you mean, come on, ” Lena says, resisting the caddie’s pull and eyeing the man pulling up his pant leg to show a bright red mark already forming on his shins. That’s good. If she and Lex ever reconcile long enough to play a round of trick shot, she can suggest hitting each other in the shin left-handed. He’d shank it far enough he might as well hit himself again.

    “If you stand there and stare them down they might stare back long enough to realize who assaulted them, maybe,” the caddie says, tugging more firmly at Lena’s elbow. She’s strong, which makes sense - Lena sees the sleeve of her polo tighten up around her bicep. But the realization of what she’s saying comes in three waves: one, this caddie certainly knows who Lena is and two, she’s right, and three, she doesn’t want Lena to be recognized. So Lena lets herself be pulled.

    -

    The caddie sits her down on top of a massive riding lawnmower and then simply leans against the wall and looks upward at the flickering light. It’s surprisingly chilly in the windowless room.

    Lena is trying to do the breathing thing, her hands clenching and unclenching with her chest movements, but it’s hard. The room feels small around her, and the presence of another person only makes her feel like she’s vibrating out of her skin. The fear over them recognizing her, blabbing to someone about how that bitch Lena Luthor attacked them, it feels like it’s coming down around her all over again.

    Maybe it feels like it never stopped coming down around her. She can’t remember a time when it hadn’t felt like that, really, not when she was an amateur and certainly not since Pebble Beach and the Maserati Incident. 

    “You want some water?” the caddie asks, sticking a water bottle into Lena’s space. Lena tries to wave her away. “No, so, I wasn’t asking so much as telling. Drink some water.”

    Lena takes it then, the rhythmic and simple movements of cracking open the water bottle and drinking sending a wave of calm through her. She drinks two-thirds of it fast, and then hands it back. When she finally looks up again, rubbing at the sweat on her forehead and pushing up her visor, the caddie is looking at her flatly. There’s neither sympathy nor annoyance in her expression.

    “What’s your name?” Lena asks. The caddie cocks her head and smiles. A bright, easy smile that Lena feels flip over in her stomach. 

    “Kara.”

    -

    “How’d you know I’d hit the lake?” Lena asks, the next day. Kara is leaning against their golf cart, with the red bib draping over her frame. When she had walked into the clubhouse and asked for a caddie for her round, Kara had wandered around the corner with a granola bar half in her mouth. Lena had been hoping, a little bit.

    Kara has on sunglasses today, because the sun is bearing down on Midvale in the middle of December. She’s also gobbling up a pack of peanuts so loudly that Lena forgets about focusing on the ball in front of her.

    “You looked pissed,” Kara says, shrugging and chewing.

    “And that has exactly what to do with the game of golf?”

    Kara shrugs again, shoving more peanuts in her mouth and talking around them. “Being a good caddie is about the golfer as much as it’s about the course. More so, maybe.” 

    “Has anyone ever told you you have terrible manners?” Lena asks. Kara reaches up and adjusts her sunglasses, flashing a grin.

    “Hit your three,” Kara says. “Let’s own it today. Teamwork makes the dream work and all that.”

    Instinct tells her to hit her driver again. Maybe this time she can hit a perfect draw that won’t end up in the water or hit it softer with a slight fade that could end up hole high in the fairway, but Kara’s smile feels like it’s burning through her and she finds herself taking the proffered three wood from Kara’s hand instead. Lena lines up, tries not to think of anything at all really and just swings. The ball rainbows beautifully towards the green with enough backspin that it drops on a dime with a perfect line towards the pin and a longer carry than she’s hit any of her woods in months. Kara lets out a whoop . It’s a good hit. A great hit, even if it’s just this forgiving par four on a public course. When she turns to join Kara at the golf cart, the other woman is imitating riding a bull waving her hand up in the air and Lena isn’t quick enough to suppress her smile. 

    -

    On Lena’s third day in Midvale, it’s drizzling. The little clubhouse is quiet. The bored teen working the check-in desk checks something on her computer screen, glances outside, and then shrugs before handing over the keys to Lena’s golf cart. She doesn’t pay Lena a second glance, immediately picking up her phone before Lena’s even walked away from the counter.

    Kara is waiting at the golf cart when Lena gets outside, a massive umbrella held over her head. She’s wearing a horrifically orange hat.

    “Good morning,” Kara says cheerfully, reaching for Lena’s bag. Lena relinquishes it to her, watching as Kara starts poking at the clubs to check them over. It’s a little cold, and it makes Lena think of all the times she worked out on the small chipping green at Luthor Manor in the small days before winter descended.

    “Morning,” Lena returns, heading to the cart. Kara follows after, setting it in its spot on the back of the cart and strapping it in. When Kara settles onto the seat next to her, she starts out down the path toward the first tee. 

    “Feeling okay today?” Kara asks, soft over the patter of rain on the roof of the cart. 

    Lena looks over to find only genuine interest in Kara’s expression. She’s not really sure how to answer the question, but takes stock of herself and nods shortly. It makes Kara smile, her eyebrows lifting as she nods back. 

    “The grounds guys said that the rain has sort of pooled on parts of the green on one,” Kara says. “The drainage is bad, I guess.”

    “Near the hole?” Lena asks. Kara shifts next to her, their jackets brushing wetly against each other. Somehow, it still feels like Lena can feel the warmth of Kara’s skin.

    “Surrounds it like a moat, per Jason,” Kara says. “So, I guess you’ll just have to chip it in or putt really hard?”

    Lena hums in acknowledgment. She’s overputted enough holes to know she’s at least capable of one of those. As they slide to a stop next to the tee box, Kara hops out before Lena’s even put the thing in park. She comes careening around the front of the cart, flinging the umbrella open and extending it out so it covers the space between them, its edge catching on the roof of the golf cart.

    “Milady,” Kara says, doing a strange little attempt at a bow that makes Lena laugh, loudly. It’s the loudest thing Lena’s heard all morning. 

    She had woken up before her alarm, showered, let her hair air dry while she read over emails from Sam about sponsors and their offers, got in her rental car after grabbing a coffee from the lobby, and drove to Midvale Municipal. Somehow, in the quiet and relative gloom of the morning, Lena feels something spark to life. 

    Kara smiles back at her with a broad grin, one that Lena’s noticed she only throws out when particularly joyful. Yesterday, as they grabbed lunch at the extremely bad cafe in the small clubhouse, she had seen a dog wearing a raincoat on her Twitter feed that provoked the same reaction. On the golf course before that, their round had been interrupted by a child picking up Lena’s ball on the side of the fairway and Kara had laughed and smiled about that as well. 

    Sometimes, with Jack, Lena had joked around, especially during practice rounds or travel between tournaments. On occasion, she and Lex managed to connect through the resentment swirling between them and laughed their way through dinner and drinks. Lillian was not known for smiling or laughing or joking. When it wasn’t about golf, Sam could make her snort laugh.

    “You are an idiot,” Lena says, but there’s an unstoppable lift to her mouth she can’t stop despite the eye roll she manages. She places her hand in Kara’s extended one, warmth blooming all the way up her arm, and she steps out onto the path in the small space under the umbrella. “But thank you.”

    -

    Kara texts her the next day at around five. 

    i know ur probably tired of me by now, but do u want to get dinner at halfstack? its a diner near ur hotel

    Followed by:

    this is kara danvers, btw. ur fave caddie. “moron of the highest level” per u.

    Lena laughs - she had watched Kara put her number in Lena’s phone on the tenth tee box earlier in the day. Even if she had wanted to delete the number, she wouldn’t have quite made her way around to it, especially after their long round on the course and lunch after.

    Halfstack is indeed a diner near Lena’s hotel - she doesn’t even have to drive, it’s so close. There are only so many clothes Lena has with her that aren’t golf-related or meant for lounging around her hotel room in silence, but she scrounges up a nicer long-sleeve pullover and pants. She doesn’t bother investigating the urge to dress nicely.

    Of course, Kara is wearing a NASCAR t-shirt with Jeff Gordon, and for some unknown and likely terrible reason, Jar-Jar Binks on it. But her hair is down in loose waves, and the jacket overtop the shirt is a nice little bomber number. On the other hand, she’s invited Lena to a diner that only serves breakfast, and she can apparently eat an absurd amount of pancakes at a time.

    “Is that even safe?” Lena asks, after Kara has fit a 10-layer slice of pancake tower into her mouth. 

    “I can unhinge my jaw,” Kara says, her mouth half-full. It’s a miracle Lena can even understand her. “Like a snake.”

    It makes Lena laugh so loud that half the diner turns around to look, and the second best part is that Lena doesn’t even think to care. The best part is Kara’s grin, pancake bits escaping her jaws as she tries to rein in her own laughter. 

    -

    “Why have you moved into a Hampton Inn in Midvale, Washington?” Sam asks. Lena has only just made it in the door after a long day and night.

    “I only extended my stay by three days,” Lena says, rolling her eyes. 

    “You canceled every round I painstakingly scheduled for you at every semi-reputable golf course in the tri-county area,” Sam says, sounding quite huffy. Lena can imagine her sitting in her home office in the dark. “And then you extended your stay by three days.”

    “I’m pretty sure if I could cancel them online, you could schedule them online,” Lena points out. Sam makes an affronted noise. “I’m fine. I’ve been golfing at a local course.”

    There’s a few moments of silence as Sam seems to try to suss out Lena’s tone.

    “Are you actually fine?” Sam asks. It’s typical Sam, an island in the sea of Lena’s life over the last few years. Lillian had hired her to manage Lena after a particularly vicious fight over whether they should accept a sponsorship from Qatar Airways - it had probably been meant to be a punishment, proof that Lillian didn’t care enough to manage Lena if she was going to be intransigent. But Sam had been a gift, really. Sam had been the one to come get her at the police station after her arrest; Sam had been the one who fed her for ten days while she hid out in her house; Sam had been the one to yell at the tour for three straight hours after she had received her suspension terms. There’s no one in the world she trusts more. There’s no one in the world she wants to let down less.

    “Well, you haven’t seen me on Twitter smashing up a Ferrari or anything, have you?” Lena asks.

    “That is not an active measurement of how fine you are,” Sam says. “Look, I’m just saying that if you want to pull out of the first few tournaments of the year so you can have more time to get your feet back under you - ”

    “I’ve had six months to get my feet back under me,” Lena says. “I’m good.”

    She isn’t sure that that’s true, but it feels like she’s starting to find the path to being and feeling good. Playing on Midvale’s shitty course is helping her find the things she likes about golf, fresh, for the first time since she was maybe twelve. She likes the chess moves of it, the choices laid out before her at the tee, finding a hundred different ways to get to the flag. Left to her own devices, without Lex or Lillian or even Jack watching, she finds herself looking forward to another round. And she doesn’t worry about being judged or measured when Kara is the one following after her through the course. She doesn’t resent Kara when she suggests a path to the green. She doesn’t get angry. 

    “We could pull you back to just majors,” Sam suggests, and the very thought of the majors makes Lena’s skin crawl.

    “No,” Lena says, dropping onto her bed and looking out on the Hampton Inn’s parking lot. There’s a small family unloading an overpacked minivan. “No, I want to play. I told you I was good and I am.”

    “Okay,” Sam says, though she sounds entirely doubtful. 

    “I’m just - working on something,” Lena says. “I’m feeling good.”

    “Okay,” Sam sighs. “Well, since you’re still insistent on barreling through the season, have you looked over the list of caddies I sent you for Hawaii?”

    “No, I haven’t had the chance,” Lena says, glancing over at her laptop. She hadn’t thought to check her emails this morning before heading out the door. 

    “Well, you have some time, but I thought you’d like to get connected and working sooner rather than later,” Sam says, quite gently. “And as far as I know, Jack is still available, too, if you wanted to - rehire him for the sixth time?”

    “God no,” Lena says, prompting a laugh from Sam. “No, I’ll figure it out. Thank you for putting a list together.”

    “No problem,” Sam says. “Guess what Ruby did today? You’re not going to believe it.”

    “What?” Lena asks, laughing.

    “She set her chem lab on fire!” Sam yelps.

    By the time Lena hangs up with Sam, it’s almost midnight. She has another round scheduled tomorrow morning with Kara - the course has started auto-assigning her beforehand. It’s quiet in her hotel room, aside from occasional thumps from adjoining rooms.

    When she checks her texts, empty and barren as they have been for the last several months, there’s one from Kara. It’s a meme of a cat in a banana suit, photoshopped to look like it’s crying. And then, five minutes later: see you tomorrow. sleep well.

    And Lena does.

    -

    Somewhere between the third and fourth holes, it occurs to her. By the time she reaches the eleventh, it’s all she can think about. She says it on the fourteenth.

    “I think you should be my caddie,” Lena says, on her sixth day in Midvale. Kara is crouched next to Lena on the green, eyeing the path downslope with her hands in her bib pockets. She realizes maybe her phrasing isn’t entirely clear. “What I mean is, I want you to be my caddie.”

    “I am your caddie,” Kara says, then looks up from where she’s been reading the grass, a teasing smile on her lips. “Right now, even.”

    “Yes, I’m aware,” Lena says with an eye roll that has Kara’s smile blooming further onto her face. “That’s not what I meant.” 

    “What did you mean?” 

    “My suspension is almost up,” Lena says. Kara cocks her head as she stands up.

    “I believe the wording of your press release was taking the time for myself, not suspension,” Kara says, a cautious but still teasing note to her tone. 

    “The time I’ve carved out for myself was very strongly suggested by the tour,” Lena says. Kara nods, then kneels down again to get a closer look at the green. “And now that it’s over, I’d like you to be my caddie.”

    “Once again, I am caddieing for you at this very moment,” Kara says. “It looks like someone stomped this to a flat overnight or something.”

    For all of Kara’s good qualities she seems to have become considerably denser overnight. 

    “I’ve come to like you, somehow,” Lena says and this , at least, makes Kara pause what she’s doing and look up, squinting into the sun and smiling a little. She forgot her sunglasses today, allegedly because her sister stole them. Lena gets the sense that Kara is just forgetful. Or she broke them. That’s why this feels right. She gets all sorts of senses about Kara, from just six days and eight rounds of golf and one trip to an abominable diner that Kara had claimed was the best place in the whole world. She feels like, too, that Kara has all sorts of senses about her.

    “Well, gee, thanks,” Kara says with a soft chuckle. “I like you too.” 

    Lena nods, feeling as if that’s maybe decided it then, but Kara just turns back towards the green and starts to mumble, “I think it’s going to play to the right.” 

    She clears her throat a little and glances back up at Lena, a strange look on her face.

    “Would you want to - ”

    “I want you to caddie for me on the tour,” Lena interrupts, voice firm and clear. It successfully pulls Kara’s attention back to the matter at hand. “You can have half my winnings.”

    Kara’s mouth shuts, then opens again, then shuts. The startled look in Kara’s eyes isn’t one Lena’s cataloged yet and she takes a moment to enjoy it, the way Kara blinks slowly and seems to process everything all at once. 

    “We haven’t even done this on a real golf course, free of infirm grass,” Kara says, after a second, looking back down at the green and plucking at the admittedly terrible grass. “I haven’t even done this with you in anything resembling a competition.”

    “You can have three quarters of my winnings,” Lena says, unconcerned with the objections Kara’s offering up. Lena’s made the decision - the moment it had sprung into her head, it felt undeniably correct. Kara splutters, finally vaulting herself up off the ground and looking Lena in the eye, her head dipping just the littlest bit to get under the rim of Lena’s visor.

    “Are you on coke or something?”

    “No,” Lena says. Her putter feels loose in her hand, light, and Kara is looking more and more alarmed by the moment. “That’s not really my style.” 

    Kara just stares at her, the silence drawing out between them longer and longer until finally she says, “I don’t want three quarters of your winnings - you don’t need to bribe me or something to caddie for you. I’m just trying to say that’s a big decision and - ” Lena’s already pressing past her to approach her putt, knowing Kara will eventually realize the finality of the situation. “Lena, wait, listen to me for a second.” 

    “What are you thinking here?” Lena asks, eyeing the patch of grass between her ball and the hole. “If you think it’s flat I’ll swing straight.”

    Kara pauses, clearly uncertain about her place in life at the moment. Lena doesn’t glance backwards, just lines up her putter and the ball and thinks through the motion in her head. Slight pull back, tap, follow through. Easy as pie. When she gets no response, Lena glances back. Kara is standing there, mouth open, her eyes on Lena’s hands on the club. They flit up to Lena’s face, then to the hole, and then back to Lena’s face. Her eyes are the kind of blue people write whole books about. Something a little Helen of Troy in her face. 

    “I think it’s got a little bit of a break,” Kara says, faintly, after a moment. “To the right.”

    Lena corrects left. The ball makes that ever-satisfying noise when it sinks in, a plucky noise that always brings a wave of relief over Lena. Kara hums a little. 

    “You’d really abandon me to another caddie this close to the beginning of the season?” Lena says, attempting another approach. 

    Kara’s cheeks look a little flushed, her smile rueful. “This is a municipal course in Midvale. And it’s just us here,” Kara says, gesturing around them. “It’s not exactly Augusta, is it?”

    Lena cocks her head, eyes evaluating Kara a moment. “I thought being a caddie was about the golfer as much as it was about the course,” she says. 

    It’s clear the echo of Kara’s own words does the trick or maybe it’s that she does know Lena as much as Lena suspects and understands the decision has already been made, maybe even before they’d even set eyes on each other. Kara’s shoulders drop down a bit, her smile soft. 

    “You want me to caddie for you,” Kara says matter-of-factly as she reaches to take Lena’s putter from her. Their hands clasp over each other on the grip, sweaty and sticky, as Lena holds on a second longer than she normally would. 

    “I thought you already were,” Lena says, smile on her face. “Right now, even.”

    Kara smiles back. 

    -

    HAWAII, UNITED STATES - PLANTATION COURSE AT KAPALUA - TOURNAMENT OF CHAMPIONS

    “You’re telling me you hired a girl from a public golf course in Midvale, Washington to be your professional caddie,” Sam says, rage in her eyes. Kara is carrying Lena’s club bag, rolling two suitcases, and grinning happily at the front desk agent of the hotel, checking in for the two of them.

    “Yep,” Lena says. More than one person passing in the lobby stares at her as they pass by, all of them afraid to stop and say hello. She had thought jeans, a sweatshirt, and a Nike hat would hide her well enough, but apparently not. 

    “You know, Lena, you don’t have to offer people ten percent of your earnings just because they’re good in bed,” Sam mutters, eyeing Kara as she laughs at something the agent is saying, taking a veritable mountain of paperwork from the man. 

    “We aren’t sleeping together,” Lena says, rolling her eyes and a little offended at the suggestion. 

    “Oh, so you’ve just lost it,” Sam says. “Good to know.”

    “I haven’t lost it,” Lena says. “I told you, I’m onto something - ”

    “And it was too much to hope, clearly, that what you were on was her strap.”

    “Sam.”

    “Lena. The girl is more climbable than a jungle gym.”

    “Be that as it may - ”

    “Oh, so you admit it! Good, I’m glad that your therapy for your emotional repression is working.”

    “I hate you.”

    “Another successful confession! Maybe I should transfer to your therapist, she’s a miracle worker.”

    Kara has turned back from the counter, weaving through the check-in traffic back to where Lena and Sam are standing and managing not to take anyone out with the masses of bags attached to her body. Lena had informed Kara more than once that she was capable of wheeling her own smaller suitcase at the least, but Kara had dutifully insisted she handle it all on her own. 

    “Okay, I think I’ve got everything,” Kara says, handing over the thick sheaf of papers, pamphlets, and dangling lanyards and adjusting the arrangement of bags in her perimeter. “Hi.”

    “Kara, this is Sam Arias,” Lena says. Kara nods, reaching forward to shake Sam’s hand. “She’s my agent and manager. On occasion she is also my very bad coach.”

    “Rude,” Sam says, but puts on an impressively professional expression for Kara considering her incredulity moments earlier. “Nice to meet you, Kara.”

    “Same,” Kara says, grinning widely and adjusting the hat on her head. “So. When do we get to the swinging?”

    -

    “I’m thinking you want to land here,” Kara says, circling a spot on the very edge of the fairway toward the pond. She looks up from her yardage book to survey the course ahead of them once more. “I don’t like this valley. But I think a slice is best, so you can take distance in the air.”

    The player ahead of them - Siobhan Smythe - is in the middle of taking her swing from the aforementioned valley. Kara isn’t even watching, her back turned as she leans against their golf cart and jumbles holding Lena’s yardage book and the course map she’s scanned and blown up to a massive size. Siobhan hits her shot on the valley, but it rolls way right from the pin and then onto the back left, almost off the back side of the green.

    “Fuck!” Siobhan shouts, her caddie laughing behind her. 

    “Why is she so much the worst?” Sam asks. She’s sipping a drink from her seat in the cart. Lena hums her agreement, watching as Siobhan and her caddie begin arguing with each other in a jumble of angry words. 

    As Kara eyes the clubs in Lena’s bag, Sam turns to her, an inquisitive look on her face Lena knows does not bode well. “So, Kara, tell me. How did you come into caddieing?” 

    Eyeing Lena’s driver for a prolonged second, Kara grabs it and hefts it out of the bag to hand towards Lena. “Well, I hate golf,” she answers as if that makes any kind of sense. 

    Lena’s arched brow must convey her disbelief because Kara lets out a soft chuckle and shrugs a shoulder. Though her answer is for Sam, she continues to look at Lena. “I won like, six NCAA tournaments my freshman year of college and that was enough for me to know I didn’t like it. So I quit.” Lena can’t help the way her mouth drops open a bit at this new tidbit of information. 

    “Excuse me?” It’s quiet enough Sam doesn’t hear her, but that doesn’t seem to matter because Sam says the exact same thing except her excuse me is shouted as she chokes through a sip of her drink. 

    “You gonna take him, or what?” Kara asks, bemused, as she continues to hold out Lena’s driver in her direction.

    Lena takes the offered club, watching as Sam stares open-mouthed at Kara. Kara isn’t paying any attention, picking her head up to watch as Siobhan and her caddie hop in their cart to go pushing up the hill toward their wandering ball. She looks back at Lena, her hands on her hips, and grins.

    “Feeling okay?” Kara asks quietly, just for Lena as she had most mornings in Midvale. Lena nods. Kara offers up both her hands in fists, halfway between them. They’re close, so Lena doesn’t have to reach much difference to press their knuckles together. It’s so strange, so foreign to her. But Lena likes it. Feels comfortable, more comfortable than she ever has on a golf course. “Good. Let’s mash some potatoes.” 

    It makes Lena want to laugh, but she doesn’t want to spook Sam, so she swallows the sound into a smile and takes a ball from where Kara’s offering her one. 

    “Kara, just to be clear, you’re saying you quit golf even though you were quite good at it,” Sam says, as Kara sits on the cart next to her and Lena goes to place her tee. 

    “Yep,” Kara says. “It’s boring. Like calculus. But as a sport.”

    Way down the course, Siobhan is chipping toward the hole, and lets out an absolutely feral scream when her chip goes careening back down the vaulted green, settling in the rough toward the lip.

    “She really is the worst,” Kara says, turning to look at Sam. “Did we get randomly assigned to swing together?” 

    “I’m sure they thought it’d be a good test of my patience,” Lena says, watching the ball on the tee and miming a swing back. The whole world falls silent around her, Kara and Sam quiet, Siobhan’s histrionics nothingness. She twists, pulls through, the ball pings, and then she pulls her head up a second after it’s gone. When she looks, it’s falling rightward, thunking once and then twice onto the very edge of the fairway, far from the line set by Siobhan. 

    “Hell yeah,” Kara says, coming up on Lena’s side with her bag. It’s all-black, fresh, stamped with LENA LUTHOR and both a US and Irish flag. Nike had shipped along her new apparel and shoes this morning, along with Kara’s. Lena’s decided to try to break it all in early on their practice walkthroughs. But Kara has shown up in what appears to be a mismatched collection of odd gear: a beanie printed with the word caddie, a bright yellow polo with a chevron down its front, green shorts that match her hat. She’s at least got on the Nike Roshes that had been sent along in her care package. 

    Sam is already bombing her way down the hill toward Lena’s ball, so Kara and Lena start their walk down. 

    “You didn’t tell me you had played,” Lena says, offhand. She and Jack hadn’t really used to make much in the way of conversation even in practice rounds - it was mostly Lena demanding clubs and Jack handing them over dutifully. After rounds they’d just go to their hotel room and sit there, watching Trackman readouts and occasionally kissing. But Kara is talkative, easy, a rebounding sort of surface. Lena likes her.

    “You never asked,” Kara says. “You didn’t even ask if I had a job before you offered me this one. Watch out for the frog.”

    There is indeed a frog hopping across the path they’re walking. Kara’s hand arrives on the small of her back, moving her around the obstacle before it slides away. 

    “I’m sorry,” Lena says. “I - realize I sound rather selfish now.”

    “Oh, very,” Kara says in the same casual tone she reports yardage in. “Lucky for you I’m out of a job right now anyway.”

    When nothing else is forthcoming, Lena summons her manners to ask a prodding, “Oh?” 

    Kara hums. “I used to be a duckling in the editorial department at the National City Tribune , but I was laid off in the summer. Went home, decided to pick up some work at Midvale. The owner of the course gave me tons of hours, and then I just sort of got stuck there.”

    “So you’re a writer,” Lena says, processing somewhat slowly. She can imagine Lillian’s sneer. She’s not sure what Lillian would detest more, a writer or a journalist . 

    “Reporter,” Kara says. “Would-be, I guess. I was churning up a little article pitch to Golf.com, a caddieing column or something. But then you offered me this, and this is way more fun.”

    “Even though you hate golf?” Lena asks. They’ve made it to her ball, burrowed a little into the thin thatch of rough next to the cart path, where Sam is eating gummy worms and watching them. Kara sets the bag down. 

    “I’m not golfing,” she says. “You are.” 

    Lena eyes Sam who has looked away to fish another drink out of the cooler in the back of her cart. “And that’s different.” 

    “Well, sure. I hate golf, but I like you, remember?” Kara says like that’s a normal thing you say to people in the middle of a round of golf. “Plus, we’re in Hawaii. I can go to a luau at our hotel and drink mai tais. It’s practically a vacation.”

    “I’ve never once thought of this job as a vacation,” Lena says, laughing. She can’t help it. It’s crazy, what Kara’s saying, the loping way she walks, the easy way she pulls Lena’s yardage book from her back pocket and glances up at the sky while she sifts through Lena’s bag for whatever club she wants Lena to swing. 

    Even now, the careful way she fingers through the clubs in Lena’s bag before landing on an iron to hoist out and hand towards Lena. 

    “I mean, I’m sure talking to reporters and having people up your ass about sponsorships and traveling and the pressure of being a prodigy do actually suck,” Kara says. “But golf is just a stick and a ball and a hole. Best advice I ever had was to remember that golf is relaxing.”

    “Golf is relaxing,” Lena repeats skeptically, shifting her grip on the club and watching Kara. Kara nods, grinning. 

    “It’s just a game. For fun.” 

    Lena barks out a laugh, having not thought of golf that way in what feels like a lifetime. “Just a game.” 

    “Just keep telling yourself that,” Kara says, her hand pushing lightly at Lena’s hip. “I’m thinking we aim for - see the weird plant back there? That’s the line I like. You?”

    Lena looks to the back right of the green where there is indeed a very strange looking plant. It seems like a sensible line considering the leftward slope of the surface underneath it headed towards the pin. 

    “Sounds good to me.”

    When Lena finally lines up for her shot, clear and clean, unobscured by the hill Siobhan had had to work her way up from the valley, she closes her eyes for half a second. Golf is relaxing. She nails it.

    -

    Kara and Lena have adjoining rooms. She hadn’t thought about it when she had told Sam to book for two, that she had found a caddie. But now, it’s a mix of comforting and odd. It feels like her own room is caving in, really, and so she’s spent the last ten minutes looking at the door.

    Her bed has been consumed with sponsorship things being sent to her over the past few days - Bose headphones, Hublot watches, Nike shoes, three whole cases of Powerade, four different putter grips from Super Stroke, and an amusing box of gear from Stanhope University, addressed to Kara through Lena. By the time the Golf channel on television switches over to covering her upcoming return, reviewing the circumstances which lead her to here -

    She knocks on the door, with the Stanhope box in hand. It takes a couple seconds, but it opens, Kara wearing a tank top and obnoxiously pink Nike shorts with an enormous swoosh up the side that Lena couldn’t bear to add to her life. She’s drinking what looks to be a mai tai based on the colorful garnish in the drink and yellow umbrella. 

    “‘Sup,” Kara says, then pauses, her eyes focusing a little more on Lena. “You okay?”

    “I’m fine,” Lena says, dismissing the concern in Kara’s eyes with a wave. “I have a package for you.”

    Kara glances at it, then glances behind Lena to the mess of things on her bed, letting out a low whistle. 

    “Wanna come chill in here?” Kara asks, gesturing behind her. Their rooms are basically identical, but the thought of sitting in Kara’s room sounds infinitely more appealing. So she steps through without a word, brushing past the warmth of Kara’s body. Kara doesn’t move away really, just lets them touch, and doesn’t close the door to Lena’s room.

    Kara’s clearly made a home of her space - there are five Nike shoe boxes stacked on her small table, a mess of papers and her lanyards on her desk. She’s also got a fresh bib out from the course, a white one with LUTHOR nameplated across the back, hanging off the chair at the desk. Her suitcase is flung open at the side of her bed, the door to her deck open where it looks out over the pool. She has three books on her nightstand, a laptop slung down to the foot of the bed. The television, though, still has Golf Channel on.

    Lena drops onto the bed, scuttling up toward the head of it. Tries to remember to breathe like a normal adult human being.

    “Want me to order you a mai tai?” Kara asks, her hand already hovering over the phone at the desk. Lena nods.

    -

    Kara ends up ordering three mai tais at once. They arrive fast, and they sit on the bed together and watch the fucking Golf Channel, where it seems content to bring up her name every ten minutes. Apparently her return has been much questioned, brought up to other golfers, to anonymous professionals. There’s also talk of Jack partnering with some upstart named Eve Tessmacher, as well as pictures of Kara walking the course today with Lena, hovering over a ball and tugging at the pom-pom atop her beanie while Lena laughs.

    “You know, I think it’s totally cool that you trashed your brother’s vintage Maserati with a seven iron. It’s the most useless club,” Kara says, her head tilting against the headboard. The commentators are remarking now on the property damage charges that had been dropped, Lena’s firm jettisoning from the family and its functions. Lena sighs, looking Kara’s way.

    “You don’t have much of a filter,” Lena says.

    “You didn’t know that before you hired me?” Kara asks with an unrepentant sip of her cocktail. 

    Lena’s lips thin, her eyes rolling away from where Kara continues to sip at her drink cheekily. “I’m still getting used to it.” 

    “Sure,” Kara says with a laugh. “If you want, I can start to be more closed off and fake around you like everyone else.” 

    Lena sighs, thinking maybe she should throw a pillow at Kara’s head in response. She can tell Kara’s teasing her, as she’s been wont to do, and reminds herself that this is exactly why she wanted Kara here with her, why she feels more comfortable with Kara than she had with Jack. It’s the lack of pretense and judgment. 

    “Yes, I’d appreciate that, thank you,” she says, knowing Kara will laugh the sarcastic reply off. 

    The chuckle comes as expected and so does an equally joking, “Will do, boss.” 

    They’re quiet for a moment, a commercial finally interrupting the seemingly unending coverage of Lena’s family and past. 

    “You aren’t going to ask why I trashed my brother’s vintage Maserati with a seven iron?” Lena asks, fingering a trailing water droplet on the plastic side of her mai tai glass. Kara shrugs, her legs shifting until her body is more turned toward Lena.

    “I think if you want to tell me, you can tell me. But no, I wasn’t going to ask,” Kara says.

    “You aren’t going to ask why I fired and rehired the same caddie four times in a season?” Lena asks. Kara hums, sipping at her drink for a second. Her eyes are very blue, behind thick black frames. Her arms are amazingly unaffected by a golfer’s tan.

    “I think I’ll just focus on being a good caddie and hope you don’t fire me four times,” Kara says.

    “And you don’t care that I’m about as toxic a golfer as you could have picked,” Lena says. 

    “ You picked me ,” Kara corrects, then smiles. “Though given the choice I wouldn’t have picked any other golfer than you. You’re not toxic or whatever the heck you think you are.” Another pause, another long sip of her drink, Kara smiles again. “I do like you, you know. We’ve discussed it.” 

    Lena looks over at the other woman, unsure how to even respond. Kara is grinning, really, calm and collected, uncaring. It feels like a cool rain after a day on the course, like a relief. 

    Eager to change the subject, Lena searches for an avenue. “Are you still writing?” 

    Kara blinks, glances down at her laptop shuffled between them on the bed down toward the end. “Not really. Definitely not about you or anything, if that’s what you’re worried about.” 

    That, in particular, hadn’t occurred to Lena, though now it races to the forefront of her mind as an idea forms. “I wasn’t,” Lena assures her. “But if you’d like to write about me, I wouldn’t mind.” 

    “I guess I could,” Kara says, as though the idea had not once occurred to her before this moment. It’s surprising, but seems on-brand for Kara. Like a burst of sunshine who’s never seen the rain. It only solidifies the idea in Lena’s head. 

    “You should,” Lena says, insistent. A mutually beneficial arrangement. Kara can stretch her journalistic muscles and Lena can retain control over her rapidly spiraling PR image through a person who strangely seems incapable of seeing the bad in Lena everyone else so easily sees.  “I’m sure plenty of people would love to read the inside story of my descent into madness.”

    Kara smiles wider, grabbing for her phone on her nightstand and then reaching further for the remote. Her tank top slips up at her back, showing off the fine-tuned muscles just above the waistband of her shorts. Lena watches, bobbles the remote when Kara tosses it at her.

    “ You should tell me what your favorite pizza toppings are,” Kara says, swiping around on her phone. “And pick literally anything else to watch. We can think about the course, your madness, and the weight of your myriad crimes tomorrow.”

    “It was one crime,” Lena says. She rolls her eyes, flipping through the channels. The soft sounds of the commentators drifts away and she takes a deep breath. “And anything but pineapple.”

    “Anchovies,” Kara says.

    “Anything but pineapple and anchovies,” Lena returns.“Wow. Demanding. I can see why you and your last caddie didn’t get along so well.”

    Lena glares at Kara long enough to bring Kara’s attention up from where she’s placing an order for pizza, but Kara does nothing in light of Lena’s stare other than smile. 

    -

    Thursday is a veritable zoo. They warm up at the range and on the chipping green for thirty minutes at six in the morning, Kara yawning repeatedly and shuffling her feet. There’s a camera flash of her driver swing nearly every minute, at least once, and she hooks right fourteen times in  thirty swings. Kara doesn’t say anything about it - if it were Jack, he’d make a humming noise, Lex would mutter a shit or Lillian would offer some suggestion. But Kara stands there and watches patiently, her hands shoved into her long pants. 

    Siobhan’s caddie, Leslie Willis, has already made at least three efforts at conversation with Kara, who seems both tired and uninterested. Sam is hovering in the background, talking to the TaylorMade rep who’s been skulking around her bag all week. Down the line, Veronica is warming up under the watchful eye of her new boytoy caddie, some guy named - Darren. She had thrown Lena one hello again before setting up. 

    Kara lets her swing about four more times after the fourteenth pull and then finally appears at Lena’s side, grabbing for the club. Lena expects to be handed another one, but Kara just looks at her closely for a second.

    “What’s bumping you up the most, right now?” Kara asks, leaning a little on Lena’s driver and tilting her body so that Lena is blocked off from the cameras set up yards back. “The cameras? Your ex-girlfriend? Your return to golf on a national stage after seven months of being off the tour?”

    “She isn’t my ex-girlfriend,” Lena mutters. She does not pay Kara’s assumption the dignity of glancing Veronica’s way. 

    “Your ex-one night stand,” Kara says. Lena makes a face, but Kara rolls her eyes. “I’m going to give this driver back to you, and I want you to swing it like you’re a fourteen year-old boy at TopGolf trying to impress the girl he’s brought but like, he’s never going to impress her. He took her to TopGolf.”

    “Somehow, I feel like this is from life experience,” Lena says. Kara shrugs and grins.

    “My sister has tried it more than once,” Kara says. “She’s banned, actually. Smashed too many clubs. Me, I know that golf is not what gets ladies going. Literally anything else, if done well and in front of the right woman, could do it.”

    “You’re making my confidence rise with every successive word,” Lena says dryly. Kara hands Lena back her driver, reaches out to tap the bill of Lena’s visor hard enough that it shunts down into her eyeline. She huffs as she fixes it back up.

    “I’m just saying. Stop thinking about your drive like you have to impress someone. Because it’s only really going to impress you,” Kara says. 

    “You’re saying you won’t be impressed even if I nail this drive,” Lena says, focusing again on the span of grass ahead of her as Kara steps away from Lena’s swinging area, her hands resettling in her pockets. 

    “Nope,” Kara says. “The only attractive thing about it will be your own satisfaction and, also, how good that stupid skort looks with your legs.”

    Lena pauses, looking from the ball to Kara. Kara’s got her head tilted, a bright smile on her face as she waits for Lena to swing.

    “You’re an idiot,” Lena says. 

    “Go on, do the ball hitting thing,” Kara says. 

    Lena does. The drive is perfect this time, flying out exactly as Lena intended. Brow arched, she glances at Kara who is grinning ear to ear, but her eyes are focused on Lena and nowhere near the flight of the ball. 

    -

    “Jesus Christ, I think I’m going to die,” Kara says, tilting her head up to look at the sky. They’re delayed between seven and eight right now, waiting for the group ahead of them to finish up. Someone is taking an absurdly long time to putt on the green, 450 yards and down a hill. “It’s hot as dicks.”

    “Where’d you put our water?” Lena asks. Kara is using the bag mostly as a leaning post, so she has to bat Kara out of the way so she can grab their water bottles. There are plenty of passerby who keep stopping to notice them, notice her - even though Veronica and Siobhan are five feet away, chatting about something or another while Darren stares at Veronica’s tits and Leslie chats with Siobhan’s coach. 

    Kara hums gratefully when Lena forces one of the bottles into her hand, lifting up her cap and spraying water into its top. It runs in rivulets down her face, into the braid of her hair down her back. Lena laughs in amusement when she shakes like a dog instead of wiping it off.

    “Do you still think it’ll be okay if I hit the bunker?” Lena asks, narrowing her eyes and leaning further into Kara’s space to look down the hole, where a quartet of bunkers are treacherously placed right in the middle of the fairway. Kara pushes at Lena’s shoulder until she can comfortably rest her arms on the sides of the bag without headbutting her. 

    “Yep,” Kara says. “Better than the mess on either side. Do you think Veronica realizes that I can read lips?”

    Lena glances over at the other woman, whose eyes are trained on Kara as she talks what is probably a load of shit with Siobhan. Kara is wearing Ray-Bans, another endorsement who had shown up out of the woodwork to bless Lena with a box of sunglasses. 

    “Probably not,” Lena says. “What’s she saying?”

    “She thinks we’re fucking,” Kara says. “She’s said some interesting things about your preferences in bed. I, for one, am considering taking a seven iron to her Maserati.”

    “Of course she thinks that,” Lena says. “I think she’s fucked every caddie she’s ever had. I think it’s how she pays them, to be honest.”

    “Now she’s talking about how she’s got anal fissures,” Kara says, her head cocking to the side. She doesn’t even look like she’s not watching the conversation. Somehow Veronica hasn’t noticed. “Jesus. I don’t think I’d say that within two hundred feet of television cameras.”

    -

    They finish Thursday in fourth, and the minute they walk off the 18th green, there’s a reporter from the Golf channel nearly on top of them. Kara is in the middle of an intense dissection of why she thinks that full chicken wings, as opposed to drums and flats, are the truest form of the chicken wing. 

    “Do you have a second, Lena?” the reporter asks. She’s a nice one, clearly sent as a softball by the crew. Sam gives Lena a nod from up ahead, and Kara glances over at her. “And actually, we’d love to talk to you, Miss Danvers.”

    “We don’t have to,” Kara says quietly, turning her head away from the reporter. “We can shower and meet her later.”

    “No, it’s fine,” Lena says. Kara looks less than impressed, her expression a bit reproachful. “It’s fine. Of course we have a second, Tina.”

    “Awesome,” Tina says, shuffling them off to an empty patch of grass that’s been roped off for Tina’s sideline reporting. Kara sets the bag on the ground in front of the two of them in what’s probably a conscious effort to build a wall between Lena and Tina. For a second, the producer to the side looks like he might ask her to move it, but Kara rests her hands on the top of the bag protectively. “So, Lena, how does it feel to get back out there on a tour event?”

    “It’s been amazing,” Lena says. “The course has been good, the weather’s been perfect. Everyone’s been so lovely. It feels like I never left.”

    “Out there on fourteen, you didn’t look too concerned that you landed in the bunker with a plugged ball. Was that all a part of the plan?” 

    “I wouldn’t say it was part of the plan, but we had worked on that through the week,” Lena says, glancing over at Kara, who’s nodding. “I think we felt that with how many hills and valleys there are on this course, there were times where hitting out of a bunker might be preferable.”

    “And, for the people watching right now, your caddie right here is Kara Danvers,” Tina says. “If the name sounds familiar, she was an NCAA champion golfer with Stanhope a few years ago. How has this experience been for you, Kara?”

    “Awesome,” Kara says, grinning. “Lena’s an awesome golfer, Hawaii is awesome, and I get an awesome workout lugging this thing around all day. It hasn’t been too tough for me.”

    “Well, Lena Luthor has closed out her first round of play here at the Plantation course with a fourth place finish, seven under. We’re looking forward to seeing you tomorrow,” Tina says. Lena nods, says thanks before the camera light turns off. Kara leans more heavily on the bag, knocking the brim of her hat up to show more of her forehead.

    “I feel like my body is made of sweat,” Kara says, tugging at the indeed sticky arm of her polo peeking out from under her bib. Tina laughs, handing off her mic to some wide-eyed intern.

    “You guys look like you’re having fun out there,” Tina says. She’s looking at Kara with unvarnished interest, which seems to be a common reaction to Kara. But Kara isn’t paying any attention to it, reaching down into a side zip in the bag and handing a granola bar to Lena before fishing a pack of peanuts out for herself.

    “We are,” Kara says, popping open the bag and shoving a handful of peanuts in her mouth all at once. 

    “How’d you two meet up, anyway?” Tina asks, her smile entreating. “Off the record, of course.”

    Lena has to restrain herself from rolling her eyes, taking a bite of the granola bar.

    “Kara was my caddie at this tiny public course in Washington,” Lena says. “I ignored her advice for a full round of golf.”

    “Very rude,” Kara says, nodding through a mouthful of peanuts. Tina is smiling at her completely goofy-faced. 

    “Once I decided to listen to her, it was a good match,” Lena says. Kara doesn’t look Lena’s way, but offers her hand out for a low high-five that ends a bit slow, Kara gripping her fingers and squeezing for a half-second. Tina watches it with some interest, clearly trying to piece together what exactly is happening here. Lena isn’t really interested in giving her an answer either way. “Thanks for your time, Tina. We should probably go get showered, before Kara here turns into a new body of water.”

    “Disgusting,” Kara says, grabbing for the bag in front of her and hoisting it back up onto her shoulder. She waits for Lena to join her on the path back to the clubhouse, where Sam has grabbed a cart for transport back. The tour official up front gives a smile while Sam reaches out for a high-five. Kara offers out a hand for the step up onto the back of the cart before she climbs up after her, wrapping her arms around the bag.

    “That was an awesome round,” Sam says, half-turning in her seat. Lena tries to turn to face her, her knees bumping Kara’s thigh. Kara pays no mind to it - she’s watching the scenery blur by as spectators let out yells of appreciation at Lena. “Fucking awesome, Lena. I’m proud of you.”

    “I did not pack tissues in this bag so you better not cry,” Kara says, her arm settling behind Lena’s shoulders. 

    -

    Saturday night, they’re sitting in third, and Lena is sifting through what Nike’s sent over for championship Sunday with Kara and Sam when there’s a knock at the door. Kara hops up from where she’s lying reclined on the bed with a mess of shirts piled on top of her, plodding over to the door.

    “You’re not room service,” Kara says, sounding very disappointed by this. Lena’s out of line of sight from the door, but glances up at Sam, who’s sitting by the window and blinking at the doorway.

    “Uh, no, I’m not. Sorry, I suppose I must have the wrong room,” the voice says. Lena winces, and Sam makes a face that approximates somewhere between a frown and a smile. “You - you’re Lena’s new caddie, right? Have you seen her around?”

    “He can come in, Kara,” Lena says, even when Sam makes a face. It takes a second, but Jack slowly ambles into view, his hands deep in his pockets and shoulders rounded. Kara follows after him. The contrast between them is amusing, to say the least - Jack has on his typical outfit of polo and golf shorts, braided belt on display, sunglasses perched in his well-coiffed hair. Kara is wearing a sweatshirt and the same pink shorts she had been in on Wednesday night, hair long and loose around her shoulders. Kara retakes her spot on the bed while Jack hovers.

    “Hey, Lena, Sam,” Jack says, nodding at each of them. “I thought I’d stop by and say hello. You’ve been playing well.”

    “Seven months away really let me work on some things,” Lena says. Jack looks like he’s unclear on how to proceed. She’s not really sure she wants him to proceed, honestly. She had always liked Jack, loved him after a fashion, and he had always loved her too much. After the suspension, after she had broken the headlights and dented the hood of Lex’s Maserati and her mother had told her she would never win another tournament, she hadn’t had the heart to answer his calls or ever call him back.

    “Right,” he says. “I wanted to wish you good luck tomorrow. And uh, on the rest of the tour. Maybe we could meet up for dinner sometime. I’ve missed you.”

    “We should meet up,” Lena says. She knows somewhere deep down she might owe it to him, and it’s fair enough that she had liked Jack when he wasn’t on the course with her, on the road with her. She had always found him perfectly agreeable sharing a bottle of wine talking of anything but golf. 

    “Wonderful,” Jack says, his grin appearing on his face. “Well, I’ll let you all be. Nice to see you, Sam. And to meet you, Kara.”

    Kara salutes from the bed, and Sam gives a pinched smile that lasts barely nanoseconds after he’s turned his back and made his way out the door. 

    “God,” Sam mutters. “I wish he’d just grow a spinal column.”

    “Don’t,” Lena warns, reaching for the shorts she’s been eyeing for tomorrow. They’re black, short, and would pair perfectly with the all-black sleeveless shirt she’s been thinking of rocking. She can imagine her mother’s thoughts on that: I didn’t realize you were attending the funeral for your career, Lena. 

    “Don’t what? Express opinions about your ex-boyfriend?” Sam asks, shuffling in her seat as her phone starts ringing. When she pulls it out, she sighs. “It’s my mom. Let’s hope Ruby hasn’t drowned or anything.”

    Lena waves her off, and Sam picks up the phone and wanders out the door. Kara is shuffling through the much smaller contingent of merch that’s been sent to her - she seems to be favoring a bright blue polo with navy blue shorts. 

    “So, you were fucking your last caddie,” Kara says, grinning when Lena throws a headband at her. “Explains why you fired him four times.”

    “I fired him four times because he treated me like I was made of glass,” Lena says, rolling her eyes. “I think we had sex maybe twice last year. And he cried after one of those times.”

    Kara makes a pained noise, clutching at her sternum for a second and shaking her head. She grabs for a pair of Clubmasters and slips them on, flopping back onto the bed with her arms behind her head. Her not inconsiderable biceps are on display. 

    Lena finds herself looking at them for a few long microseconds, and then speaks without thinking for what could easily be the first time in several years.

    “Us dating was not the problem, is what I’m saying,” Lena says. “Plenty of golfers date their caddies. I think maybe M’gann is married to hers?”

    Kara makes a drawn out humming noise that Lena vows not to micro analyze. Eventually, she decides, finally, on a visor she likes - all-black, head to toe - and sets aside her picks on the dresser. She shoves everything else out of the way, so she can climb on the bed as well.

    “My last boyfriend was an absolute idiot, too,” Kara offers. “I was, admittedly, sixteen. I think all boys might be idiots at sixteen. And then I went to college to play golf and had capital R realizations about myself.”

    “Who didn’t have capital R realizations about themselves in college?” Lena asks. She rolls onto her side, knocking a box of watches into Kara’s hip on the way. Kara doesn’t seem to mind, just laughs a little, adjusting the pillow behind her head.

    “Emotionally stunted people, most likely,” Kara says. There’s a small pause, a comfortable one, where Lena blinks her eyes shut and listens to Kara breathe a few inches away. “Will dinner be awkward, then?” 

    “Most likely,” Lena says. “I didn’t exactly end things very well.”

    “Want me to fight him?” Kara asks. Lena laughs.

    “No, I think I’ll be alright,” Lena says. “Thanks though.”

    “Any time. You just let me know if you change your mind.”

    “I will,” Lena says. 

    They sit in a comfortable silence for a few seconds, Kara sorting through the Nike bag and humming something under her breath. She’s flipping through the hats when Kara pauses, gasps, and then vaults off the bed.

    “Wait! I have a gift for you,” Kara says, frantically digging through her suitcase where it’s spread out all over the floor. Lena watches on in amusement, until Kara unearths a black hat that looks nearly identical to the ones she’s just been looking over, with the Titleist logo on the front. 

    “You know I get these for free, right?” Lena asks, laughing. She flips it to the side and finds the usual Hublot logo, with the NetJets logo on the other side. When she flips it around to the back, though, the Nike logo has been supplanted by something else. 

    She finds herself tracing the shoddy stitching of Midvale Municipal’s logo, a lone pine tree and MMGC written up its trunk. It’s small, and the Nike logo above it dwarfs it even further, but it’s there. Warmth drifts through her as Kara looks at her and Lena looks at the hat.

    “I, uh, had the clubhouse send me a couple hats with this,” Kara says, gesturing down at the hat. “And then I ran into the rep people and they said it was cool and put the other logos on. I just thought…maybe it would be a good reminder, on Sundays especially.”

    “Thank you,” Lena says, dropping it atop the pile of black clothes she’s wearing tomorrow. She turns just the slightest, feeling tears in her eyes, and presses her face against Kara’s bicep, and feels Kara slip her arm around her. It’s warm, and good, and suspiciously perfect.

    Eventually, Sam comes breezing back in, then, thankfully confirming that Ruby has not drowned but has instead passed her math test with an A. 

    -

    She loses. She watches Lois Lane sink a putt fifteen yards out from the comfort of the locker room clubhouse, having finished a whole hour before Lois had. Kara is sitting next to her, her feet kicked up on the coffee table, one arm around Lena’s shoulders on the back of the couch and her other hand rolling two golf balls like they’re Baoding balls. Around the room, golfers turn away from the television and head to the changing area, caddies drift toward their own attached locker room. The cameraman who’s been assigned to the lounge area zooms in on Lena’s face long enough that she witnesses it come up on the television. A couple attendants grab players who are being asked to give interviews - Leslie, who lost two balls on one hole, for instance - and one dutifully stands just to the side of Kara’s body, looking terrified.

    “Um, Miss Luthor, tour officials would appreciate it if you would head to the media area,” she says. She looks like she might puke. And normally, this would be reasonable. The last tournament she lost, her last one before her suspension, she had refused every interview, had broken her driver on a marble countertop in the locker room. Had eventually broken every head and taillight on her brother’s car and been disowned. 

    Kara’s hand presses just the slightest into the skin of Lena’s shoulder, both soothing and questioning. Lena nods, and Kara says thanks to the attendant, who scurries away quickly, the cameraman in tow.

    “I’m excited,” Kara says, picking her feet off the table and grabbing for her hat which she had carelessly tossed to Lena’s lap when they had collapsed onto the couch. “You played fucking amazing. You know that, right?”

    “You don’t need to do this,” Lena says. She sits up, waits for Kara to be ready to go back into the pen. Twines her hands together and breathes around the feeling in her chest of failure, and knowing that everyone’s going to ask her about it when she goes out there. You got so close…

    “I’m not doing anything,” Kara says. She drops one of her hands on Lena’s mass of fingers in her lap and sort of squeezes at it. “You did play amazing. We lost because Lois Lane played one stroke better. That’s all that happened out there today. Nothing we could have done about it.”

    “I don’t know why losing is cause for excitement,” Lena grumbles, letting Kara grip her wrist and start to pull her upwards off the couch. 

    “Because we’re going to win,” Kara says, grinning, her face close and hat off center on her head. When Lena reaches up to pull its bill so it frames her face properly, her grin only gets larger. “We’re going to win so much, people will hate us.”

    “People already hate me,” Lena says. Kara rolls her eyes, her hands gripping at Lena’s hands between them.

    “We’re gonna give ‘em an actual reason to hate you,” Kara says. Lena isn’t usually into trusting people, or believing them. But something about Kara, in this quiet, unhappy clubhouse, makes her want to. So she does.

    -

    ARIZONA, UNITED STATES - TPC SCOTTSDALE - PHOENIX OPEN

    She has a timeshare she splits with some football coach in Phoenix where he gets July through January and she gets the rest of the year. Her mother had always insisted that she should just buy a condo or some desert ranch house there, but basically anything Lillian suggested was cause for questioning in Lena’s mind. She had stuck with the weird non-house arrangement, even if it meant the dude was always messing with the settings on her Trackman system she had graciously allowed him to use throughout his time.

    “You want to play Scottsdale?” Lena asks, fiddling with the computer system settled on the bar in the basement. Kara has just changed into her usual lounging clothes and Lena is trying to break in a new pair of shorts that had been in a massive shipment on her front porch. They had gone through the boxes for a while after Kara had hauled them in. There were plenty of simple colors and a dark set that was clearly meant for Sunday - if she got there - but there was also a small assortment of brightly colored and patterned items that had been in Kara’s size she had spent many minutes excitedly pouring over.

    “Sounds boring,” Kara says. She’s going over the liquor cabinet right now. “You want a vodka soda?” 

    “You don’t want to get on the course early? And sure,” Lena says. 

    “We can get on the course early on Monday,” Kara says, a bit whiny. Lena can’t help but smile as she starts shoveling ice into their cups. “Pick whatever course you play for fun.”

    “You know I don’t do fun,” Lena says. 

    “Then close your eyes and just press the next course button until you land on one,” Kara says. “Didn’t realize I’d have to teach you how to have fun when I agreed to be your caddie.”

    “I don’t think you get to complain when you just got paid seventy five thousand dollars for handing me clubs all weekend in Hawaii,” Lena says, but she closes her eyes anyway and starts clicking. Kara laughs, and Lena feels her body heat close behind her, can imagine her watching the screen.

    “Were you a mean girl in high school?” Kara asks, and Lena can feel her breath brushing the side of her face. She keeps on clicking.

    “I went to boarding school,” Lena says, airily. Kara laughs again, her hand pressing briefly into Lena’s shoulder blade.

    “Stop,” she says. When Lena opens her eyes, she’s looking at the visage of a club she didn’t even realize was on her Trackman system. GC Budersand, which she has a vague memory of golfing while blitzed with Lex a few summers ago. She isn’t sure they even finished the course. 

    “Is this fun enough for you?” Lena asks, gesturing at the screen. Kara shrugs, leaving a vodka soda in a cup with an obnoxious bird logo on it on the bar in front of Lena. When Lena had asked if she had wanted to get to Arizona early and get some work in together, Kara had agreed readily, and had brought her own clubs. They look worn and loved, except for some fresh grips and a new driving iron that Kara has repeatedly called big sexy. DANVERS is printed on the bag, along with Stanhope’s logo and school colors. Compared to the six golf bags along the wall with Lena’s name and clubs, it seems simple. 

    “Super fun,” Kara says, taking a sip of her vodka soda while Lena finishes adjusting their settings. “Boarding school, huh?”

    “Yep,” Lena says, popping the p and typing Kara’s name in as ASSFACE. She hesitates for one second before she types her name in as WINNER. 

    “Were there short skirts at this boarding school? Some hot science teacher you were super into?” Kara asks. Lena throws a glare her way, and Kara is smiling into her red bird cup, watching Lena with bright blue eyes and her glasses. “Come on, give me the juicy deets so I can write an expose on the torrid childhood of Lena Luthor and sell it for millions of dollars.” 

    “I’m sure Golf Digest is not interested in my crush on my chemistry teacher or how short my skirts were,” Lena mutters. Kara laughs.

    “Hey, if I have to go around to all these beautiful locales carrying around golf clubs sweating my ass off for a beautiful woman, I’m aiming higher than Golf Digest,” Kara says. “Maybe like. GQ. They’d make you do a photoshoot in lingerie.”

    It’s said casually, but Lena can sense the underlying crackly feeling that has been growing more and more present around Kara. Lena hasn’t worn lingerie in years, but now she’s considering it.

    “I hereby revoke my offer for you to write an article about me,” Lena says, starting the game and crossing around the bar to get to the soft green where Kara is idly leaning on her driver and drinking her vodka soda. Lena reaches for her own.

    “What was it I said?” Kara asks, her grin ever present and beautiful. It only grows when her name pops up on the screen, teeing off over a simple enough looking fairway. She looks at it for a few seconds before she leans off her driver. “Skins? A hundred a hole?”

    “Sure, if you feel like owing me eighteen hundred by the end of this,” Lena says, shrugging. Kara ambles over to the tee and settles her bright orange golf ball on it, laughing a little.

    “Where’s all this confidence when we’re in an actual tournament?” Kara asks, looking over the hole before she settles her driver behind the ball. Her form is tall and looks picture perfect, like the kind of thing they’d show a beginner. Tilt your shoulder down, ball on front foot, slight perch on the knee. She can remember Lionel telling her all of it, his hands pressing down on her shoulders and poking teasingly at the soft of the back of her knee to get her to bend them. 

    “You really want to dig into my psyche right now? Right before I crush you?” Lena asks.

    “Oh, I know your psyche,” Kara says. “You’re deathly terrified of disappointing everyone, and you’re much less scared to do it when it’s in private and a one-on-one competition against someone you’re pretty sure you can beat in your sleep.”

    Lena pauses, feels the fray of her nerves at having been so thoroughly pinned. Her therapist had said almost the same thing after their third session when Lena had spent twenty minutes discussing why she thought therapy was an expensive waste of time, complete with citations. You want to be in control. And any moment you’re not is an opportunity for failure to you, and failure is unacceptable in your mind. But you can’t fail at therapy, Lena. And you can’t fail at golf either, as long as you tried your best. 

    “Feel free to not publish that in GQ,” Lena says. “It won’t pair well with the lingerie.”

    Kara pauses, steps off the ball and looks at Lena, her head cocked to the side with a funny smile on her face. The crackling feeling between them sparks.

    “Feel free to not discuss you wearing lingerie while I’m about to tee off,” Kara says. 

    “I mean, I could change into some right now, if that’d help you concentrate,” Lena says, tugging at her polo just the slightest, so it bares some of her midriff. Kara’s eyes valiantly try not to follow the movement, but Lena watches with amusement as they fail. Kara raises her hand up in front of her, laughter in her throat as she tries to reset on the tee.

    “Mercy, please, ma’am,” Kara says. “We can play strip golf some other time.”

    “Go swing, Kara,” Lena says. Kara is already squared up again, looking altogether unfazed, a smile on her face and tongue sticking out of her mouth as she rears back, pulls through, and picks her head up after her swing has settled on her shoulder. The ball flies true, the stats pulling up on the board. A near-perfect hit. Kara’s still grinning when she falls out of her stance, bumping slightly into Lena’s shoulder as she passes by to lean against the bar.

    “Your turn, winner,” Kara says, gesturing up at the screen and reaching for her drink. Lena throws her a glare that only makes Kara laugh, and then she has the gall to interrupt Lena’s swing three times by playing “Sexyback” quite loudly off her phone. 

    Lena still wins.

    -

    “Kara Danvers,” says a drawling voice behind them. When Lena turns around, Cat Grant is standing there with her son and caddie, Adam, her arms crossed. She is glaring at Kara, and Kara is making a face that comes across as a wince underneath her sunglasses. 

    “Hey, uh, Miss Grant,” Kara says. She looks amusingly apologetic. 

    “First, you had the gall to disappear from the sport of golf after my thoughtful mentorship to chase something as uninteresting as journalism. Second, you break my son’s heart,” Cat says. Adam, who’s clearly trying not to burst into laughter, makes a face of confusion, tries to interject.

    “Mom, we went on one date, and I met Abigail literally two days later - ” he says. 

    “ - Third, you chose to caddie for a golfer of questionable renown despite the fact that you could easily join the tour yourself. And fourth, you call Carter on his birthday and yet, do not seek me out despite the fact that we were all on the same small island in the Pacific for a week’s time.”

    “I missed you too,” Kara says, setting Lena’s bag down and coming closer to offer out her arms in a hug. Cat does not see fit to join it, but Adam does, rolling his eyes and wrapping Kara up. “I’ve been super busy, I’m sorry.”

    “I didn’t realize you two knew each other,” Lena says, leaning on her bag and watching as Kara grabs for Cat anyway, despite her icy demeanor. Cat shoves her away with a warning glare, even though there’s a fond quirk to her face. 

    “Adam and Kara went to school together,” Cat says, coming closer to Lena and avoiding Kara’s arms, which are still reaching for her. “I found her talent intriguing. Too bad she’s wasted it.”

    “I have not!” Kara says. “Are you our practice partners for today?”

    “I suppose,” Cat says. “I thought it prudent to make sure you were aware I was still alive. And it has been a while since I’ve been paired with you, Lena.”

    “It has,” Lena says, haltingly. The last time they had played together was a championship Sunday, when she had a meltdown over the course of the last four holes. That night she had got absolutely blitzed, Lex had called her a fool and a failure, and she had broken her seven iron on his stupid car. Pleasant memories. 

    Kara is already bumping into her shoulder, pulling her bag up onto her back, and the thought drifts away under the bright Arizona sun.

    -

    The grandstands are packed on Saturday as she and Cat walk into the confined grounds of the 16th hole. Kara starts laughing when the crowd catches sight of them; they start up a boisterous chant of mas-er-ati. Lena’s in a comfortable eighth place on a course she knows just isn’t quite her “jam,” as Kara called it.
