    “Maybe you should get a Maserati sponsorship,” Kara says, setting the bag down at the edge of the teebox. “Voiceover: I like fast cars and fast women. Or you could do a mysterious one like Matthew McConaughey. Playing pool and then all of a sudden you’re driving.”

    “I bet Adam talks to his golfer about the hole,” Lena says, thumbing the face of her pitching wedge and watching as Adam and Cat huddle on the opposite side of the box, pointing and whispering. 

    “Oh? Are we golfing?” Kara asks. She turns and looks up into the massive grandstands behind her, popping the brim of her hat off her forehead. “Is this not a NASCAR race?”

    Cat’s name gets announced over the loudspeakers first, and the crowd goes raucous cheering for her. She’s about as no-nonsense as it gets, gives one wave to the crowd and then squares up for her shot. The hole today is an unenviable middle-left, about ten feet off the bunker. The green’s trimmed to about as short as Lena’s ever seen it - one off kilter shot can roll right off in any direction. 

    “Were you not going to tell me, by the way, that you knew Cat Grant?” Lena asks. She’s been wondering all week, and has been turning it over in her head. She knows Kara, can already feel her like an extension of Lena’s body. But what does she really know about Kara? Besides that she’s an unemployed reporter with a complete lack of reverence for golf?

    Kara’s head is tilted, her arm resting on the headcover of Lena’s driver. The crowd’s volume only gets louder when Cat’s shot goes - it’s an absolute bomb, high and towering. 

    “Didn’t think you’d care, honestly,” Kara says, her voice somewhat quiet. She glances over at Lena and gives a small smile. “I’ll tell you whatever you want to know, Lena.”

    It’s a soft moment in a hard setting; the first time Lena had ever walked onto this hole, it had felt like stepping into a spotlight so severe she could feel its burns. It’s a par-3 that she’s bogeyed her entire career. Her brain has always been filled with enough people shouting, and the addition of a twenty thousand deep crowd never helped. But she’s looking at Kara, right now, uncaring that the crowd is booing Cat’s shot rolling innocently off the green and into the bunker. Kara’s got her Ray-Bans upside down on her hat because of the shaded area they’re in; Kara’s glowing and not halfway to burnt; Kara’s smile is widening by the millisecond as they look at each other.

    “I want to know,” Lena says. “Two way street.”

    “Two way street,” Kara agrees, leaning off the bag so that Lena can pull the wedge free from its confines. She keeps looking at Lena, intent, even as Lena backs away to the spot they had agreed would be best for the shot they were looking for.

    Lena hears thousands of people in her ears. Mas-er-ati. There’s an impulse buried deep in her brain that her therapist has tried to root out of her to just wall it off, to shut herself into a box so dark and cloying that other parts of her head shut down too. But she can hear them, so loud. Can feel the sun, warm. Can feel Kara, a few yards back, watching her, looking at her in that way she does. 

    She swings. It thumps the green, rolls left and back, and settles, perfectly, in the hole.

    It’ll play on TV all night. Lena’s completely unaffected expression as she watches the ball sink down as if she’d expected it to do just that. Kara’s smile, the cocky way it sits on her face as she looks at Lena and jostles her in exuberance. Watching it all back, Lena won’t remember a lot of it apart from the way Kara plucks the wedge back out of her hand with a wink and whispers conspiratorially, “Okay, that was a little impressive.” 

    -

    She does four brief interviews in a row about the hole-in-one Saturday night, before the tournament’s charity gala. Every single one of them asks what she and Kara talked about on the tee box right before she shot. She doesn’t even know why until she’s in the hotel room they rented nearby just for the occasion of changing for the gala. 

    “I mean, Scott, you know as well as I do that golf is a game that has a lot of highs and lows, and that a WPGA season has plenty of highs and lows as well. But I think we can all agree that Lena Luthor is playing better these last two tourneys than we’ve ever seen,” says white man in suit number one. Scott, down at the end of the panel, is solemnly nodding. 

    “Yeah, Paul, she really is,” Scott says. “She’s always been a talented golfer, obviously. But it seems like breaking from working with her family - a decision a lot of people thought would turn out horrendously - has helped her out.”

    “She seems so much more relaxed on the course,” says the One Girl Commentator. “We all saw that hole-in-one today; she didn’t even watch Cat Grant’s shot. She was talking to her caddie. The tee box mics on the eighth hole picked up them arguing over a Jeopardy clue.”

    “The caddie change is absolutely a motivating factor in the resurgence in her play. Jodie Tapper caught up with Lena after her round today,” says Paul. It cuts to an image of her, listening intently, her face wet with sweat and water that Kara had just sprayed in her face before the cameras started rolling.

    “Lena, congratulations on that hole-in-one today. You probably know this, but you’ve never even parred that hole, let alone done that. What was going through your mind out there?”

    “Where’s the watch you said they sent me?” Kara asks, appearing in the bathroom doorway. Her shirt is only half done up, but she’s fiddling with her cufflinks. Sam had made Kara go to a tailor sometime in Hawaii, with the promise to bill Lena for a set of three bespoke suits to wear at whatever events need be. She looks handsome, and Lena only remembers she’s not totally dressed when Kara gives her a once-over twice. “Are you going to wear a sweaty golf polo and no pants to the gala?”

    “Would you mind?” Lena asks, arching her eyebrow and leaning sideways for the box of accessories that Sam had shipped to the clubhouse for them to take to change into. The watch she pulls out is massive, bulky, has see-through elements so the metalwork can be seen on the face. Kara takes it from her hand and hovers where she’s settled at the foot of the bed.

    “I wouldn’t say I’d mind so much as I’d have to adjust my expectations for the event,” Kara says. There’s one more once-over before she glances at the television, where they’re showing the clip of Kara and Lena looking at each other before Lena heads to the tee. It cuts back to Lena, post-round.

    “Kara’s been great out there for me,” Lena says, shrugging and glancing to the side. “I trust her. She trusts me. That’s pretty much all there is to it.”

    “Thanks, Lena,” says Jodie.

    “Thanks,” says the Lena on the television, before it cuts back to the commentators.

    “Should I ask how much this watch costs?” Kara asks, adjusting the thing on her wrist. She looks good, her eyes moving from Lena’s legs to the television to the floor-to-ceiling window showing off the setting sun on the desert. Truthfully, Lena wishes that she and Kara were in a Lyft back to her homeshare on the outskirts of the city, where Kara would make her watch some dumb show before ushering her to bed at ten. But still, this isn’t so bad.

    “No,” Lena says, standing up from the bed and reaching up for the buttons still left undone on Kara’s well-pressed shirt. The tailor had done a very good job - it was slimmed to her body well, cut perfectly on her broad shoulders. She does up two more buttons and leaves the neck button and the one just below, presses her hands along Kara’s collarbones. Kara looks down at her with steady blue eyes. “I do trust you, you know. That wasn’t lip service.”

    “I trust you, too,” Kara says. One of her hands presses, loosely, against Lena’s ribcage. “I know you don’t know me super well yet, but that isn’t an easy thing for people to get from me.”

    “And here I thought you were very easy,” Lena says. Kara rolls her eyes, her fingers clenching along the bones of Lena’s ribs.

    “Go put your dress on,” Kara says. 

    Putting the dress on is worth it just for the look on Kara’s face when she sees her, complete with a groan and a loud Jesus Christ are you kidding me.

    -

    They finish fifth on Sunday after a perfectly average day. Lena has been golfing her whole life; she has never once finished a round of golf and thought that she’d go again if she could. She’s seen people say it and mean it, wistful, looking back out over the eighteen: damn, I love this. Couldn’t you just stay out here forever? 

    It’s the first time she thinks she gets it, watching M’gann sink a putt to secure her win. Kara’s hand is pressed onto the skin of Lena’s ankle, her head resting on her fist as she regards the television in the locker room. Lena isn’t sure how Kara knows it, but her ankle is feeling a little off, probably from a harsh turn to get out of a bunker. Lena presses her heel down and away into Kara’s stomach and she picks her head up and blinks at Lena.

    “You still think we’re gonna win?” Lena asks. It’s childish, and she can imagine Lillian’s response in her ear. Something cold and harsh. Lex’s grin, saying he’d do it, easy. Jack’s silence. Kara nods surely.

    “Absolutely,” Kara says. 

    Kara calls for their car, throws Lena’s bag and their duffel bags into the trunk, and they get back to the timeshare after wading through event traffic. Kara gets Lena an ice bag when they get there, makes Lena lay there on the couch while they watch a reality show she’s never heard of. Lena falls asleep to the sound of some girl on the show screaming something about ice cream in her hair, while Kara plays Wordscapes on her phone and laughs.

    -

    CALIFORNIA, UNITED STATES - PEBBLE BEACH GOLF LINKS - PEBBLE BEACH CHARITY INVITATIONAL

    “My sister is thinking of coming in this week,” Kara says, poking her head through the adjoining hotel room door. She’s in another tank top and shorts combo. “Are you down for that or should I tell her to fuck off?”

    Lena, who’s in the middle of a trikonasana pose, blinks down at the mat beneath her feet. She’s never been amazing with other people, and has certainly never been good at families. But Kara is asking, not demanding, and she wants to be on Kara’s good side more than she’s ever cared about being on anyone’s side of anything.

    “Um,” is how she starts her response. Kara is ambling into the room now, looking at her phone as she launches herself onto Lena’s bed.

    SENTOSA ISLAND, SINGAPORE - THE NEW TANJONG COURSE AT SENTOSA GOLF CLUB - WOMEN’S WORLD CHAMPIONSHIP

    “Common misconception,” Kara says.

    -

    “Do you want to go watch some giant robots dance?” Kara asks. She’s sprawled on the living room couch while Lena practices chipping into a little net. Her focus is all over the place; she’s lucky that she’s not so out of control that she’s smashing her chips, or else she’d be explaining shattered glass. 

    “I thought you were writing,” Lena says, lining up on the mat and eyeing the ball placed just so. Chipping is a chainbreaker, Lex had told her once. You can miss the green all day, every day, but if you’re a good chipper, you can save yourself. Lex was always talking like he was the better golfer between them; it had taken Lena a long time to even doubt that it was true. She isn’t sure she doesn’t believe it even now.

    “I was,” Kara says. “I wrote a sentence. Or, I mean, I wrote the title.”

    “I can answer your questions if you have any,” Lena says, miming her swing. Visualizing a small green right where the net is. The ball rolling rightward and spiraling into the hole. Lean down, hit on the ball, take the spin. She has memories of being young, Lex’s hands pressing on her elbow and nudging her knee and holding her head in place. She had thought that the attention was love, in the way that kids think that attention is everything. The truth, Lena’s learned, is that love is not attention, but awareness. 

    She doesn’t even have to turn around to feel the reticence in Kara.

    “I don’t have any right now,” Kara says.

    “Are you ever going to have any?” Lena asks. The ball is staring in her face, and she takes her swing, soft and easy. The clunk noise on the heavy iron of the club sings, especially when the ball takes a curve even over a short distance and plunks into the waiting net. 

    “Yeah, I mean, sure,” Kara huffs. Lena can hear her shuffling around on the couch. “Of course I have questions, I just - don’t want to distract you.”

    “You don’t distract me,” Lena says. “You’re distracting yourself.”

    “Now you sound like my psychiatrist,” Kara mutters. Lena sifts another ball out of the small cloth bag in front of her chipping mat. It takes a few seconds to adjust it into the position she wants it, and to think of how to respond carefully. It’s funny; Lena hasn’t had to think hard about how to talk to someone in a long time. She hadn’t thought to care.

    “Do you even want to write?” Lena asks, watching the gleaming surface of the Pro V1 before her catch the light in its pebbled surface. Kara huffs again.

    “Of course I do,” Kara says. “Why are we psychoanalyzing me now?”

    “Why aren’t you writing, then?” Lena asks, turning finally to look at Kara. She’s in disarray, her legs flung over the back of the couch and shirt riding up her stomach. She’s also scowling. “I read your old articles. You’re a good writer, with interesting things to say in interesting ways.”

    “Yeah, and I got fired,” Kara mutters, shutting her laptop and setting it on the coffee table. Lena leans on her wedge, eyeing Kara’s posture as she sits up and crosses her arms. 

    “I think that’s more a commentary on the current state of journalism than your abilities,” Lena says. “Is that the problem? You know what you want to do, but you’re worried that you aren’t good enough to do it?”

    “Sure,” Kara says. “Yes, fine. Okay.”

    “And I know I’m good enough to do something, just don’t know if I want to,” Lena muses. Kara huffs a little. Lena turns back to her mat and ball, centering her body on the back heel. “We make a funny pair.”

    “I like it. Us,”  Kara says, softer, and Lena feels her eyes on Lena’s soft pullback and swing through. The ball swishes as soft as silk into the net. “You wanna go see the giant robots dance?”

    -

    As it turns out, Kara was not lying about the giant robots. Two massive robotic shipping cranes have been programmed to do a mating dance that lasts thirty minutes every hour or so, their silhouettes somewhat like birds as they maneuver and spray water. Kara seems to view it all with a soft amusement, eating an ice cream cone she picked up on their way over. It’s still a little hazy out, and so Kara is wearing a bright green windbreaker Nike’s gone and enabled her with, while Lena is in a normal jacket. Kara is also not sitting normally, her ass set on the concrete between the metal benches that make up the viewing area while Lena sits and watches in a sort of daze as the bird cranes fall in love.

    “Aren’t humans crazy?” Kara asks, taking a bite of her ice cream cone. Lena hums. The cranes are rising up into the air together, the massive video boards underneath them showing their hearts starting to beat as one to the music. Some kid goes sprinting across the walkway with a squeaky yell, their parent chasing after them. “We spend millions of dollars making two shipping cranes look like birds so they can dance and fall in love. Wild.”

    “I make millions of dollars hitting a small ball into a hole,” Lena says. Kara starts chomping on the actual cone part of her ice cream, giggling as one of the birds raises up into the air and starts spraying water from its sides in some sort of mating display. 

    “It’s crazy,” Kara says. “Who thought of that?”

    “The Scots,” Lena says. 

    “Sometimes I just think about things and realize how lucky as shit we are,” Kara says. “We’re in Singapore, watching two hydraulic cranes dance. I’ve got ice cream. I’ve got you. It’s wild.”

    Lena’s never stopped to smell the roses like that, and she knows it, knows that sometimes Sam tells her to calm down because Lena is lucky, that her problems don’t often extend beyond a wild putt. Kara is pressing her hand into the small of Lena’s back, digging beneath the rain jacket to press into the fabric of her t-shirt. For a few seconds, Lena lets herself sit in the world Kara is talking about. 

    “I’m worried about Lex,” Lena says, then. Kara’s fingers splay wide and then start moving, drifting over her back in soothing circles.

    “I know,” Kara says. And she sits up, onto the seat next to Lena, turning until she tucks her knee into the side of Lena’s hip, reaching for Lena’s hands. Kara’s hands are a little chilly, but Lena doesn’t miss the usual heat from their touch. “I know you are.”

    “I do feel lucky, I just - ” and Lena is ashamed to realize her voice is cracking, tears filling into her eyes. Kara tugs at her, navigating Lena’s body until Lena is more in Kara’s lap than not. “I just - he ruined me, before, and I feel lucky right now, and good, and I can’t stand - ”

    “I know, baby,” Kara says, her head knocking into Lena’s temple. “I don’t know what I need to be saying to help you feel better about this. But I’m here.”

    “I think I’m supposed to figure it out myself,” Lena huffs, but she turns her head sideways and ducks herself into Kara’s shoulder, winding closer even though the windbreaker is wet and the cranes are still dancing, bowing and twining on the water ahead of them. “He was so cruel, for so long, and it was like I was just walking around with broken legs and I had no idea. And now I feel - you feel - ”

    “Like some super cool robo crutches,” Kara says. Lena laughs, Kara’s lips form into a kiss at Lena’s earlobe. 

    “Right. Super cool robo crutches,” Lena says. “But it has to be me, in the end. I have to - I have to beat him. The like, psychological him, I mean. His psychological burden on me.”

    “I was fully ready to steamroll him physically, but I guess I’m okay with freeing you from his cage of emotional abuse,” Kara says. Her hand is pressing into the skin of Lena’s jaw, pressing them close together. “Look, but I’m not crutches. You know that, right? I’m like, a wall, or something. Maybe you’re leaning on me now, but I’m not gonna go anywhere. You’re like - my shipping crane. You can beat him on your own, sure, but I’m always gonna back you up.”

    “I’m lost in the metaphor now,” Lena says. Her hand presses down into the arm wrapped around her waist. She’s amazed no tourist has told them to get a room, to be honest, but it’s rainy, late, a Monday night.  

    “You’re not,” Kara says. 

    “I’m not,” Lena says. She slips her fingers underneath the hem at Kara’s wrist, wrapping around the strong bones there. She can feel Kara inhale and exhale against the side of her head. The shipping cranes are cradled together now, their heartbeats one and the same as their necks dovetail together. It is crazy, that humans feel love so deeply that Lena looks at these cranes and understands it as easily as reading the alphabet. It’s one of those things that anyone could recognize anywhere, its symbolism universal and clear across cultures. There’s no translation needed for the dance; it just is.

    -

    On Tuesday morning, Kara vehemently suggests that they go to an amusement park that is conveniently located on the island. It’s still rainy, but Kara in an Anorak rain jacket and a pair of her tight tapered golf pants, and excitedly dashing from one food vendor to another makes it semi-okay. They end up in line at a ride themed after Transformers, and Kara leans against the rail and Lena leans against Kara. 

    “You know, I never went to amusement parks with boys. Or girls, I guess,” Lena offers, somewhere around the fourth turn in the steady line. Ahead of them, there’s a couple so wrapped in each other that an actual Transformer could show up and she’s not sure they’d notice. Kara is navigating holding Lena’s hand and holding the corn on the cob she had mysteriously acquired while Lena was in the bathroom, before they joined the line. Kara huffs.

    “Emo,” Kara says. “I went to Knott’s Berry Farm with this boy named Cody when I was fifteen and he was sixteen and he got whiplash on one of those rides. And now we’re both gay.”

    “I was always golfing,” Lena says, looking up at the covered sky. “I never thought about it. What I was missing. I’d see people I knew on Facebook posting about going on double dates to Six Flags and I convinced myself I didn’t care.”

    “I get that,” Kara says. “I mean, I golfed a lot too. But uh. Probably not as much as you did.”

    “I mean, what’s the etiquette on that?” Lena asks, gesturing at the two teens vigorously feeling each other up in line. “I have no idea. Everyone seems very okay with it.”

    “It’s the atmosphere,” Kara says, letting go of Lena’s hand for a second to wiggle her fingers. “Teens sense something in the air when they’re in line at an amusement park. It’s like an aphrodisiac. Would I say that I’m okay with it? I don’t know. But you know, I too have made out with people in line for some dumb shooty mcshooty Scooby Doo ride.”

    “Wow, a floozy,” Lena says. Kara snorts, reaching for a nearby trashcan and shoving her corn on the cob inside. Lena puts her own unoccupied hand on Kara’s waist, wrapping her finger into Kara’s belt.

    “Yeah, yeah,” Kara says. “Why’re you bringing it up? You trying to make up for lost experiences?” 

    Lena watches her eyebrows waggle for a second, endlessly charmed and unable to pretend she isn’t. Kara’s arm wraps around her waist, pulling her close as they shuffle down the line. They probably look as stupid as the teenagers. 

    “Oh, do you see anyone around who I could practice with?” Lena asks, craning her head around and laughing when Kara makes an affronted noise.

    -

    “What’s the worst shot you ever hit?” Kara asks, much later, slumped in a small little round booth at their hotel bar with a rum runner set on her thigh. She’s abandoned her jacket to set it on Lena’s legs, her polo tight and unbuttoned, arm slung around Lena’s shoulders in the small leather booth. The lounge is loud, accumulating drunken partiers on a makeshift dancefloor down toward the other end of the bar.

    Lena sips her scotch and thumbs at a stain on Kara’s thigh that’s been bothering her for the last twenty minutes. 

    “Gut answer,” Kara interrupts, before Lena can piece together a response. “No thinking.”

    “God,” Lena says, then shrugs. Lets the memory come. “I hit this bunker shot at the Canadian Open two years ago.”

    “Oh boy, the one you smashed?” Kara asks, her lips fumbling attractively for the straw on her rum runner. 

    “Yeah,” Lena says. “It was plugged to hell and I second guessed myself. A lot. Ended up hitting right back to the other bunker. You saw?”

    “I saw,” Kara says. “Brutal.”

    “It was,” Lena says, then sighs. “Lex got me back out on the course somehow, after everything was closed down. Made me practice it over and over.”

    Kara huffs, shifts her legs a little. Her fingers are fiddling with the collar of Lena’s pullover as she takes another sip.

    “Best shot?” Kara asks. Lena shrugs.

    “The one in Phoenix, probably,” Lena says. A part of her is aware that people are aware of them, that they’re wrapped around each other and intoxicated in a dark hotel lounge where plenty of other golfers are most likely arriving. There were times last year where the thought of even Jack holding her hand would make her feel crazy, itchy and irritated. But she’s too drunk on scotch or Kara to really care. 

    “That was pretty fucking dope,” Kara agrees, nodding sagely. “Can’t teach that. You did also look hot as shit.”

    “You’re drunk,” Lena says, laughing, because it’s true. She doesn’t doubt the vehemence of Kara’s feelings in this regard, but it’s funny because of the way she says it, sort of like a fratboy about ready to puke and pass out. “Do we need to go back to our room?”

    “Whatever, you’re drunk too,” Kara says. “A lady can’t compliment another lady without getting called intoxicated? Messed up. Objectively, it’s true. I never thought country club clothes for women were hot until I saw you in them.”

    “And what exactly are country club clothes?” Lena asks, tilting further sideways and sipping her drink, burrowing into Kara’s side more and more. Kara’s hand is resting more in the space between Lena’s throat and sternum than her shoulder now.

    “You know, skorts and visors and bermuda shorts,” Kara says. “Tennis, golf, long islands, repeat.”

    “Thanks. I like how you dress too,” Lena says, pinching at the meat of Kara’s thigh underneath the stain. It’s impossible to miss the sudden shiver and tense that goes through Kara before she seems to shake her head out of it, dropping it on the back of the seat behind them. Lena’s drunk, and the boundaries between them had disappeared a long, long time ago. She shifts to chase, setting her chin on Kara’s shoulder and watching as Kara’s eyes slip lazily closed, her hand sliding to rest on the side of Lena’s neck. “You’re very handsome.”

    “Yeah, someone started a Twitter dedicated to my fashion choices,” Kara says. “You’re palling around with an icon.”

    “Palling around?” Lena asks, raising an eyebrow. Kara opens one eye to look at her, a soft, big smile on her face. 

    “Yeah, yeah,” Kara says. “All that other stuff too.”

    -

    Kara goes for a jog on the beach at sunrise, and Lena does her morning yoga routine. This is when there’s a knock on the door. For a few seconds, she considers ignoring it.

    “Hey,” is how Jack sheepishly greets her. He’s wearing jeans and a polo shirt with her family name, Luthor Golf, on it, like he was carrying it around in his suitcase just waiting for the chance to whip it out again. Jack had never liked trading on Lena’s name, but he had never turned down opportunities either. And that was fair enough, considering Lena was dating him and hardly ever touched him. “Can I - is Kara here?”

    Lena rolls her eyes. Jack’s shoulders slump. 

    “Would it matter if she was?” Lena asks, before she steps back. “Come on in.”

    Jack steps inside, glancing around as though he’s expecting someone to jump out and yell boo. His hands are shoved deep in his pockets, giving off the overall impression of someone who’s been harangued enough times to think forgiveness should be a part of their posture. 

    “Sorry, Jack, I’m just - a little short on nerves right now,” Lena says, after a few seconds. “Kara’s staying here, but she’s out on a jog right now. For some reason, she thinks beach running is fun.”

    “Oh, okay,” Jack says. His hands come out of his pockets, and he follows Lena to the sitting area, accepting a water bottle when Lena hands it to him. He doesn’t open it, just starts twisting it around in his hands. “Are you two, uh - ”

    “Did you know Lex was going to start coaching Eve?” Lena asks. Jack looks so taken aback he nearly recoils away from Lena.

    “No, uh, I didn’t,” Jack says. “I knew she had fired her old coach before we started in Hawaii, but I didn’t realize she was getting Lex. I thought Lex was part of your camp, actually.”

    “Lex and I haven’t talked since he told me I didn’t deserve to be a Luthor and got me arrested smashing his car up,” Lena says. Jack’s hands crack the water bottle violently enough that Lena’s half afraid he may just pop it open.

    “He said - what?” Jack asks. “What?”

    “Are you saying that because I need to repeat it or because it’s shocked you?” Lena asks. Jack squeezes the water bottle again in his hands, spine straightening. 

    “Why would he say that to you?” Jack asks. “Had he said other things like that to you?”

    “Does it matter?” Lena asks. “It happened. I lost my temper and tried to destroy his Maserati. And now he’s here, and I wanted to know what you knew.”

    “I didn’t know anything,” Jack says. “I swear, I would never have worked for Eve if I knew - I never would have left you, even.”

    “Jack,” Lena says, sighing. 

    “No, listen, I - I had no idea you were struggling,” Jack says. “I shouldn’t have left you when you were getting attacked like that, by your own family.”

    “Jack,” Lena says, more firmly. “I didn’t want your help. I didn’t want anyone’s help. Sam knew I was a mess, and I shut her out too. I appreciate that you wanted to help - ”

    “I want to help,” Jack says. He moves the slightest bit closer, his hand reaching for Lena’s. “How can I help, now? I can quit working with Eve, and Lex. I can come back to you.”

    Lena sighs again, letting Jack squeeze her fingers for just a few moments before she gently draws them away and places her hands on her knees. It surprises her how much it hurts, Jack’s face twinging in something like pain. She doesn’t love him, had never really loved him the way he had wanted her to, had resented him for never noticing how much she hurt, and for how much she had been expected to react to his hurt. It was complicated, messy, and not in the enjoyable way that she was starting to realize loving someone was supposed to be. Even when it ached, it should feel worth it.

    “I can’t speak to whether you should keep working with Eve, or Lex,” Lena says. “It’s taken me a long time, but I think I realize now that Lex has always been a monster, at least to me. And Eve seems like - a lot. Talented. But a lot. Sweet. But I don’t need you to help me, or come back to me. I’ve just started to feel okay. Lex has thrown that out of whack, for now, is all.”

    “Lena,” Jack says. “I still love you. You must know that. I never stopped loving you.”

    “Even when I fired you four times?” Lena asks. It’s hard not to laugh. It isn’t funny, to love someone like that. She knows that now. She had felt that way about Lex, when he froze her out or when he’d make her work until she cried. “Jack, I wasn’t good for you. It wasn’t you, obviously, because I don’t think I would have been good for anyone. I wasn’t good for myself.”

    “We could be better now, then,” Jack says. His smile is earnest. Lena shakes her head, trying to piece together her thoughts, a way of rejection that doesn’t sound like an excuse or an insult. 

    “Jack, I like you,” Lena says. “But as my friend. I think I’ve been needing friends.”

    Jack’s face all but falls, his hands gripping on his water bottle as he looks down at the carpet beneath their feet.

    “Is it Kara?” Jack says. Lena sighs again. It feels as though she’s sighed a thousand times in this conversation. 

    “No,” Lena says. “I’m not rejecting you because of Kara. I’m rejecting you because of me.”

    “Sure, sure,” Jack says. And the best thing about Jack, what had always almost lifted her up, when she was really down in it: he puts on a smile, conspiratorial and sweet, even when he’s hurting. “But, Kara…”

    “Is good for me,” Lena says. It doesn’t feel right to define her and Kara with someone else before she and Kara had worked their way through that conversation themselves. She’s aware enough of how she feels, and of how Kara feels, to give a basic version of things. But the clarification feels sacred. 

    “Yeah,” Jack says. He nods, shuffles his feet up under his seat. “I know. I mean, I can tell. I think anyone could tell, probably.”

    He looks sad, boyish and chastised. It was all the things Lena had found irritating about him, that she disappointed him so thoroughly and that she hadn’t cared enough, was drowning so deep, to try to help him understand. She reaches out slowly, to place a hand on his forearm.

    “I’m really sorry, for how I treated you,” Lena says. “You deserved better than all that, and I knew it. I really did like you, and sometimes it was just...good, to have you around. When I was really bad.”

    Jack nods, drops his large, warm hand on top of hers and squeezes at her palm.

    “It’s on me, too, right? I think I knew things weren’t right, and I just - didn’t do anything about it. About you or us,” Jack says, shrugging. “Look, about Lex - ”

    “You don’t need to quit,” Lena says. “Eve is a good player. You two seem like a good team. And I don’t want to - confront him, or have him back in my life, even. I guess I just feel - scared. Like I’ve finally got something good going for myself, by myself, and when I saw him - ”

    “I’ll see if I can suss him out, at least, then,” Jack says. “And if I can keep him away from you two - ”

    “It’s humid as all hell, but I picked up our tee time at the club and then I ran into the Vokey rep who asked me how your wedges were feeling, and then I got swarmed by all these reps and social media nerds, so I’m sorry I’m late coming back for breakfast - oh, hi, Jack,” Kara says. She’s carrying a backpack and no less than five sponsorship tagged bags, clearly very sweaty and with sand still stuck to her shins. Lena can’t help but laugh at the sight of her, standing up to help.

    “Hi, Kara,” Jack says, standing as well. Lena starts offloading Kara. One of the bags Lena gets her hands on has the Maserati logo printed on it. She’s also got a new watch clamped onto her wrist, an almost amusingly large green Hublot monstrosity. “Blimey, you really did get swamped.”

    “Yeah, I don’t know, everyone got really excited about me showing up,” Kara says, shrugging. The smile that she’d come into the room with has frozen as she looks over at Jack. For a second, Lena doesn’t even realize what’s wrong - and then she laughs a little, recognizing the strange protective posture that had almost led to Kara fighting Doug Weemsdale. She pokes Kara in the stomach as she takes bags off her hands.

    Kara glances down at her and sees enough to understand that Lena’s telling her it’s okay. Lena nearly laughs again when her shoulders sink a little, and she looks back to Jack with her normal, less murdery smile.

    “How are you doing? How was your flight?”

    “Lots of turbulence,” Jack says. “Plus, Eve gets very sick on planes. Doesn’t make for a fun ride.”

    “Who on earth gave this to you?” Lena asks, laughing as she pulls out a bright pink Nike polo printed with tiny green Nike swooshes. There’s a matching pair of green pants down below it. 

    “Our Nike rep! Nice dude. Said he’d been trying to get ahold of me for a while but that we’re hard to track down, I guess,” Kara said. “Apparently, he’s got big ideas about how to dress us.”

    “He wants to dress you as an abomination, then?” Jack asks, laughing as Lena tosses the full ugly outfit onto the back of their couch. Kara makes an affronted noise, dropping the backpack on the floor. 

    “Rude,” Kara says. “I’m gonna go shower, and then we can go eat - you wanna come down with us, Jack?” 

    “Ah, no thanks,” Jack says, a half-smile on his face that Lillian had endlessly called charming. “We’re one of the first practice rounds of the day. Have to go check the bag, you know how it is.”

    Kara nods amiably. Lena thinks back to those mornings, when Lex would always make sure that she was one of the first ones out on the course, walking with him while Jack ambled along behind. Occasionally, Lillian would join the walkthrough and stand off the teebox with her arms crossed and sunglasses imperious. For half a second, Lena wonders if she’s here, and whatever face she makes at the thought has Kara moving the slightest, brushing Lena’s side as she reaches to clasp hands with Jack.

    “Cool, we’ll see you out there,” she says. While she’s doing some odd bro-hand slap thing with Jack, Kara’s hand presses gently into Lena’s hip, an assurance that clarifies the brief storm in her head. Lena reaches up to hug Jack when he and Kara move away from each other. 

    “I’ll let you know if I learn anything,” Jack says, softly, and then he’s nodding one more time at Kara and heading for their door. Lena watches him go, giving a soft wave when he does the same. By the time she turns back around, Kara is dropping onto the couch, her long legs kicked up onto the arm. 

    The funny thing about all this is that even with Lex hovering over her head, she feels like the space she’s carved here, alone with Kara, is precious and unpunctured. Kara has her eyes closed, but she only gives a small laugh when Lena comes to join her on the couch, Lena’s body pressing against the long length of Kara, until she’s reaching up to run her fingertips across Kara’s still-sweaty hairline. Kara hums, an arm loosely wrapping around Lena’s waist and the other coming around to meet it, locking Lena into her circle. 

    “You good?” Kara asks. Lena keeps running her fingers along the skin of Kara’s face, slipping from her hairline to the slope of her temple to her eyebrows and whittling downward. 

    “I told him about Lex,” Lena says. “What he said to me that made me bust his Maserati. And asked if he knew what Lex was doing.”

    “And?”

    “He said he had no idea, about how things were, and that he had no idea Eve was hiring Lex,” Lena says. “And then he asked me to get back together with him.”

    Kara laughs again, her chest rumbling underneath Lena’s cheek as her fingers move down the slope of Kara’s nose, moving briefly to her earlobe.

    “You have to respect it,” Kara says. “Shoot your shot.”

    “What if I had said yes?” Lena says, trying to sound irritated, but instead, her fingers are brushing against Kara’s lips now, tracing their outline. Kara doesn’t even bother opening her eyes, the lazy thing.

    “Did you say yes?” Kara asks. She knows the answer, of course. The both of them do, as though it’s been sealed years ago, eons before they ever set eyes on each other. There’s something about security, the promise of an easy landing.

    “No,” Lena says, watching as Kara’s mouth opens just wide enough to let Lena’s finger touch her teeth. She gently bites down then, in an oddly affectionate movement that makes Lena laugh and Kara smile after Lena draws her finger away.

    “I have to shower,” Kara says. “Wanna come with?”

    “Very forward,” Lena says. Kara’s eyes finally open, a smile on her face.

    “I know, right? It’s about the long game,” Kara says. “First, you get fired - ”

    “Do not outline your dastardly plan,” Lena says, sliding off of Kara’s body and standing, stretching up toward the ceiling. She glances at Kara’s watch, her hand wrapping around Kara’s strong forearm. It’s 10:47am. “What’s our tee time?”

    “Noon,” Kara says, coming up after her. “Why, anything you want to do?”

    -

    “Honestly, I think I’d be having the same experience if I were back in the shower,” Kara huffs. She has her hair up in a bun underneath her hat, but her polo is soaked in sweat thanks to the humid air. There are bugs buzzing over every collected piece of water on the course. Up ahead of them, M’gann is talking with her caddie where she’s bogged in a water-filled bunker. Lena has to think for a few long seconds.

    Lena had been strong enough to decline Kara’s invitation, but only just. Kara had paid her back by wandering around in her boxers and sports bra “looking for her pants,” like an idiot.

    It’s been a very long time since Lena’s actually felt blue-balled, but she’s got half a mind to name it as such.

    “A better one, that’s for sure,” Kara continues, and Lena can feel in real time Kara’s eyes tracking down her body. This is, so far, about the only good thing that’s happened on the course so far. Lena’s hit just about every hazard she could, shorted her nine iron by ten yards, and pulled her five wood so far left that Kara put the cover back on it and put it in time out.

    “Can you not look at me like that?” Lena asks.

    “Like what?” Kara asks, adjusting her hat and continuing on looking at Lena.

    “Like that ,” Lena says. “It’s distracting. And unprofessional.”

    “Right,” Kara says, voice quiet. There aren’t really any media around for practice rounds, but there are enough course workers and tour officials that Kara glances around before she abandons Lena’s bag and comes closer. “But - ”

    “No,” Lena says, interrupting Kara’s inevitable and disturbingly effective comment. “I just - I want to keep a boundary. On the course, and not. Things with Jack - ”

    “I’m not Jack,” Kara says. Lena sighs, adjusting her visor and leaning on her driver, squinting into the distance. She tries to draw strength, to talk to Kara, and be honest and open. It’s about as easy as golf is today.

    “I know that,” Lena says, carefully. “But Jack and I used to be sort of okay at this, together, before everything. And I care about this way more than I cared about that.”

    “Okay,” Kara says. “I mean, that sounds reasonable. I just, you know, I think maybe the lines have been kind of blurry for the past few tournaments.”

    Lena sighs, breathing through the ache in her chest.

    “I agree,” Lena says. “But until we get things hammered out, I just want to focus on golf when we’re on the golf course. Or at least, not us. So if you could please…”

    “I assume that hammered out means, in this case, we kiss as lovers do,” Kara says. “And then have sex.”

    “I don’t have sex during tournaments,” Lena says. “My trainer told me once that it tightens up my glutes and I lose two degrees of rotation on my swing. Also, your statement is a direct violation of the rule I just set down.”

    “Well now I’m not sure how I’m supposed to recover from no sex during tournaments,” Kara whispers, looking now somewhat upset. “We’re in a tournament every other week for the rest of the summer into September! What kind of sex are we talking here?”

    “I’m shutting the conversation down now,” Lena says. “My therapist has requested multiple times that I enforce boundaries when I feel they are necessary.”

    “Okay, okay,” Kara says. “I’m with you. You know that, right?”

    “Yeah,” Lena says, and she sinks down into the relief of Kara taking a solid step back, and her soft smile. “Thank you.”

    “Of course,” Kara says. “We’ll hammer all that out later. I will restrain my eyes.”

    “Thanks,” Lena says, squaring back up on the ball. It’s a long par four, the green obscured by a patch of palm trees to the left. Kara flips her sunglasses back over her eyes and tugs at her hat, leaning on the bag. She looks strong, somehow, and it’s distracting to think of her abs underneath the long stretch of her stomach. Lena has to breathe through her nose to get her head out from underwater.

    “You think you could hit one of those palm trees?” Kara asks. Lena pauses, looking up to her caddie. 

    “You want me to hit one of them?” Lena asks.

    “Yep,” Kara says. “I just decided. Sounds fun.”

    “Alright,” Lena says. Despite the fact that Lena has been playing golf since she was just coordinated enough to swing a club without falling over after, her ball falls in the water hazard on the right side of the fairway, instead of the grove of palm trees on the left. There’s a second of silence after.

    “Worth a try, I guess,” Kara says, hoisting the bag up onto her shoulder and grabbing for Lena’s driver. She gives it up easily, bumping into Kara’s side as they start ambling down the tees to the fairway.

    -

    By Saturday, Lena has pulled her golf game together enough to claw her way up to twelfth. It’s not great, but considering the state of her mental health, it’s as much a victory as anyone could ask for. Until, of course, the day finishes and she and Kara get their tee time and partners for the next day. 

    “Hi there, Doug, right?” Kara says. She’s carrying Lena’s bag and still wearing her orange reflective Ray-Bans, even though they’re indoors. The clubhouse is bustling with media, and Lena can hear the insistent buzz of her own name getting said. Over and over, where’s Luthor?

    “Can you tell me what went into the decision making on our assigned group for tomorrow?” Kara says. She sounds nice, in the sense that she also sounds like she might take Lena’s divot pick out and take it to Doug’s jugular. Lena has half a mind to try to stop her, but she had been more than frozen since a small-time golf YouTuber had broken her the news first as she stepped out of the locker room. 

    “Well, as per WPGA rules, groups on Sundays are assigned by current total round score,” Doug says.

    “Yes, that sounds - right,” Doug says. He looks like a man who knows he’s about to get punched. Kara looks like she may just do it despite propriety. Lena sort of feels lightheaded.

    “Is there a particular reason the people running this tournament have placed Lena in a group with these two golfers, in consideration of your demands for us to stay away from the esteemed Mr. Luthor?” Kara asks. “Or are you just hoping she takes her seven iron to his face on the eighteenth?”

    “Look, Doug, I know I’m yelling at the wrong person here,” Kara says. “So if you could simply direct me to who it is I need to ream for this, I’d appreciate it.”

    “Yes, of course, uh,” Doug says. He starts patting down his pockets like he’s going to randomly appear a whole tour official.     “I feel like it,” Lena says, clenching her fingers tight into the fabric of Kara’s sweaty polo and digging her face into Kara’s shoulder. “I don’t understand - ”

    “Sorry to interrupt,” says a voice from the doorway, coming with a gentle knock. Lena feels herself freeze in place, feels Kara become as tense as a statue. She’d know that voice anywhere. “I was told you were looking to discuss your placement with my client?”

    “Are you fucking kidding me, Doug?” Kara says, her arms still tight around Lena like she could in some way prevent Lena from having to actually speak to the ghoulish specter of her mother, who’s apparently managing Eve and has been here this whole time. Maybe it wasn’t the recurring image of Kara’s ass in tight golf pants that was fucking Lena up, but the cursed presence of evil.

    “Uh, I apologize, ma’am, technically Miss Tessmacher’s representation is allowed to be present for a discussion,” Doug says. “Also, here’s Thomas Plarovich, he’s one of our tour officials on site!”

    “Nice to meet you, Miss Danvers,” Thomas says. “And Miss Luthor, uh - are you alright?”

    “She’s being dramatic, I’d imagine,” Lillian says. She sounds like a snake. Lena has to take a deep breath before she withdraws from Kara’s arms and turns to face them. Lillian is standing there with her arms crossed, Thomas Plarovich hovering on the opposite side of the room closest to the door with his hands in his pockets. “Do you always wear sunglasses inside, Miss Danvers?”

    “Thomas, it’s nice to meet you as well,” Kara says. She does not even attempt to mime removing her sunglasses. “We’d like to discuss changing our grouping for tomorrow’s round.”

    “As much as I’m sure our television affiliates would be unhappy, that is of course within your rights in the rules,” Thomas says, smiling very nicely. Lena can hear the shoe hovering above the drop.

    “Miss Tessmacher does not approve the switch,” Lillian says.

    “Unfortunately, all members of the group must approve the switch,” Thomas says, his hand gesturing in Lillian’s direction with a completely bland expression. Lena feels all good will toward him fade as Lillian smirks. 

    “Did you two rehearse this in the hallway?” Kara asks. “I was told, to my face, that it was of the opinion of the WPGA legal department that it was in the interests of mutual safety that Lena was to stay as far away from Miss Tessmacher and her team as possible. Is this no longer true?”

    “If you feel that Lena is a threat to anyone’s safety, Miss Danvers, feel free to say so,” Lillian says. “Otherwise, Miss Tessmacher and her team do not have any such requests with the association. Bygones are bygones.”

    “Are you kidding me?” Kara asks. “Thomas, are you fucking kidding me?”

    “Miss Danvers, I assure you that this tournament’s integrity is of utmost importance to the WPGA and we have made our decision in light of that,” Thomas says. 

    “You’ve made your decision in light of an opportunity to further take advantage of an intensely personal situation, you asshole,” Kara says. She’s pointing a finger now. 

    “Miss Danvers,” Thomas says. 

    “She has as much of a temper as you do, Lena,” Lillian says, sounding amused. 

    “Don’t talk to her,” Kara says, stepping fully in front of Lena. 

    “I assure you I am fully capable of speaking to my daughter with civility and respect, things you seem to be lacking,” Lillian says. She sounds bored now. Lena can see Kara’s spine straightening ever higher, to the point that she has to reach out and grab for the back of Kara’s belt before she decides to charge on either of the other two in the room.

    “You’re an asshole, too,” Kara says. “Whatever. Thomas, we’ll play.”

    “Alright, Miss Danvers,” Thomas says. “If you have any further concerns, feel free to reach out. And please, try to refrain from causing a stir with the media outside with your language. We like to appear clean cut.”

    “Right,” Kara says. Lena can hear her whisper asshole as the door gently closes, leaving them in a training room with Lillian Luthor. “Come on, let’s get out of here before I burn down the building.”

    “Actually, Miss Danvers, before you commit arson, I’d like to speak to my daughter,” Lillian says, her voice fake gentle. Kara actually laughs. “Alone.”

    Kara laughs louder.

    “Okay,” Lena says, before Kara can give whatever expletive-laden response rests on her tongue. Kara almost hits her with her bag as she swings around to stare at Lena, looking gobsmacked. Lena tries to impart strength and assurance into her gaze, pressing her hand to Kara’s hip in a gentle shove. Kara seems less than impressed by it, considering her jaw goes tight. “Go ahead.”

    “Fine,” Kara says, after a few tense seconds. “I’ll go see if I can find a back way out of this place to get to our car. I’ll be back in three minutes tops.”

    She almost leans down to kiss at Lena’s forehead, clear as day, but Lena gives a minute shake of her head that seems to worsen Kara’s mood. Her frown dips heavy. She moves through the doorway and Lena hears multiple voices shout Kara! with a wince. It’ll probably be more than three minutes.

    “She seems very polite,” Lillian says, her arms placed so approachably at her sides while Lena tries to breathe around the hot feeling in her head and the way her hands are buzzing. “Is it true that you found her at a public golf course? Or was it - I don’t know - Tinder?”

    “Why are you doing this to me?” Lena asks. Lillian laughs, her head shaking as she looks up to the ceiling and back down again. 

    “Sweetheart, you’ve always assumed the worst of people. I’m not attempting to do anything to you, and neither is Lex. He hasn’t even spoken to you, and yet you and your little blonde thing seem to think we’re on the verge of executing you,” Lillian says. She drifts to the side of the room, her fingers sliding along a display of irons. “Jack misses you.”

    “I know,” Lena says, raising her chin in as much defiance as she can muster. Something about talking to Lillian has always felt more like a battle than an exchange. Any other person, and Lena might reassure them that she and Jack had talked, that things were being worked out, but she suspects that if she told this infernal woman that, Jack would be out of a job in minutes. “I don’t care.”

    Lillian scoffs. 

    “Of course you care,” Lillian says. “You always care so much. It makes for bad golf.”

    It feels like a bomb in Lena’s head. Thankfully, the door comes open again.

    “Hi, Miss Luthor, my name’s Nia Nal,” the girl in the door says, smiling very gently. She’s got on a visor and a very cute skirt and pullover combination. “Kara sent me to get you through the back entrance?”

    “Thank you,” Lena says. Nia looks over at Lillian with a similar smile to the one she gave Lena, coming over and offering wordlessly to lift Lena’s bag. Lena lets her, lets herself get led down a back service hallway to a car, climbs in and lays across the back seat and waits and waits and waits for Kara to come back, until she does, and it’s almost like she can breathe again.

    -

    “Today is the anniversary. Of my parents,” Kara says, as she kneels on the ground and taps at the back of Lena’s foot until she nudges it forward a little bit. Whatever esoteric thing they’re doing seems to be centering the both of them in the drizzly morning. Lena’s hitting putt after putt with a laser and a line and Kara’s constant adjustments to her stance. She wonders if it’s an excuse to touch her. Lena watches Kara’s head as she finishes adjusting Lena. When she stands up again, her face is tight and shockingly cold, for Kara. 

    “We’re gonna have a rough day,” Lena says. Kara laughs a little, squaring her feet up and crossing her arms. She hasn’t yet draped her caddie bib over her shoulders, so her bright orange pullover draws all manner of attention to her biceps. 

    Lena may be on her way to a mental breakdown, but at least she has that.

    “Yeah,” Kara says, shrugging. “I think that’s probable.”

    -

    Lex doesn’t speak a damn word to her. He follows along the course, Eve straying close to him at the top of the tee box where he’s hovering behind the ropes so he can mime swings at her. Jack gives Lena more than a few loaded glances, and Eve plays well. Every time Lena takes a shot, she can feel Lex’s gaze on her, and it brings her suddenly back to a year ago, his frowns and his groans of frustration with bad shots. He doesn’t do that with Eve, really, even when she takes a chip from the sand on the fourth hole that moves more sand than ball. 

    On the eighth hole, Lena hits water. She can hear the groan of the crowd behind her as she does it, and then Kara’s huff as she picks up the bag and reaches for the driver in Lena’s hands. By the time they arrive to their spot, the crowd’s moved with them. Jack and Eve are on the narrow fairway about thirty feet away, and Lena’s ball is clutched between a thick patch of grass and the lip of the water, where the out of bounds line is spray painted. And Lena can still feel Lex watching her.

    “Can your pants even roll up?” Kara asks, dropping the bag and digging out a nine iron.

    “You think I’m going in there?” Lena asks. But she’s already kicking her shoes off.

    “Oh, for sure,” Kara says. “You’ll probably fall in, too. SportsCenter not top ten material.”

    “You want me to fail more than he does,” Lena jokes. But Kara frowns, pulling her sunglasses off her eyes and wiping sweat away for a second, leaning closer to Lena as she works her shoes off and pulls her socks. 

    “You know that isn’t true,” Kara says. “Right?” 

    “I know,” Lena says, kneeling to start rolling her pant legs up. It’s hard to judge the depth of the water, and she’s having trouble remembering right now. Kara kneels down as well, reaching to help, her fingers soft as she knocks Lena’s hands away.

    “No, like,” Kara says, then huffs. “I know how we say that we’re on each other’s side and shit, but you know that when I say that at least, it’s like - in a big, cosmic way, right? You’ve got me. I’ve got you. I would never want you to fail anything ever.”

    “I know,” Lena says, softer, reaching for Kara’s elbow and squeezing. She’s aware, in some sense, that a whole world is watching them. That her Twitter and Instagram that Sam’s been half-heartedly running will probably blow up all over again with images and references to this. Kara taps her thigh to get her to switch feet, her very tight pants successfully maneuvered up to her knee on one side. “I know, darling.”

    “Okay,” Kara says, and Lena’s half-surprised and half not to hear the tremor in Kara’s voice. She squeezes harder at her elbow, even though her real urge is to pull Kara in and hold her. “Sorry, I just - I always feel like, today, I always feel like I have to tell everyone I love that I love them like, so much. I bought Alex a fucking edible arrangement.”

    “What’d you buy me?” Lena asks, as Kara folds her pant leg up. 

    “A loss in this tournament,” Kara says. Lena snorts. “And a dress.”

    “I think I’m buying myself a loss in this tournament,” Lena says, letting Kara pull her up and feeling the grass under her feet. The tight feeling in her chest has drifted a little bit under Kara’s steady hands, but when she hears some errant claps from the crowd and sees Eve muttering something to Jack, it slips back in. 

    Kara helps her back into the pond, which thankfully turns out to be not so deep. She’s not as choked up on her iron as she had been anticipating. The ball doesn’t move when she steps into the cold water, drizzle falling all around her. She tries to just look up and see Kara, but it’s hard too, to not feel the presence at her back, watching her, judging her.

    She pulls back and swings before she can wallow in it too much. She splashes water up, the ball goes careening wildly, right up against the spectator pool at most only forty yards away, and she does indeed fall in.

    -

    “How’d you feel about your performance today, Lena?” a reporter asks. She’s at a press conference, a towel wrapped around her neck and wearing Kara’s pullover after she had thrown her disgusting, damp polo in the trash. Its arms are a bit too long for her, but there’s something comforting about her hands being covered as she leans on the podium. 

    “Well, it wasn’t great,” Lena says. “It was frustrating. But I guess that’s golf.”

    “Your caddie, Kara Danvers, looked like she was giving you some advice before you climbed into the water on eight. Can you share what she said to you?”

    “No,” Lena says, shrugging. “Golfer-caddie confidentiality.”

    There’s a small round of laughter up around the room, but Lena’s beyond tired, feels fried almost. Kara is leaning against the wall of the pen in her untucked polo, hat pulled low, a small smile on her face. Next to her is that girl who had escaped her from the clubhouse yesterday, Nia.

    “Your brother Lex was out on the course today coaching Eve Tessmacher today. Have you thought about rehiring or replacing him as a coach, considering your last few results?” someone asks. Lena twists her fingers in the extra fabric covering her hand and smiles.

    “We’ll see,” Lena says. She had fired Lex in so many words by destroying his Maserati, and she hadn’t thought to hire a new coach even once. Considering she connotated his coaching with terror over doing anything wrong, that was maybe not surprising.

    “Both your camps have said that your relationship is fine after the incident last year,” another reporter says. Lena feels ice up through her veins, her expression staying in place as she waits for whatever this will be. “Tell us about how it felt to have him out there, even if he wasn’t working with you. ”

    Lena feels, for a second, like saying it felt terrible, that Lex’s eyes on her on a golf course made her feel like her skin was sloughing off. But there’s a lingering, needling sense that her family is her family. 

    “It was nice,” Lena says, then gives a half-hearted shrug. “Wish I could have played better.”

    Chapter 3: when to go home

    Chapter Notes

    hello :) sorry for the delay :) love you, go golf :)

    NATIONAL CITY, UNITED STATES

    Sam and Ruby pick them up from the airport. Kara is dressed down in jeans and a t-shirt, hair down with it. Lena’s in a Nike sweatshirt and jeans, a hat, sunglasses, the whole shebang. 

    “Hi Aunt Lena!” Ruby cheerfully greets when Lena slides into the front seat. Kara and Sam are negotiating the Tetris of getting their bags and Lena’s clubs into the car. She’s settled into the back seat on her phone, flicking her fingers around.

    “Hi, Rubes,” Lena says. She’s tired from the flight, having really only just fallen asleep as they crossed over Denver. Kara had passed out in her lap nearly as soon as they had taken off, her face burrowed into Lena’s stomach. They had spent all night in Singapore wrapped in the sheets of their bed, eating pizza and ice cream and intermittently crying. Kara had whispered little facts and memories about her parents and Lena had held her hand. 

    “Wow, cool sunglasses,” Ruby says, leaning halfway into the front seat to tap at the arm of Lena’s reflective aviators. “You’re like, a badass.”

    “Ruby,” Sam warns, climbing into the front seat of her Highlander and glaring at Ruby. Kara closes the trunk, jogging around the side to open the passenger door. She actually does jog, giving a quick smile to one of the poor workers of National City International Airport who has to shoo people from the pick-up lane. 

    “Mom, I’m sixteen, I can say badass,” Ruby says, rolling her eyes. “I’m not saying like, motherfucker or anything.”

    “You literally just said the word motherfucker,” Sam says. “And I will change our wifi password.”

    “I have LTE,” Ruby says.

    “I will hide every charger in the house,” Sam says. “See what you can do with your LTE after your phone dies in four hours.”

    “Only because you won’t upgrade us!” Ruby exclaims. Lena can’t help but laugh at that one, feeling Kara’s hand land on her shoulder and press quickly into a knot that has her whole body shivering. “Whatever. Hi, I’m Ruby.”

    “I’m Kara,” Kara says amiably. “Nice to meet you. I’ve heard a lot about you from your mom.”

    “Same,” Ruby says, her jaw cocking up as she looks Kara up and down. “You know, I thought you’d be cooler looking in person.”

    “Ruby,” Sam says again, looking like she may smash her face into her steering wheel to avoid parenting any further. Lena’s pretty sure that ends up with Ruby under her control, so she gives a gentle smile to Sam and pats her thigh.

    “You can’t censor literally everything I say,” Ruby says. “And whatever, it’s a compliment. You dress cool on the course. Even kids who don’t know anything about golf keep retweeting your outfits. There’s this girl who’s a friend of mine who’s obsessed with you.”

    “With me?” Kara asks, sounding amused while she digs into Lena’s shoulder. It’s a mystery how she knew it was aching from how she fell asleep on the plane, but she’s grateful all the same.

    “Yeah, but I mean like, she’s gay and she’s on the golf team, so it’s not like a rando,” Ruby says. “She can hit her pitching wedge like ninety. I hate her.”

    “Ruby’s in love with her,” Sam interjects.

    “Mom!”

    “She’s always Snapchatting her. I didn’t even know people still Snapchatted, but I guess they do when they’re in love,” Sam says. 

    “Dang, that’s so true,” Kara says. “I deleted Snapchat when I broke up with my ex-girlfriend.”

    This easy commentary gains Lena a look from Sam that seems ascertaining, like she’s trying to scope out how exactly Lena feels about this statement. Lena resolves to lean her head back to give up more of her shoulder to Kara’s touch, shutting her eyes as they merge onto the crowded freeway.

    “You’re gay?” Ruby asks, sounding suddenly as though she might cry. 

    “So gay,” Kara says. “Why?”

    “Is it true then that you and Aunt Lena are together and in love?” Ruby asks, clearly having thrown all caution to the wind. It makes Lena snort, picking her head up off the headrest and glancing Sam’s way. 

    “Ruby!” Sam says. 

    “Because Katie says that you aren’t and I want to prove it to her that I’m right, and then I have to tell all of my Twitter followers - Aunt Lena, I run this Twitter account to honor you, mom uses it for like, analytics or something and she refuses to let me run your actual Twitter account - but if I just, lean into it, I think we’ll pick up a lot of followers,” Ruby says, in a whirlwind. 

    “We are not using anyone’s relationship for social media clout,” Sam says. She adds, voice somewhat considering: “Until they consent to it.”

    “That’s why I’m asking for their consent, mom,” Ruby says. “God.”

    “We’re going to talk about how you ask people for consent later,” Sam says. “For a while. At least an hour.”

    “Fine, whatever,” Ruby says. “But, so, Kara - ”

    “Kara, as your de facto manager, do not answer any of her questions,” Sam says.

    “Right on,” Kara agrees easily. Her hand rests over Lena’s collarbone, warming it until she dozes off.

    -

    The sun is out in National City, and Lena settles herself in the shadow left by the umbrella on the patio of a restaurant close to Lena’s house. Ruby’s gone off to do something with someone, per Sam, and Sam’s taken them for a “business meeting.” Kara, of course, sits on the sunny side of their bench seat, tilting her head back and letting the sun crawl over her. 

    “First things first, I wanted to check in on how you guys' partnership is going,” Sam says. She says it very politely, and Lena sees in real time her eyes track Kara’s arm as she throws it over the back of the bench near Lena’s shoulder. She can’t help but laugh.

    “Are you also trying to see if Kara and I are together and in love?” Lena asks. Kara snorts, reaching for her glass of water and taking a sip. She looks gorgeous in the sun, her eyes twinkling as she makes a small noise of excitement at a passing dog. 

    “No, I’m asking about your business relationship,” Sam says. “I have eyes and a better gaydar than my sixteen year-old daughter. I don’t need an answer to that question.”

    “It’s good,” Lena says. “We don’t have any problems understanding each other.”

    “And you are comfortable with the status of your current agreement?” Sam asks, happily taking her margarita from the waitress’s hands as she comes by. Kara’s eyes nearly bug out of her head when her rum runner is placed in front of her.

    “I am,” Lena says. She bumps Kara, who’s sipping greedily at her drink.  

    “I am,” Kara echoes. 

    “Good,” Sam says, scratching something off her list of items. “Cool. Then I won’t have to cancel your hotel rooms for the rest of the season?”

    “Nope,” Kara says, popping her lips as she leans over to sniff Lena’s whiskey sour and makes a grossed out face. “We’re good to go.”

    “Wonderful,” Sam says. “Well, here’s what I’ve got going for you guys since you’re on a mini-vacay: a therapy appointment on Wednesday. It’s two hours long. You’re welcome. And then a consult for training updates on Thursday, then a bunch of sponsorship meet-ups. Nike, Vokey, NetJets. I booked you two for a round at National City Pub for Friday afternoon, added myself because I want to see that gorgeous girl at the turn bar - ”

    “Obviously,” Kara nods. 

    “ - booked you for another round next Friday as well, added myself for the turn bar girl again, added Ruby so she wouldn’t complain,” Sam says. “I got your pool cleaned and the house cleaned and your tires checked as well.”

    “Uh, okay,” Lena says, glancing Kara’s way. She’s eyeing the food menu as her thumb traces Lena’s shoulder. “In what sense?”

    “To manage the ins and outs of your life on tour,” Sam says. “But also so I don’t have to waste my time calling the clubhouse at the Pub when I need to be managing your accounts and contracts and high-level stuff. Someone who can make sure your caddie here doesn’t blast your family in an interview.”

    “I’m not going to apologize,” Kara huffs. There’s been more than a few clips of Kara in the clubhouse in Singapore fresh off calling Lillian Luthor an asshole rolling her eyes at the mention of Lex’s name.

    “I’m not asking you to,” Sam says. “And I know you two want your vibe to keep vibing. But honestly, Lena, you’re a big enough brand that having an assistant on tour with you twenty four seven would be good. Especially as this season progresses and more gays catch onto Kara’s dweeb clothes.”

    “ Dweeb clothes?” Kara asks, tugging at her t-shirt and frowning. “Why is she attacking me?”

    “Do you have any suggestions?” Lena asks, patting Kara’s thigh as she pouts and grabs for her drink. 

    “Nope,” Sam says, quite cheerfully.

    “Helpful,” Lena says.

    “I also think you should consider getting a coach again,” Sam says. “Just for some maintenance.”
