Her club is still not in her hand, so she turns to look at Kara, who has set the bag down and is watching the conversation, hands splayed protectively over the mass of clubs. “We have a week on the course. I’ve played it before.”

    “If you put the shot in your routine now, it will be rote by Sunday,” Brainy says.

    “Why don’t we focus on Friday first?” Lena says. 

    “Sundays are the only goal worth focusing on,” Brainy says. “It is when championships are awarded.”

    “I know when championships are awarded,” Lena snaps. Kara does something weird with the clubs that shakes the irons around and distracts Lena. When she turns back to Brainy, he is tilting his head curiously.

    “I believe that you are capable of winning every tournament you play in, Miss Luthor,” Brainy says. “Your talent is immense. Your tactics and choices and frustrations are what hold you back. I advise you to take the shot again.”

    When the club arrives in her hand, gentle as a lamb, it’s a driver. Lena is forced to walk back to the tee, takes the shot again, and it careens over the trees with a rainbow grace and lands within forty yards of the green. She does not give Brainy the satisfaction of acknowledging it, but Kara whoops so loudly that she frightens some birds from the trees.

    -

    “I know how to make a grasshopper,” Kara mutters. “Where’s the blender?” She’s wearing a golf polo, boxers, and a backwards hat, and she is more than a little tipsy. Sam, thankfully, is also very tipsy, slumped against the counter. 

    “You’re supposed to do your contest tomorrow,” Sam says. It’s three in the morning. Lena is at her kitchen counter and consciously avoiding even thinking about where the blender is. Kara doesn’t even have mint.

    “Babe, where’s the blender?” Kara asks, turning blue puppy eyes on her. Lena maintains her steadfast devotion to not even considering the blender as an object. Sam snorts. Ruby thankfully had been sent to bed at midnight.

    “Yeah, babe, where’s the blender?” Sam asks. She’s poking fun, Lena knows, at the not well-kept secret that Kara and Lena are together. All the same, Lena flips her off. Sam flips her off right back.

    “Babyface, blender,” Kara says.

    “There’s no creme de menthe,” Lena says, shoving at Kara’s ribs when she comes close to try to get her way through physical contact. She’s also still maintaining her flipped bird at Sam, who is now bringing up a twin to her own middle finger. 

    “Creme de what?” Kara asks. She very helpfully joins the process of demolishing Sam, leaning on Lena’s side and flipping Sam off.

    “It’s in grasshoppers, dummy,” Sam says. Kara throws another middle finger up, and now Sam is truly beaten. “You should sleep. Competition.”

    “I’m not sleeping until I make a grasshopper,” Kara says. She’s crouching on the ground to flip open one of the fourteen billion cabinets in the kitchen, but then she seems to get distracted by Lena’s legs dangling from her stool, her fingers tracing onto Lena’s ankle.

    “Does she know that you put down a million dollars of your own money for charity and that if she does well in the contest two million dollars will go to charity?” Sam says, her sentence see-sawing along with the waves of her drunkenness. Lena is very distracted by Kara’s hands on her ankle. Maybe she could break her rule and let Kara use one of those strap-ons she had packed. Sex can’t possibly affect her swing that bad, can it?

    “I know,” Kara says, from the ground. That she’s now sitting on, having apparently forgotten her quest for the blender and focusing entirely on rubbing at Lena’s calf where a strain had popped up after she tripped over a divot. 

    “You should sleep,” Lena says, looking down at Kara, who tilts her big blue eyes and cute little glasses up at her and full-on pouts. It immediately makes Lena regret the very thought. 

    “She probably won’t sleep without you,” Sam says, the asshole. Lena remembers to reassert her dominance by flipping Sam off again. “Oh, I’m so sorry I’m onto your lesbian antics. You’re lucky Ruby hasn’t figured out you two are boning.”

    “We’re not boning,” Kara says. She says it so despairingly that Sam starts laughing very loudly. Lena is absolutely certain that even Brainy could hear it in the guest house, and so she tries to lean across the counter and smack at Sam. 

    “Shut up,” Lena hisses. Sam is still laughing, so, so hard, and then Kara is laughing, too, down at Lena’s feet.

    And then Lena is laughing too.

    -

    “I’m going to die,” Sam mutters. She’s wearing sunglasses. Realistically, everyone around them is wearing sunglasses, because it’s quite sunny. Kara and Brainy are standing a few feet away, with Brainy’s hand on the crown of Kara’s head as she swings over and over with her pitching wedge. Ruby is on her phone, texting frantically, or Snapping, or something - Lena has no idea - allegedly minding Lena’s bag. 

    “You’ve been saying that all morning,” Lena says. Her head is throbbing. Brainy had gracefully not mentioned her puking in a trash can while they walked from the second to the third.

    “Yeah, and it’s true,” Sam says. “I haven’t felt this nauseous since I was pregnant.”

    “You let Kara make you grasshopper missing its key ingredient at three in the morning, not me,” Lena says. 

    “I’m blaming you anyway,” Sam says, shifting her feet and nibbling on a packet of crackers Kara had given her out of the bag on twelve. She’s managed to eat one cracker out of the six in the package, and they’re on seventeen now. “Fuck me, these people are loud.”

    They watch as Adam steps up to the tee and the crowd quiets. Kara is still swinging with Brainy’s hand on her head a few feet behind him and in front of them. Cat Grant is watching her son/caddie with a half-smile. He swings; the crowd immediately starts screeching, and then his ball smacks on the green in the middle of the pond loud enough it carries back to the tee. It’s hard to tell where it landed from here because of the awkward slope of the green, but the crowd seems to like it.

    “Lena, one of my friends bet me NewJeans tickets that Kara wouldn’t hit the green. What an idiot,” Ruby says, looking excited. When Lena spares a passing glance to Sam in the hopes that she can reveal what NewJeans is, Sam shrugs. 

    “How do you have friends who bet on a charity caddie competition?” Lena asks, instead. Ruby looks at her, and then at Kara, who looks more like a pro golfer than half the caddies on the course. She’s wearing shorts printed with little golf balls and a blue polo that brings out the blue of her new Ray-Bans. She had woken up very cheerfully this morning, because she had chosen to puke up all her alcohol just before going to sleep. Lena had hated it until Kara had started rubbing her head and kissing her eyebrows.

    “My friend is super into any gay girl who gets like, even a little bit famous,” Ruby says. “I think it’s a complex. Speaking of - ”

    “No official Instagram,” Sam interjects. “I’m hiring an assistant for that.”

    “I could be the assistant!” Ruby says. “Aunt Lena, you wouldn’t even have to pay me. I ran the numbers and posts that have Kara in them, especially with you, are really popular! You just have to lean into the rumors. I mean, which, you’d obviously have to tell me if they were true, because I’d be your assistant - ” 

    “Oh my God,” Sam says. “You’re a scamp.”

    “I’m not a scamp,” Ruby says. “Aunt Lena, I’m not a scamp.”

    Thankfully, this discussion is interrupted by someone with a megaphone announcing that it’s Kara’s turn to take the tee and also announcing Adam’s distance from the hole - a solid six feet that he pumps a fist at. Brainy and Kara do an extremely intricate high five that takes more than a few seconds to complete - and then Kara slings her wedge up onto her shoulder and starts strolling over to the tee.

    Lena joins her at the front of the pack, bumping into her side.

    “What’re you thinkin’, pal?” Kara asks, adjusting her hat on her head and talking in some weird old-timey voice. Lena adores her. Instead of doing what she wants to and slinging her arms around Kara’s shoulders and kissing her, she bumps into her again.

    “I’m thinking that Ruby is ready to sell us out to TMZ for hard cash,” Lena mutters, holding her hand over her mouth. Kara snorts, turning to glance at Ruby and Sam and Brainy and giving a little wave and a thumbs up. 

    “I have to admire the investigative spirit,” Kara says. “We should start her on true crime podcasts. That’ll distract her.”

    “And traumatize her with stories of women getting dismembered?”

    “Trauma builds character,” Kara says. “Obviously, we’re both very well adjusted.”

    “Go swing,” Lena says, patting Kara on the arm. Kara nods dutifully, her old-timey, weirdly British accent coming back as she starts backing away and bows towards Lena.

    “As you wish.”

    Lena takes a few steps back as Kara takes a few steps forward, standing off the tee and giving one swing, then two. This is Kara’s whole golf routine, amusingly. She swings once for the motion, swings twice for the distance - Lena hears the swish of her wedge brushing the grass as she does the second one - and then she steps onto the tee and swings the third time. It’s firm, precise, decisive. She doesn’t wait, or think, or step off when the tension in her body goes too tight. It’s nearly everything Lena wishes her own process could be. 

    The ball sounds good coming off the club, and it rainbows perfectly over the water, toward the hole, and drops - and then it disappears. Kara looks somewhat confused for a second, her stance loose as she glances back at Lena. And then the crowd bursts into a raucous display, people screaming, beer flying everywhere and several people jumping up and down. 

    “ Hole in one ,” comes the announcer’s voice on the other end of the hole. “We have a hole-in-one!”

    Lena feels some sort of euphoria grip ahold of her. This is her only explanation. Kara turns back to her, a great big smile on her face as she starts ambling toward Lena, looking pleased as punch - but she just hit a hole in one on TPC Sawgrass seventeen - and Lena is happy. She’s happy, and she throws her arms around Kara’s neck and kisses her. 

    -

    Sam’s search for an assistant ends that night with a familiar looking girl standing in Lena’s living room and looking at a display of Lena’s old chess trophies. Lena struggles to remember her name until Kara comes ambling in with Brainy at her side, the two of them arguing about something in her yardage book.

    “Nia!” Kara exclaims, looking suddenly thrilled. Sam frowns.

    “You two have met?” Sam asks. “How many people do you know?”

    “I’m friendly,” Kara says, waving that off and coming closer to Nia and wrapping her into a hug. “What’re you doing here?”

    “She’s you two’s new assistant,” Sam says. “She’ll handle stuff like scheduling, transpo, lodging, social media.”

    At social media, she throws a glare over at Ruby, whose video of Lena and Kara kissing on the teebox at seventeen got put on ESPN top ten. Ruby looks unrepentant, texting away on the couch. Nia laughs, squeezes hard at Kara’s shoulders before withdrawing.

    “Leaving the dark side of Doug Weemsdale, I’m a fan,” Kara says. “Welcome to the team. This is Brainy, he’s - ”

    “Querl Dox, mental strength coach,” Brainy says, stepping up to Kara’s side and actually doing a funny little half bow. Kara throws a look of pure confusion at him that he steadfastly ignores. Nia laughs, shaking his hand.

    “Nice to meet you,” Nia says. “I listened to one of your podcasts when news broke that Miss Luthor hired you.”

    “Oh,” Brainy says, and then he sort of - honks, like maybe he’s trying to laugh. Kara now directs her look of confusion at Lena, who shrugs in amusement before dropping onto the couch next to Ruby, who immediately begins burrowing into Lena’s side. Lena actively sees a WhatsApp thread on Ruby’s phone titled “GOLF GAYS WIN ALWAYS” that she is furiously typing in. 

    “Right,” Kara says, patting Brainy on the shoulder. “We were gonna watch Star Wars, speaking of the dark side. You wanna stick around? There’s like, ten guest rooms in this mansion too.”

    “Oh, um, if that - is that - ” Nia says, looking from Sam to Lena. Sam has wandered off to the kitchen to rummage through the drinks fridge. Lena smiles, and it feels easy. Kara is crowding onto the couch next to Lena, leaning around her to try to look down at what Ruby is typing up. It’s so different than last year, watching a course breakdown with Lex and Jack, nervous about Thursday. 

    “Of course,” Lena says. “You’re part of the team now.”

    -

    Lena wins. 

    She shoots a six under on Thursday, Friday, and Saturday, and on Sunday, she ends her round in an all-black outfit in the hot Florida sun with a seven under and a very shiny trophy. She’s never once considered or really even cared about winning the Players. It was always about the majors with Lex and Lillian. And so it had been with her. But the trophy is heavy, and shiny, and Kara picks her up off the ground when she sinks her putt and she laughs so hard that she cries. In the clubhouse, Kara picks her up again, puts her up against a locker, Lena drops the trophy - and Lena arrives at her press conference ten minutes late and somewhat dazed.

    Lena sits down for the media and smiles, and she finds that she isn’t nervous. It’s more perfect than the most perfect. It’s lucky. Kara is hovering at the side of the room with Nia and Sam and Ruby, all of them badged up and grinning.

    “Hey Lena, what would you say led to your amazing performance the past few days?” says someone towards the back. Lena laughs, takes a sip of her water.

    “Honestly, I think I’m just in a good mood,” Lena says, and the room laughs with her. “I was just having fun, playing golf. I didn’t worry or overthink and just played my game.”

    “You have a new coach, Querl Dox - how does he compare to working with your brother?” asks another reporter. Lena’s happiness is briefly impeded by the mere mention of her brother, but she smiles anyway and shrugs.

    “Obviously it’s different to work with someone you haven’t known since you were four,” Lena says. “I’m happy Querl and I’s partnership seems to be a good one.”

    It’s a non-answer so palpable that even Lillian might have been proud. The reporter doesn’t look thrilled by it, but dutifully makes notes anyway. 

    -

    The doorbell rings at almost midnight. Kara and Nia are in a heated debate over whether “quale” is a word acceptable for Scrabble, Sam is singing loudly to “You Oughta Know” and Brainy is swinging one of Lena’s practice clubs over and over so that Ruby can watch him. They’d tried to make Ruby go to bed more than once, but it hadn’t stuck. 

    Lena is the one who ambles into the massive foyer that Lillian had called gauche when she had picked the house from the options presented to her - a bit judgmental considering Lillian was the one who had presented Lena with the options.

    And so Lena is the one who opens the door to find Jack Spheer standing there with a duffel bag slung over his shoulder and a sheepish smile on his face.

    “Hi,” he says, shifting his shoulder and the heavy bag draped there. And Lena feels her heart sink just as suddenly as it had become buoyant.

    -

    “What a fucking joke,” Sam says. She’s immediately glared at by a nearby mother with a gaggle of children, and gets laughed at by Ruby, who’s wearing Mickey Mouse ears bedecked with a tuxedo and “happily ever after” written across them. Kara had gleefully bought them for her in a gift shop while Jack was relaying his sad tale to Sam.

    The long and short of it being his recent firing as Eve’s caddy by a combination of Lillian and Lex Luthor.

    “I wish I could say I was surprised,” Lena says. Sam takes a gulp from her massive beer stein and glares impassively toward the buffet where Jack and Kara had absconded to. 

    “Your family sucks, Aunt Lena,” Ruby says, in some form of agreement. Sam widens her eyes, but doesn’t scold her daughter. Probably because it’s true.

    “I’m gonna look at his contract when we get home,” Sam says. “But I doubt there’s anything there.”

    “Me either,” Lena agrees.

    “Me either,” Ruby says, looking all-too-solemn in her Mickey Mouse ears intended for recently-married men. “Can we go ride the golf ball after this?”

    “Sure,” Sam says. “Judi Dench’s calming tones are what I need after hearing about this bullshit. And more beer. Where’s our waitress?”

    There’s a band assembling on stage. Lena finds herself watching them, in their culturally accurate lederhosen, and watches the accordion player flick his fingers and up and down the keys. It makes her think, unnaturally, of Lex teaching her where to place her hands on a golf club when she was five. It’s one of her first real memories, his much larger hands squeezing at hers, her gripping the shaft of a too-large club until she had blisters. And Lillian smiling when she encountered them in the basement at Luthor Manor. It had been one of the first times Lillian had ever looked at her with anything other than contempt. 

    Lena wonders what it is that Lillian saw in that moment. Her newly adopted daughter who had probably seemed an inconvenience up to that point, bonding with her well-loved son. Maybe she realized in that moment that she had something worth money, millions of dollars, right in front of her. Maybe all of the moments that had come after that had been illegitimate; Lex pushed by Lillian to see the virtue of dealing with Lena and her moods, the two of them convincing Lena to see the virtue of golf as her only meaningful contribution to the Luthor family.

    And now that Lena had soundly been kicked from the family and found the grass on the other side greener - anyone who had helped Lena, who had seen Lena and not Luthor - they were most likely in their firing line. Jack was the first domino. And the last - 

    “Some girl just tried to tell me that I couldn’t take seven sausages, but I said it was for my family so if you could just look like you wanted some of these, that would be great,” Kara says, dropping an obscene amount of sausages on the table and climbing onto the bench next to Lena. Ruby immediately buys into the plan and stabs one of them with a savagery that would make any Disney employee believe that she had been on the verge of death from starvation. Sam looks less thrilled, but she eventually grabs for her fork. 

    “Where’s Jack?” Lena asks. Kara has to swallow around the huge piece she’s just bitten off a sausage.

    “He’s on the phone with someone at Callaway,” Kara says. “Tried to call in some favor for a job on the tour? Doesn’t sound like it’s going well.”

    Lena looks across at Sam, who is chewing contemplatively on the sausage. She doesn’t look surprised. Lena can only imagine the freezeout Jack has found himself in for no fault of his own. Lillian and Lex would find any way they could to keep him cornered. And to keep Lena cornered.

    “We’re going to the golf ball next, Kara,” Ruby says. Kara claps like a seal, and Lena breaks from her misery-laden reverie to smile at her very stupid girlfriend. 

    “I’m pumped,” Kara says. “Where’s our refills, though?”

    “Missing in action,” Sam mutters. She gets distracted by Ruby holding up her phone to take a selfie with her, and Lena sighs gratefully when Kara’s warm hand presses into the small of her back and her low voice arrives in her ear.

    “You okay?” Kara asks. Lena is demonstrably not okay, considering she had spent all of last night crying in Kara’s arms and had woken up with a perma-frown on her lips. But Kara has been plying her all day, has been chipping at the quick layer of ice that Lena had thrown up around herself. It’s what she does. It’s why Lena loves her. A warm hand digging in through the cold to touch Lena, skin-to-skin.

    “No, not really,” Lena says. Kara nods, drops a kiss to her shoulder that Ruby sees and probably takes a picture of for her weird WhatsApp group. “I’m worried.”

    “You worry too much,” Kara observes, reaching again for her be-sausaged fork and taking a bite. Lena feels anger rise quick up into her throat at Kara’s blase attitude. “Well, let me rephrase. The problem is real. But how much can you do about it?”

    “I can withdraw from the season and retire,” Lena says. “Become a llama breeder or something.”

    “I wasn’t meant to live on a farm,” Kara says. “So no. We promised each other. We finish this season, and then after that, who knows.”

    “When did we promise that?” Lena asks, watching Kara chew on her sausage and swallow and finding herself hopelessly, terrifyingly endeared. This is too precious to lose. And now the abstract fear is clear and present and lurking.

    Kara looks back at her with her blue eyes and a little smirk on her face, her hand still on Lena’s back and her thumb in circles over the fabric. And Lena lets it go, for now, for this moment, as the band starts up a rousing round of noise. 

    -

    GEORGIA, UNITED STATES - AUGUSTA NATIONAL GOLF COURSE - MASTERS TOURNAMENT

    “Lena, we see your former caddy and Eve Tessmacher’s former caddy, Jack Spheer, has joined your team for at least this tournament. What kind of guidance are you looking for from Jack?”

    Lena is wearing dark enough sunglasses that she doesn’t hold off on rolling her eyes. Kara is hovering a few feet away over the bag, watching her with her jumpsuit half undone. The rueful smile that Kara throws her way is the only thing that prevents Lena from snapping.

    “Jack’s a good friend and a good golf mind. He knows my game and has been with me here before. When I learned he was open for a job, my team and I felt like we couldn’t not add him,” Lena says. It’s the fifth time she’s said something like this on camera in the two days they’ve been in Georgia. 

    “Eve Tessmacher just released a statement saying that Mr. Spheer had been involved in fraternization with other golfers on tour, to the point that she and her team - which includes your brother as her coach and your mother as her manager - felt that he was no longer working for the betterment of her game and was perhaps, helping other golfers gain an advantage. Do you have anything to say to that?”

    Kara straightens up a little on the bag, and Lena feels herself do the same. There’s too much to process, it’s hot, and she’s sweating so much under her copious sunscreen that she must look like she went swimming just before the interview. 

    “I - have known Jack for a long time, and I have full confidence in his character,” Lena says. 

    The reporter in front of her, who is somehow allowed to wear shorts, keeps his dumb microphone in her face. 

    “Do you think that we’ll be seeing Mr. Spheer make an appearance on your bag this weekend? No offense to your wonderful caddy over there,” the reporter says, waving over to Kara. Kara does not bother to return his acknowledgment, looking like she’s on the verge of swinging a club at his face. 

    “No,” Lena says, through her teeth. 

    “Of course not,” the reporter says. “We all saw that kiss at the Players. You guys have an amazing connection. And I have to ask, will we get to see you and your brother working together on the Par 3 Contest tomorrow, just like old times?”

    “I doubt it,” Lena says, and she does actually laugh this time, and she knows that it’s the soundbite the reporter’s been digging for the whole time. He withdraws his mic with a big smile, reaching out to shake her hand - but Kara is already reaching for her, gripping her fingers steadily and starting to pull her from the reporter’s orbit.

    He doesn’t look too put out, turning to his cameraman and already muttering as Kara starts moving them toward the clubhouse. More than a few people stare at her, and it feels - too much. It feels like her skin is crawling and splitting open. 

    “You’re fine,” Kara mutters, leaning close and squeezing her hand. “He was an asshole.”

    “I need to see that statement,” Lena says back, turning her head away from any wayward cameras and speaking up into Kara’s ear. 

    “I’m sure Nia and Sam already have copies printed,” Kara says. “We have an appointment with your physical therapist and then we can shower and go debrief it.”

    “No, cancel James,” Lena huffs, as Kara shifts them into the security of the clubhouse and past a hub of WPGA reps. Lena spots Kara’s arch-enemy Doug Weemsdale amongst them. Kara scoffs at that one, flipping her hat by the brim off her forehead and looking down at Lena.

    “We’re not cancelling your physical therapist,” Kara says. “You have a tight knee. You scooped on ten instead of twisting through.”

    “My knee is fine,” Lena says. “Cancel James. Call the car early.”

    “Lena - ” 

    “Kara Danvers! Just who I’ve been looking for!” comes the crowing of someone down the hall with a sponsor badge hanging around their neck. More than a few other sponsor heads turn to look at them. Lena takes the moment to slip into the locker room, leaving Kara in her wake. 

    There are only a few golfers in the locker room at the moment. Cat Grant is on the phone and eyes her as Lena stalks past her to the corner she had claimed years ago. No one says anything to her, but the eyes follow her as she collapses onto the bench in front of her locker, and promptly realizes Kara has the keys. 

    She feels the familiar burst of anger bubbling in her, the same unhinged feeling that had made her break her seven iron on Lex’s stupid fucking car. Lena flexes her hands outward, tries to breathe, but she’s so irritated, so mad - at Eve Tessmacher getting used the same way that Lena had been used, at Kara for knowing her too well, at reporters looking to take a bite of her instead of letting her breathe. 

    There’s no one in the bay of lockers she’s at, but Lena tries to will back the tears in her chest. She kicks off her shoes, one of them thunking hard into the wood of the locker. She doesn’t even have her phone - that’s in the bag too, of course, and so she just ducks her head into her hands and listens to the quiet-loud of the locker room, people gearing up or gearing down.

    It takes some time, but Kara appears next to her eventually. Lena doesn’t pick up her head, but she knows it’s Kara without looking or even listening. Kara treats her with a grace that she doesn’t deserve, has never deserved, and when Kara whispers a small hey, and her hand presses onto the fabric covering Lena’s sore knee, the tears she’s been willing down come crawling back up her throat.

    “Let’s get dressed for James,” Kara says, eyes blue and face red because she had forgot to put on sunscreen even though she’d had to apply seven layers to Lena’s uber-pale skin. “We like James. He’s tall? And nice, even though he’s clearly into you.”

    It breaks through the malaise, a snorty laugh bursting through her. But the cloud of misgivings comes back just as quickly as it had retreated, and she looks down at Kara and is scared, and is scared, and wants.

    “I think he’s more into you,” Lena says. Kara laughs a little. She’s kneeling in front of Lena, her hand warm in a good way even though Lena is too-hot. 

    “Well, he has good taste. Which I know because he’s into you,” Kara says. “C’mon. One thing at a time, okay?”

    “Okay,” Lena says, letting Kara pull her up by the hands when she stands up. She collapses into Kara’s arms when she opens them up and pulls Lena into them, digging her face into the sweaty space of Kara’s collar. 

    “Don’t shut me out,” Kara whispers. “We’re on the same team.”

    “Yeah,” Lena says, and at least one, leaky, amorphous tear gets out of her closed eyes when Kara presses a kiss to her temple.

    -

    James Olsen is her private physical therapist and, per Kara’s words, too tall. He’s also handsome, talks a lot about the effects of cayenne pepper and is capable of finding every single knot or bruise or tweak in Lena’s body just by watching her swing a club.

    “Okay, so there’s some tightness on your front leg,” James says, reaching out and tapping at her knee. Kara looks distinctly triumphant about this, wearing a Maserati t-shirt and shorts, even though James had just put her through a ten minute sprint with a weighted vest on and deemed her stamina sub-optimum. “Hm. Have you added a new movement to your routine?”

    “No,” Lena says. She’s been doing the same exact workout routine since she was fourteen. “I’m sure it’ll be fine if I just ice it.”

    “Well, we should definitely be doing that twice a day,” James says. He’s scribbling something on his notepad that he will inevitably hand to Kara and make her swear to make Lena do all of it. He had greeted Kara’s arrival to Lena’s team with thrilled fervor. “You have a coach. What’s he been having you change up?”

    “Nothing,” Lena says. “He wants to change my brain.”

    “He’s into mind re-mapping,” Kara adds. James nods, looking disturbingly pleased by this.

    “Stress?” James asks. Lena nearly laughs. He seems to think better of it and Lena watches him actually write alleviate stress with heavy rest and careful management. 

    “And you’ve been avoiding intercourse during tournament weeks?” James asks. Lena does laugh this time, mostly because Kara starts glaring over at him from the corner of the room. James smiles too, and for a second, he feels like a real person. “Your legs are your engine, and intercourse is an added workout the engine doesn’t need.”

    “I haven’t changed anything,” Lena says. James nods, writing something down and then looking back toward Lena with a big smile.

    “Well, you’re gonna have to go careful on the knee,” James says. “Let’s take squats out of the routine for at least this week, and do the ice and the elevation. I wanna see you in a green jacket, and I won’t let physical muscle health get in the way of it.”

Her hands feel almost alien when they settle on Lena’s elbow. And she tugs, a little, to try to get her to turn and face Kara’s beautiful face and Lena short-circuits.

    “We won’t,” Nia says, softly. “We’re working on contacting them to get it taken down, but - it’s gained some traction with the misogynistic golf crowd.”

    “There’s no strategy,” Lena says. “There’s no response. I just want to golf. Put me on mute.”

    “Lena, you know I can’t just put you on mute,” Sam says. “You just won the Masters. You have interviews all week, and every reporter on the line will want a soundbite.”

    She says this while gripping a water bottle so hard it begins to crackle under her fingers, and Sam sighs, runs a hand through her hair.   

    “Okay, I’ll do that,” Sam says. “I’ll let you know.”

    “Let Kara know, I need to focus,” Lena says. And she drops the water bottle, feels every joint in her body as she begins moving further away from the door. “I’m going to get in the shower. If there’s anything else - ”

    “Gotcha,” Lena says. “Let Ruby know that I love her.”

    -

    Her practice round kicks off at noon. There’s more than a few cameras trained on her when she and Kara hit the range just before, and a few too many follow them when they amble onto the putting green. It’s a routine; routines are important for when your mind is elsewhere, but golf is not a game that rewards people whose minds are wandering.

    Cat Grant is paired with them on the practice round, and she gives Lena a wide berth. She talks to Kara, though, Cat murmuring quietly and Kara murmuring quietly back. But Kara doesn’t talk to Lena too much, has been quiet since Lena had got in the shower. She’s led Lena to the van to the clubhouse to the course with a squared jaw, a perma-frown that’s sent a few people Kara’s clearly befriended scattering away. A Vokey rep had been about to walk up to Kara in the clubhouse and turned around so fast that he tripped.

    And Lena - Lena is stretched so tautly that there’s barely room to contemplate the basics. Her drive on one goes hilariously left, piling up onto rough grass next to a thicket of trees. And again, Kara doesn’t speak. She walks Lena to the ball, picks it up, tosses it on the fairway, and hands Lena her eight iron.

    And Lena swings again. This time, she chunks the ground hard enough that a divot the size of an iceberg comes out with the ball, and the Titleist ball falls far short of her target range, placing her in a no-man's land between wedge and iron, too close and too far from the green.

    Kara fixes the divot. Kara takes the eight iron and starts walking up to the ball.

    No one was ever supposed to know that Lena was a Luthor, actually, by blood.

    Lillian had told her vindictively once, when Lena was nineteen and moody about something - probably golf, or a girl, or who knows what, and had been claiming she was leaving the house, quitting golf, and traveling the world. It was rare to see her mother out of control, but it was easy to tell when she was drunk, because her cruelty became more textual than not.

    “If I had had it my way, you never would have got your inheritance,” Lillian had said, looking unimpressed by Lena’s whinging. “But your father loved you. Just like how he loved your mother.”

    And she had taken a sip of her wine, impassive.

    “Whatever part of you that is Lionel - you don’t deserve it. You never will.”

    “Lena?” Kara asks, and Lena is greeted from her reverie of staring at the green grass by Kara’s face. She’s tanned, eyes very blue, a bucket hat imprinted with “pray for birdies” obstructing much of her hair and shading her face. She looks concerned, and Lena realizes it’s the first time since this morning she’s looked at Kara long enough to piece together her facial expression.

    “Club?” Lena asks. Kara looks at her for a second longer, before she gives Lena a wedge. 

    “Just smash it,” Kara says, stepping away. 

    Maybe once upon a time she had known that her father had loved her. But he had been distant, and had allowed Lex and Lillian to bulldoze her, and then he had got sick and died. If someone was going to tell her that her life had been a sort of fairytale - that she had been begotten by love and inducted into luxury through terrible tragedy, she would have wished it had come from him.

    Lillian and Lex had wanted her to be a Luthor, but not too much of one. But she was, and she wasn’t, and she never wanted to be one. And her mother - Lena had never known her besides a face, besides a safe harbor. She had been gone before Lena might have thought to ask her name.

    Lena smashes the ball. It leaps up onto the green, backspin wild enough that it sits itself on the edge of the light rough. There’s a hill down to a sand trap on the other side of it, and Lena knows she’s just been lucky. 

    The round keeps going. Kara hands her the clubs, Cat plays on with rote ease, Lena runs through the holes like a horse unreined. It isn’t good. In fact, it’s so bad that Lena knows it isn’t accomplishing anything; she’d do better to call the round and regroup than keep trying. Kara doesn’t say anything, Lena doesn’t say anything.

    Lena wasn’t good enough to be a Luthor. She was so not good enough that her father had never even told her, not even hinted, that she really was one. She was excessively good at golf, even if she had a habit of ruining the ending, and she wasn’t good enough. And she was a Masters winner, a green jacket owner, and she had Kara, who was perfect, and sweet, and her brother - he had always known she had it in her. She wasn’t good enough.

    On the eighth hole, she swings her driver so hard that the whiparound pulls her quad. It’s an immediate, sharp, dense pain that locks the entire muscle in place. She has to hold the swing up for a second before she can even think about moving her weight around; when she does, Kara is right next to her, taking the club.

    “We’re done,” Kara says. Lena blinks.

    “What?” Lena asks.

    “You borked your leg,” Kara says. “I saw.”

    “I’m fine,” Lena says. She tries to start moving down the fairway, toward her wayward ball - which is definitely somewhere. But her quad is dead on the vine, and she nearly loses her footing on the downslope off the tee. Kara catches her.

    “You aren’t fine,” Kara says. “It’s Monday. Let’s just call it and rest up.”

    “I’m fine, ” Lena says. “It’s just a strain. I’ll go easy.”

    “You are a bull in a china shop right now,” Kara says, disbelieving. “I know you can’t go easy.”

    “You don’t know a fucking thing,” Lena says, and she rips her arm out of Kara’s loose grip. “You don’t know a fucking thing.”

    Kara blinks at her, glancing around. There aren’t cameras on the course for practice rounds at this event, but they’re close enough to the turn bar at the clubhouse that they might be being watched. Lena knows this. 

    “Lena, baby,” Kara starts again, quietly and gently. “You just injured yourself. Let’s not make it worse.”

    “I can take care of myself,” Lena says, shouts, starts to move again toward the carpath along the fairway. “I know my body. I know you think you do because you get to fuck me - ”

    Kara catches her again when Lena’s knee buckles.

    “ - But I know what I can do and what I can’t. I can keep playing. I have to keep playing. And you can’t stop me, and everyone else can go fuck themselves if they think they can make me do anything I don’t want to. I’m perfectly fucking fine. I’m good enough, at least.”

    “Good enough isn’t good, Lena,” Kara says, voice stern and quiet as she helps Lena down the slope. The bag clinks quietly on her shoulder. “I understand that you’re upset - ”

    “You don’t understand,” Lena says. 

    “Then help me understand,” Kara hisses, and her voice catches a little. “I am on your side, Lena. I am here for you. But I’m not your punching bag. That’s not the deal.”

    There’s a golf cart on the path, pulling up to them with a gentle whir. 

    “Hey guys,” Nia says, looking somewhat harried. “Someone said it looked like - uh. Something was happening?”

    “Lena pulled her quad,” Kara says, her hand gentle on Lena’s back as she pushes her toward the back seat of the golf cart. Nia looks wide-eyed.

    “Did you call her down here?” Lena asks, turning on Kara. And Kara is - upset. 

    Her blue eyes look more vibrant with red surrounding them, and she shakes her head.

    “No,” she says, short. Then she looks past Lena as she makes sure she gets settled on the back seat, hands a bundle of stuff off to Nia - keys, Lena’s phone, her wallet. “Nia, I need to talk to Cat. I’ll walk the bag in. Can you call James and get him sometime this week?”

    “Yeah, definitely,” Nia says.

    “Thanks,” Kara says, and she steps off the carpath and starts walking toward the distant figures of Adam and Cat Grant. Nia seems uncertain for a second, before she starts gently moving. Lena thinks about getting up off the cart and following after Kara, and yelling at her, or kissing her, but she knows her leg actually is fucked now that she’s sitting down.

    “You know, I’ve never driven a golf cart before,” Nia says, conversationally. Lena glances behind her, where Nia is absolutely white-knuckling the steering wheel.

    “I started driving them when I was seven,” Lena says, after a moment. Nia laughs.

    “Could you even see over the wheel?” Nia asks.

    “No, not really,” Lena says, and then it’s just air passing them by as they pull up to the clubhouse.

    -

    “I am not sure that alcohol helps a strained quad,” Brainy says. How he’s found her in the bar across the street from the hotel is a total mystery, but he’s sliding into the stool next to her and picking up the menu the bartender had dropped in front of her with a look of contemplation.

    “Kara told you,” Lena asks, frowns really, into her drink. It’s a shitty whiskey neat that the bartender had definitely topped with water, the dick. 

    “She was worried and felt you might not want to see her right now,” Brainy says. “Also, our friend group in college used to call me the bloodhound, as I was very good at finding a missing member of our party if need be. It is not a matter of scent, of course, just deductive reasoning.”

    “Of course,” Lena says. Her brain feels slippery, and the anger that had been filling her up on the course has segued right into misery and guilt. It doesn’t help to hear Brainy’s honest tone declaring that Kara thought she might want her - away from Lena. 

    “Nia informed me of the article,” Brainy says. “I understand that its implications are not pleasant, nor is it pleasant to have such personal information under public scrutiny. I am sorry.”

    “Thanks,” Lena says, shifting on her barstool and knocking back the watery remains of her whiskey. She sets the glass forward, eyeing the bartender with a half-glare.

    “Do you want to talk about it?” Brainy asks. Lena snorts.

    “Do you want to hear about it?” Lena asks.

    “I would not have asked if I did not care,” Brainy says. “When I knew Kara in college, she used to become very moody - her sister, Alex, said that the best thing to do was to sit with her until she started talking. I once sat with her for more than three hours at a considerably hot baseball game in complete silence. And it turned out, she said later, that she was so quiet because she had seen a child in the concourse of the stadium on her father’s shoulders. It was very interesting.”

    Lena blinks down at the dark oak bartop. Her heart feels like it twists around, and she takes a deep breath.

    “Why was it interesting?” she breathes out. 

    “We all process things differently,” Brainy says, shrugging. “When something upsets me, I am usually briefly very vocally upset. Sometimes I cry, or yell. But then I simply try to collect myself and accept the situation. Kara seemed to need time, and space, to admit her pain before she was able to confront it. And you seem to avoid it all costs, to the point of lashing out if anyone tries to assist you.”

    Lena almost spits out a harsh fuck you at that, but then realizes she’d be proving him right, and then guilt comes crashing back in as the anger leaves. She wonders where Kara is, what she’s doing.

    “I fucked up,” Lena says. 

    “Yes,” Brainy says. “Kara was very upset. She was very quiet, at least. But she will forgive you, if you apologize and mean it.”

    “I just felt - cornered,” Lena says. “I feel cornered, I mean. I feel like everyone here is watching me and they’re all waiting for me to fail. And I don’t know why Lex - I don’t know why they’re doing what they’re doing. And I think that I was - starting to feel okay. I haven’t felt okay in a long time, and it scares me. I’m scared.”

    “To feel okay, or to have it be threatened by the machinations of the Luthors?” Brainy asks.

    It makes Lena pause. She swallows, and it feels like there’s wool all down her throat.

    “Both,” Lena says. 

    “Have you told Kara this?” Brainy asks, pure curiosity like a newborn baby idiot.

    “No,” Lena says, sighing. “I just - didn’t want to scare her too.”

    “It does not really make sense to be scared alone,” Brainy says. “Plus, Kara is not one for being scared. A rattlesnake once got in her golf bag while it was laid out next to a green and she pulled it out with her bare hand.”

    “She did what?” Lena asks. “What?”

    “Would you like me to escort you back to the hotel?”

    “A rattlesnake?” Lena asks, but she drops a wad of cash anyway as Brainy begins ambling through the bar and toward the front door. “Why would she do that?”

    “Well, rattlesnakes are usually more scared of you than anything else,” Brainy says, hitting the button for the crosswalk and standing with his arms behind his back. 

    “Yeah, and then they bite you when they’re scared,” Lena says, grabbing ahold of his arm when her quad buckles just the slightest. He glances down at it and raises an eyebrow.

    “You should consider withdrawing from this competition,” Brainy says. “There are more important events in the future. The Masters is the crown jewel, but - well. I believe you have the capability to gather all the Majors in a season.”

    Lena actually laughs at that one, snorts loudly. Brainy looks confused as they cross the street.

    “I am not joking,” Brainy says. “And you know that Kara believes in you as well.”

    “Yeah, well, I don’t,” Lena says as the doorman pulls the front door open and they hobble in. Brainy smiles, hitting the button for the elevator.

    “Many people do not believe in you,” Brainy says. “From what today’s events have shown, there are some that would do all they can to stop you. But that only means that they are scared of you and what you can do.”

    Lena’s drunk enough that it sounds prophetic and bold and like the stars aligning.

    “Maybe, soon, they will be more scared of you than you are of them,” Brainy says.

    Brainy delivers her to her room with a brief nod and makes sure she’s able to operate her key card before he’s ambling away, down the hallway. 

    The room is dark, except for the television, which appears to be playing an old episode of Star Trek . When Lena gets closer to the bed, Kara is face down, one arm thrown into Lena’s side of the bed, shirt off, and snoring gently.

    It takes some effort to get out of her clothes. She nearly trips facefirst onto the bed, but she manages to get her sweatpants off and kicks them somewhere before scuttling into the sheets, fitting herself up against Kara’s body. Kara hums in her sleep, muttering something before her hand grasps Lena’s arm and holds it warmly.

    She falls asleep to the room spinning and Kara breathing.

    -

    Lena submits her withdrawal at eight in the morning next day. Doug Weemsdale, Kara’s sworn enemy, accepts it with a smile. Nia practically growls at him.

    Kara hadn’t been in the room by the time Lena woke up. Brainy had indicated she was in the gym, or somesuch. Lena had thought about calling her to come help her - her thigh was twinging on every step - but then, she had felt the fiery burn of guilt come up her throat along with all her whiskey.

    “That guy sucks,” Nia mutters, as they pile back into the elevator. Brainy hums in response, watching the hotel’s elevator screen. It’s talking about the weather. “James is flying to meet us in Charlotte on Wednesday, but he says to rest until then.”

    “Perhaps we could try some visualization exercises in the meantime,” Brainy suggests. “I find Mario Golf very useful.”

    “What? How?” Nia asks, looking deeply perplexed. 

    “The colors are very effective,” Brainy says. Nia frowns at him like he’s said something insane, which, to be fair, Lena is not convinced he hasn’t.

    “Right,” Nia says. “Well. I’ve got a jet booked for this afternoon and called the hotel in Charlotte to let them know we’d be early. We can meet up in the lobby at around one?”

    “I will be there at twelve forty-five,” Brainy says, actively setting a reminder on his watch the minute he says this. Lena almost laughs, but when she looks over to Nia to commiserate in their amusement, Nia is looking at Brainy with a soft, affectionate expression that looks wholly different than what Lena is feeling. And it makes her miss Kara.

    “What floor is the gym on?” Lena asks. Nia shrugs, but Brainy reaches his hand out and smacks for the third floor. The elevator almost immediately slides to a halt.

    “Please inform Kara that we will be meeting in the lobby at twelve thirty,” Brainy says. “I will procure whatever we have left at the clubhouse.”

    “Right,” Lena says. She takes one deep sigh, and limps out of the elevator onto, following the posted signs toward the hotel gym. When she swings the door open, she finds an entirely empty gym but for Kara Danvers and Jack Spheer. Kara is on a bench doing chest presses, and Jack is spotting her. They are listening to Boys II Men at a very high volume.

    Jack, obviously, is the first one to see her. He stops singing along to the song playing and stares at her like he’s seen a ghost. She isn’t sure why exactly; because she had never once tried to find him while they were fighting, or because she had never once set foot in a hotel gym with him. All the same, she throws a little wave his way.

    “Kara,” Jack says, looking down at Kara, who is lifting an absurd amount of weight in just her sports bra. Kara pauses at the apex of the lift, and though Lena can’t really see her face, they seem to communicate something between them that ends when Kara thwacks the bar backwards into the stand and Jack guides it home. When Kara sits up, her face is red and she’s sweating buckets.

    “Lena,” Kara says. There’s the usual bright smile and openness of Kara’s face when she sees her, and then, it disappears into a careful blankness. “You okay?”

    Lena has to swallow around the heavy feeling in her throat as she looks over Kara’s nothing face. 

    “Um, yeah,” Lena says. “No. I pulled out of the tourney just now. Quad injury.”

    Kara and Jack frown in double time.

    “You pulled out?” Jack asks, at the same time Kara says, “I would have gone with you.”

    Jack and Kara look at each other for another moment, and then Jack claps his hands together with a loud snapping sound.

    “Righto,” Jack says. “I will get packing then!”

    He basically runs out of the room. Kara watches him go with an amused smile on her face, and Lena watches Kara watch him go. And, of course, when Kara turns to look at Lena fully again, the half-smile is gone, back to the blankness of before.

    “Hey,” Lena says. Kara nods.

    “Hey.”

    “I’m sorry I didn’t let you know we were withdrawing,” Lena says. Kara shrugs, scooting up the bench until her back rests against the bar. Lena takes a step closer.

    “I don’t need to know everything,” Kara says. Lena frowns, and takes another step closer. She has to lean on about five different machines on the way over, but by the time she makes it to the bench, Kara is contemplating her shorts with a great deal of interest.

    “You’re my partner,” Lena says. Kara frowns, then glances toward the far wall, then down to her shorts again. 

    “You’re my partner, and I shouldn’t have got mad at you for trying to help me on the course,” Lena continues, and Kara sighs.

    “It’s okay,” Kara says.

    “It’s not okay,” Lena says. “Let it be not okay.”

    “Okay,” Kara says.

    Lena sits down on the bench, careful not to let her thigh touch Kara’s knee, and takes a deep breath.

    “I don’t know anything about my birth mother aside from her being Irish. Lionel Luthor was my real father, but that was never supposed to be public,” Lena says. “And I’m terrified. I’m so scared, Kara.”

    “You don’t have to be scared,” Kara says, and she scoots forward until their legs press into each other. She doesn’t ask; she just does: her hands arrive on Lena’s thigh and at her hip, pressing into the skin where she can reach it. It lets Lena take a deep breath.

    “I’m so scared of losing you,” Lena says, softly. Kara shakes her head, and then it bumps into Lena’s shoulder. “I’m so scared that one day you’ll realize what I am and leave me.”

    “I know what you are,” Kara whispers. 

    “I don’t know if I know what I am,” Lena says. “But I’ve never thought it was good. Or worth sticking around for.”

    “Lena,” Kara says.

    “I was a bitch,” Lena says. “I’m sorry. It was wrong, but I - I just need you to know. I’m so scared, and it made me lose it a little bit.”

    “I’m scared too,” Kara sighs. “I’ve lost people too, Lena.”

    “I know,” Lena says, grabbing for Kara’s hand and squeezing her fingers tightly.

    “And when you push me away, I don’t - I can’t deal with that,” Kara says. 

    Lena hums, turning her head to press a kiss to Kara’s temple. 

    “We’re on the same team,” Lena says. “I can’t promise to be perfect, but I - I know I can be better.”

    “Okay,” Kara says. “Okay, we can both be better. Together.”

    “Together,” Lena repeats.

    It’s quiet but for the sounds of Boys II Men.

    “Doug Weemsdale looked pretty pleased to take my withdrawal,” Lena says, offhand. Kara makes an honest-to-God growling noise that makes Lena laugh, for the first time in what feels like days.

    “I’m gonna deck Doug Weemsdale one day,” Kara mutters. Her arms wrap around Lena then, pulling her into the vee of Kara’s thighs, and it’s okay. For right now, it’s okay.

    NORTH CAROLINA, UNITED STATES - QUAIL HOLLOW CLUB - PALMETTO CHAMPIONSHIP

     

    “So. What happened?” James asks. Lena’s quad still twinges every time she takes a step. Kara had lovingly encased it in a veritable mountain of ACE wrap this morning after Lena had sworn about forty times a minute with the Theragun on the damaged muscle.

    “I pushed a swing. Pretty badly,” Lena says. Kara physically hoists her up onto the trainer’s table, then hauls herself up onto the padding next to her. It contorts the paper spread out over it, but Lena appreciates her warmth at her side, and then she sighs when Kara presses her elbow into Lena’s ribs.

    “Also,” Kara says. Lena huffs.

    “Also, I’ve been under a great deal of stress due to...current events,” Lena says. This morning over breakfast in she and Kara’s room, Nia had gone over all the outlets that had been trying to get ahold of them about the stupid article. 

    “Yeah, I heard,” James says. Lena feels her spine steel up, and then she feels Kara’s hand press in at the base of her spine. She sighs.

    “Yeah,” Lena says. “It, uh. Sucks.”

    James snorts then, reaching into a cabinet and producing a sealed pack of little electrodes that Lena resents totally and fully. Kara is humming Spice Girls and texting Alex about whether or not they could get their mother a puppy for Christmas.

    “It sounds like it,” James says. He doesn’t press, because he is generally a nice man, which is what Kara had told her as she helped Lena out of the car. He’s not going to go blabbing to Deuxmoi  or whatever if you act like you’re having a shitty time, Kara had said, shifting her dumb Maserati hat on her head. You are having a shitty time. It’s okay to have a shitty time sometimes.

    James is saying something to Kara that prompts her to hop off the table, leaving Lena alone. He starts ripping open the packet.

    “Well, Lena, we’ll try to get this quad going again, get you on a rehab route this week,” James says. “Can’t help with the other stuff, but if I ever find your brother in the streets, I’ll punch him for sure.”

    “Good to know,” Lena says with a smile.

    -

    “Oh fuck me,” Lena whispers. Her golf ball has just plunked itself on the rough right next to a bush. Her quad is practically singing with soreness. Her partners for the round - M’gann and a high-scoring amateur - are nestled together on the right side of the fairway, a good thirty yards ahead and perfectly placed.

    “You know the rules, baby,” Kara says, appearing from nowhere, the little demon - and grabbing the driver from Lena’s hand. “Leg?”

    “It’s fine,” Lena says, kicking her leg out. “I just pulled the swing.”

    “Oh yeah you did,” Kara says, quite cheerfully. “Further than you did yesterday.”

    “Is that meant to be helpful?” Lena mutters. Kara shrugs, hoisting the bag and pulling Lena’s yardage book from her pocket as they start walking down the fairway. The crowd moves with them as they go, and Lena tries to ignore them, adjusting her ponytail. She hasn’t done an interview in days, despite the fact that they’ve been requested every night this week for a press conference slot.

    It’s Saturday, hole one.

    Hole two does not go better - she manages to get up and onto the green with ease, and then she three putts. Hole three sits at par. Hole four, she feels the wrap on her six iron twist on the shaft in her grip and tells Kara to never hand it to her ever again with more than a few curse words thrown in. Hole five, she manages to birdie, and then it starts to drizzle.

    By the time they finish hole fifteen, Lena is well out of the cut and she’s about ready to rip her hair out. 

    “What do you feel is going wrong?” Brainy asks, following along the fence between fifteen and sixteen. Nia is trudging after him with an umbrella up. Lena has been forced into a rain pullover, which is swishing every moment she moves, and is driving her irritation even further. 

    “I don’t know,” Lena huffs. “I can’t get anything going.”

    “Untrue, you are mostly missing on accuracy,” Brainy says. 

    “I landed in three sand traps on one hole,” Lena returns.

    “And I assume you were not aiming for them?” Brainy asks. Nia laughs, Kara looks directly up into the sky and immediately gets her glasses sprinkled over with rain. Lena nearly punches him in the sternum.

    “No,” Lena grits out. “I was not aiming for the sand traps.”

    “That is good,” Brainy says with a nod. “Have you tried closing your eyes while you swing?”

    “Closing my - what the fuck?” Lena yelps, drawing glances from fans ambling past the coaches and guests paddock. Nia is clearly trying not to laugh again. Brainy looks confused.

    “But - ”

    Kara is already gently pulling Lena away from the fence, and Lena half-hears Nia go, “Nope, we are dispersing, Brains.”

    She doesn’t know where they’re going until Lena gets pushed behind a caretaker’s shed away from the cameras. Kara sets the bag down, causing a small splash from the wet ground, and then she looks Lena in the eye, her glasses covered over with rain.

    “You are at an eleven, and you need to be at a seven,” Kara says.

    “Tonight,” Lena says.

    The best golf course in the world, where Lena had, a year ago, decimated a locker room mirror and her brother’s Maserati. And recently had a mental breakdown at during a charity tournament. Kara shifts next to her, wrapping an arm around her. Lena takes a breath.

    “We’ll see,” Lena says.

    “No, thank you,” Lena says, smiling wanly. “At least, not right now.”

    “Well, when you do,” Cat says. “I believe they’ll deserve it.”

    Lena sighs, and then takes a breath.

    “Thank you, Cat,” Lena says.

    “Oh, no thanks is needed. Your brother is a horrifying narcissist, and your mother a nearsighted opportunist. Neither of them deserve to create a legacy in this game,” Cat says.

    “This trophy is fucking massive,” Kara mutters, situating the big, shiny trophy on the small armchair on their private plane.     “We - had a flight to catch,” Lena hums, as Kara’s other hand slips down to the waistband of her pants. 

    “Can you be quiet?” Kara asks. Lena tries to answer with a sensible request for rest, or a less sensible assent, but Kara’s fingers get down past her underwear and she whines instead. Kara laughs right in her ear, which is a little embarrassing. And it rocks her.

    “You really fucking did,” Kara says, her eyes on Lena as she fumbles with the knot on the tie that seems particularly stuck. “Grab the strap-on?”
