    “Lex exaggerates any and all things if he thinks they make him sound good,” Lena says.

    “You placed in the top ten on the professional leaderboard. You beat women who had been golfing since before you were alive,” Kara says.

    Lena pauses.

    “I was lucky,” she says. Kara looks at her. Lena looks back.

    “Who told you that?” Kara says. “That you were lucky that you won.”

    Lena sighs, kicking her icing ankle into a different position on the pillow it's sitting on. 

    “Lillian,” Lena says.

    “Why?”

    “Because she’s a heinous bitch?” Lena suggests. Kara deadpans her. “She said the wind was with me on a few north-facing holes. And that I had quick swings because I hadn’t ate breakfast.”

    Kara sets her notebook on the table and puts her forehead on her hand. 

    “Okay, well,” Kara says. “Your mother being the most heinous bitch I’ve ever heard of aside. Is this not success?”

    Lena stares at her.

    “If the shot in Hawaii is a failure, because the ball got caught on a wet patch and rolled down a hill, then this, a win at a major tournament when you were sixteen , has to be a success, right?” Kara asks. 

    “I - ” Lena starts. She doesn’t make it past that word before it feels like her throat starts to close in. “Fucking hell.”

    “What did your family tell you success was? What is a win to you?” Kara asks. Lena frowns, reaching for the tissues and the water bottle and pulling them close, trying to think through the sudden pain in her head. And also trying not to cry.

    “Success was winning,” Lena says. “The right way, the whole time.”

    “And if you won, the right way, the whole game?” Kara asks.

    “Then it was the next win,” Lena says. She was crying now, of course. It takes a few shaky seconds to uncap her water bottle and take a gulp. Kara hums across from her, and then she flips her laptop shut, and turns the phone off. “Wait, I can do more - ”

    “Absolutely the fuck not,” Kara says, coming around and sitting on the couch Lena’s on, her warm hands pressing into the skin of Lena’s legs.

    “I’m fine,” Lena insists. 

    “Yes, you are,” Kara says. “You are perfectly fine. You are a completely normal human being who has good days and bad days and there’s no perfect version of you. Those people - fucking suck. They suck ass, Lena.”

    “Well,” Lena starts.

    “We’ll do more later,” Kara says, ducking her head and slipping her hands further up onto Lena’s thighs, one hand reaching for her hip eventually, holding her securely. “I just - when my parents - ”

    Lena reaches out to settle one hand on Kara’s shoulder where it meets her neck, palming the skin there as she sucks in a breath.

    “You know, I felt like no one, sometimes,” Kara says, eventually, her voice clearly caught up in emotion. “Like I didn’t exist because they didn’t either. It took a really long time for me to start to feel real again.”

    Kara’s head ducks further, the crown of her head bumping into Lena’s collarbone. Lena slides her other hand around Kara’s back, feeling the ridges of her spine.

    “And you’re real too,” Kara says. “You have always been real. You were always worth watching, or celebrating. You are worth watching and celebrating. I watched you win the low amateur at Bethpage with my high school golf team because they were complete freaks - ”

    “Wait, what?” Lena asks, but Kara pays her no mind.

    “ - and my coach stopped the tape when you do that thing, where you make that adorable face, like you’re shocked that you did it, and he said to us, like, I want to see every single one of you triumph like this. He was a motivational poster type coach, you know. And I remember looking at your face and feeling proud of you even though I didn’t know you at all and you were just a random girl good enough to beat professional golfers twice your age, and he was proud, and even Alex was proud, I remember, because she said I can’t believe she’s surprised she won , in her annoyed voice. And you were surprised. Because no one had ever let you believe you would win, even when it was obvious you could and would. And they didn’t let you believe it after, either. And they suck so much that I could tear their heads off every time I see them.”

    “Okay,” Lena says, feeling tears on her shoulder and tears on her face. “Okay.”

    “You were real, and I was real, and we’re real,” Kara says, and Lena feels Kara’s mouth press open-mouthed against her neck, and it’s not really very sexy because they’re both crying, but - there’s something in it she understands. 

    There’s always something in Kara she understands.

    -

    “You good to go?” Kara asks. It’s Sunday at Pebble Beach, and Lena is in second place, and she and Eve Tessmacher have the late start as the two leaders. 

    Lena is fine.

    The locker room has been refurbished since last year, when she destroyed a few small things. She won a tournament here just a few months ago, with Kara. Her body feels good. Kara and Brainy have gone over her yardage book and the course with her, and Nia had posted an Instagram photo of Lena and Kara huddled over a course map that had apparently made waves on the lesbian Internet, whatever and wherever that was.

    And Lena is fine.

    “Lena?” Kara asks. When Lena turns around, Kara is standing there with her hands shoved in her black pants pockets, her now-favorite PROPERTY OF LENA LUTHOR shoes on - Kara had tried explaining to her that they were Air Maxes - and a long-sleeved black polo with white detailing on the collar and sleeves. Lena, of course, is wearing black as well - a weird little gingham polo that Nike had sent along, skort, and shoes. Kara had tried to explain they were also Air Maxes, even though their shoes didn’t match at all.

    Kara is looking at her curiously, her hands steadily in her pockets. Lena sighs.

    “What do you need?” Kara asks. “Maybe another orgasm or something, who can say…”

    “Was three not enough this morning?” Lena huffs, drawn from her thoughts just as suddenly as she had slipped into them, standing at her locker and staring at the wood grain. Kara frowns, reaching for her hat and Lena’s visor where they’re set on the bag.

    “Only time will tell,” Kara says, reaching up to flip up Lena’s braid and slide the visor down into place. Lena watches as she flips her own hat on. They match, of course - Winn really needed to rein in on the couples theming.

    “I’m somewhat worried that you think orgasms are the solution to boosting my performance,” Lena says, gesturing at the bag. Kara slings it onto her shoulder easily, letting Lena walk ahead of her as they wind their way through the byzantine locker room.

    “No, they’re just fun and distracting,” Kara says. “When I’m not your caddie, maybe you’ll take up crossword puzzles or something. I have supreme confidence in your performances, orgasms or no.”

    Lena pushes the door open, and frowns when a few camera flashes go off as she turns into the hallway. Nia appears from somewhere to shepherd them through the clubhouse. There are people clapping, and plenty more staring. At some point, a kid with a lanyard slung around his neck and dressed in shorts and a button down reaches his hand up for a high-five that Lena gives without thinking, and he squeals in happiness when he turns around to what must be his father. 

    “Miss Luthor, Miss Danvers,” is how they’re greeted by the tour official set to drive them to the first teebox. Kara straps the bag in, helps Lena into the backseat, and Nia climbs into the front seat. There’s more people, masses of them, milling around on the sides of the carpath where they’ve been cordoned away. It’s Sunday. It’s the US Open. Lena is in second place.

    “Maybe a fourth would have been good,” Lena allows, tilting her head closer to Kara’s as a few girls in a lot of rainbow-decorated clothing wave frantically at them. Kara laughs, looking up from her phone and grinning widely.

    “I can get you a crossword puzzle as a poor, poor replacement?” Kara asks, flipping to her home page and looking around for the app she has on her phone, most likely. 

    “Shut up,” Lena says, shoving Kara in the hip. Nia turns a little in the seat ahead of them all of a sudden, frantically, looking wild-eyed even behind the large sunglasses she’s wearing. 

    “Sam just texted me saying that Lex is walking the course with Eve,” Nia says. “She says I just saw his fucking bald-ass head on television, the robot creep. ”

    “Not sure we needed to repeat that verbatim,” Kara says, glancing at their driver and off to the side of the cartpath as they take a sharp turn. Lena’s dealing with the sudden onset of nausea in her stomach, but she looks at her driver and finds him smiling the slightest bit. 

    “I thought it had a certain ring,” Nia says, typing something on her phone. “She just wanted you to know, so you had time to prepare.”

    Of course, they slide to a stop at the back of the first tee right then, where there are hundreds of people gathered in rows and rows backward to watch her and Eve tee off. For a moment, all of them are silent.

    “Well, you’ve had some time,” Nia says. She hops out of her seat as Brainy appears out of the wild and crowds Kara as she clambers out of the cart as well. On the opposite side of whatever the two of them are talking over, and as Nia walks towards a group of other managers and media members milling around on the VIP side of the cordon, the driver of the golf cart turns and looks at her.

    “Miss Luthor,” he says. She really couldn’t determine one thing about his face, but it’s more than likely she’s met him before in some capacity. He’s got on big sunglasses, a WPGA hat, a WPGA polo - and he smiles up at her from his seat. “Good luck. Your brother is definitely a robot creep.”

    It makes her laugh, loudly, drawing Kara’s attention across the way. 

    “Thanks, Mr…”

    “Oh, just call me George,” he says, extending his hand out to her. She shakes it, still smiling.

    “Thanks, George. Call me Lena next time we see each other,” she says.

    “Can do,” he says. She lets him go then, meeting Kara at the back of the cart where she’s checking the bag for the last time, unzipping every possible pocket and digging through to make sure they have everything. 

    “He’s nice,” Lena says. Kara smiles, pulling out a pack of Lena’s gloves and handing one over to her. She slips it into her back pocket; Kara pulls out half of their rain jackets before jamming them back in.

    “People are nice sometimes,” Kara says. She unearths a packet of peanut butter crackers that she slips into her own back pocket.

    “I suppose,” Lena says. Kara turns to look at her fully, her sunglasses upside down on the top of her hat in the shade of the tree that they’re under. Her eyes are a gleaming blue, her smile bold. Lena is so in love with her that she thinks about just screaming. Kara is looking at her like she feels just the same.

    “I love you,” Kara says, leaning forward to press one kiss to Lena’s cheek. “You ready?”

    “You too,” Lena says. “And yeah. I think so.”

    And they walk down the small hill to the tee box, where Eve Tessmacher is waiting with her caddie - John Corben, by the looks of it, that absolute numbskull - and Lex, behind her at the cordon. His head is gleaming particularly brightly today in the sun and ocean breeze. He’s wearing sunglasses, a Luthor Golf polo on his shoulders, and he looks impatient, judging by the way he’s chewing his gum. 

    And Lena understands quite suddenly that she’s going to win the US Open at Pebble Beach.

    -

    Lena and Eve are the last to tee off for the whole tournament, and plenty of people have clearly camped out just to see the last pairing. The pairing ahead of them are moving about two holes ahead. The game starts with Lena swinging first, and her ball lands perfectly, laid up under the green by about fifty yards. Kara takes the driver with glee clear on her face.

    Eve’s ball lands similarly, though a little off the right, and they start the long walk.

    Corben is happy to glare at her from a distance, and Eve really pays her no mind. Kara doesn’t really say anything either, but it’s comfortable - astoundingly so. Kara hands her a wedge when they get to the ball, and they watch Eve take her swing - the ball doesn’t land exactly right on the green, rolling away from the flag. And then Lena swings, and her ball thuds the ground and sticks, a few feet away from the hole position. There’s a few gasps from the crowd at the sight of the ball coming to a dead stop with no roll.

    When Lena hands her wedge over, Kara is grinning like a cat with a canary, her sunglasses shiny in the sunlight.

    “What?” Lena asks. Kara jams the wedge into its assigned spot and falls into place beside Lena as they walk up the edge of the fairway, a few feet away from the fans following along.

    “Oh, I think you know,” Kara says, her hand dropping on Lena’s back as they ascend the short hill up onto the green.

    “I’m not sure I do,” Lena says. 

    “Oh no, you really do then,” Kara says, laughing and stopping short of their ball as Lena kneels down to mark it and pluck it off the green. When she stands, Lena glances behind her, at Eve and Corben, who’ve just arrived at their ball. Corben is letting out a stream of words as he kneels behind the ball and Eve is nodding along at rapid speed.

    “This hole placement is inventive,” Lena says, turning back to face the flag and handing over her ball for Kara to clean. Kara snorts, her spare hand again pressing into the small of Lena’s back as they walk to the edge of the green.

    “Demonic, you mean,” Kara says, kneeling down next to the bag to wipe the ball of whatever specks of pristine poa annua grass have attached themselves there. Lena crosses her arms, adjusting the way her polo has stuck against her stomach. “They moved the little bump we kept finding the last two rounds too.”

    It takes Lena a few seconds of looking - Kara is better at reading greens than her, and Lena’s very good at it on her own - but she sees the small ripple in the green’s surface, where they’ve clearly moved some dirt around to create a unique roll. Kara stands up and hands her ball back over, now ready for what comes next.

    “Interesting,” Lena mutters. The WPGA person in charge of maintaining absolute silence raises up the white paddle board and she falls quiet as Eve steps up to her ball. Next to her, Kara shifts minutely, her bib brushing against Lena’s arm like a feather. Eve’s putt isn’t bad, but it’s not heavy enough, and it hits the ripple and curves rightward, closer than it was before but still not right.

    Kara leans down, grabs Lena’s putter, and follows her to their marker.

    -

    After ten, Lena is in the lead by two, thanks to Eve hitting the ball into the water. 

    At eleven, she’s clearly trying to draw herself together, shaking out her hands and breathing deeply before she steps up to tee. Lena and Kara are in the back of the tee box, hovering near Brainy and Nia, and Lena can see Lex on the opposite side. If anything, he looks more annoyed - his cheeks are red, he’s pulled on a hat, he’s tapping his foot on the ground. When Eve swings, it hooks a wild left. It’s not horrible, but considering her position, it’s not great either. And Lena watches the ball land on the edge of the fairway, a little in the rough, and looks back to Lex.

    He’s gone, already pushing through the crowd and heading down the carpath. And Lena looks to Eve, whose backswing is stalled as she eyes her ball. She brings her driver down on the turf of the teebox with a heavy thump, the shaft of the club shuddering from the impact. Lena watches as she turns slightly, and looks to where Lex had been.

    Lena feels a horrible burst of sympathy as the woman in front of her turns back to her club, surrounded by hundreds of people who either desperately want her to fail or desperately want her to win, and not the people she should be able to trust.

    Corben reaches for the driver, and Eve looks around with a sigh. When their eyes connect, Lena gives her a small nod of - empathy, or awareness, or something - and draws a deep breath. She watches as Eve does the same and nods back.

    And Lena’s driver appears in her hand.

    -

    On the green at eighteen, Lena eyes the hole position, tucked against the corner created by the grandstands. Eve has played a little better now with no cliffs in sight, but so has Lena - she’s birdied or parred every hole since ten, and she’s working on another par right now. One more par putt, and it’s all hers.

    “Is now a bad time to ask what kind of lingerie you want to wear for your cover?” Kara asks, whispering in Lena’s ear as Corben and Eve stand back from their ball and talk to each other.

    “Yes,” Lena hisses back. Honestly. The gall.

    “Fair enough,” Kara says, looking behind them and waving into the grandstands - apparently Alex, Kelly, and Eliza have a spot up there. “What do you want for dinner?”

    “Are you kidding me?” Lena asks. Kara shrugs, pouting.

    “I’m hungry,” Kara says. Lena wants to hit her, really, but instead she laughs, and Kara laughs too, leaning over to press a kiss to the side of her very sweaty head. “And I love you very much.”

    The guy with the white paddle board appears again, calling for full silence as Eve steps up on the ball and takes her shot. It sinks. 

    Lena doesn’t wait. Kara hands her the putter, and doesn’t follow her onto the green. She picks up their ball marker, tucks it in her pocket, and pulls out the ball. The line on the Pro V1 settles with the line she and Kara had picked out over the week. She swings once for practice, twice to make sure, and then steps up.

    Lena doesn’t really hear the ball land in the cup. Kara comes rushing up to her from behind, picking her straight up in the air, and the grandstands are cheering, and the people with the rainbow all over them are visibly crying in the stands - and Lena gets set down just in time to shake Eve’s hand.

    “Congratulations,” Eve says, her hat off, and Lena says thank you , her putter still in her other hand. 

    -

    “Miss Luthor, this is your third win at a major this year,” a reporter says over the clamor. Lena is freshly showered, dressed in a strange sweatshirt Nike had sent with a topographic pattern stitched into it, and somewhat dazed. Between holding the US Open trophy in her own two hands and Kara taking her shirt off within three seconds of being in the locker room, she’s on more than a small high. “What do you think your chances are at the Open and completing the Grand Slam?” 

    Lena can’t help but laugh, glancing at Kara on the side of the press conference room with Nia at her side, her arms wrapped around the US Open trophy and her hat backwards. 

    “I really have no idea,” Lena says. “I think I’m just going to enjoy this one first.”

    “Obviously, Lena, it’s been about a year since you started your sabbatical from golf, and since you’ve come back for this season, you’ve been on a tear. What’d you do over your time away that helped your game?”

    “Well, I took some time to not think about the game,” Lena shrugs. “I think they call it work-life balance.”

    It prompts a roll of laughter through the room, and another reporter gets picked from the pack.

    “You played your round today with Eve Tessmacher, who’s being coached by your brother and stabled under the Luthor Golf name - do you think that that gave you any competitive advantage?”

    “Uh, no,” Lena says. “The only person I had to beat or prove anything to today was myself. Eve was just there. No offense to Eve, who played a great tournament for sure and has been playing fantastic all season.”

    “As we all know, your time away my colleague mentioned was prompted by some personal problems - what would you say to yourself a year ago, if you could go back?”

    “Put down the seven iron and calm down,” Lena says, prompting another roll of laughter. “It isn’t worth it.”

    -

    By the time Lena makes it to the scheduled US Open Championship dinner, she’s done three shots with Kara, Alex, Kelly, and Nia; has drank two glasses of champagne in the limo that takes them from the hotel back to the clubhouse; has sipped at a scotch in her hotel room while she and Kara got dressed; and has also been thoroughly seduced (again) by Kara. This is all just to say that she is pretty tipsy when she steps into the ballroom and a round of cheers rises through the room.

    Dinner is surf and turf, and Kara devours it next to her. At some point, Cat Grant comes over to say congratulations personally, wrapping her into a brief hug with some air kisses for good measure - Kara basically cries - before moving over to say hello to Eliza. After dinner, Kara draws her onto the dance floor, and Brainy cuts in, and then Kelly and Lena is - happy.

    And before the last few months, she wouldn’t have been surprised to see Lex appear from nowhere to deflate that balloon. But tonight, as she’s settled in a corner talking with Kara, Leslie Willis, and Alex, she is surprised. 

    “Hello, sis,” Lex says, announcing himself with the subtlety of an elephant. His scotch glass is mighty full. Lena feels herself tense, and feels Kara stand up so straight suddenly that she nearly knocks Lena over. “Congrats.”

    Leslie looks over at her with her eyebrows raised, clearly unimpressed. Alex seems as though she’s reacting to Kara’s posture, concern on her expression. And when Lena glances at Kara, she understands why: she’s giving full, undiluted anger. 

    “Thanks,” Lena says, trying to figure out how she can get Kara away and hopefully herself at the same time. Across the room, she sees Nia - when their eyes connect and she spots who’s cornered them, she abandons her conversation and starts making her way over.

    “Three majors in one season,” Lex says. “Who could have imagined?”

    “I guess not her allegedly genius-level former coach?” Leslie says. Lex pays her no regard.

    “Your mother would be so proud, finding love. Hopefully it won’t end the same way,” he continues. Lena feels a burst of anger in her chest at that one, but - she’s angry, at least, not put down.

    “What the fuck is that supposed to mean?” Alex asks. Lex glances at her, and then looks to Kara. 

    And then he looks back to Lena, and it’s apparent that he’s come up with his final blow. Lena tries not to wince, but she tries to ready herself for whatever horrible thing he’s about to say in front of her colleagues and family -

    “Everyone will want to know if you can win the Open,” Lex says. “But I know you can’t. You’ll throw away your chance just like - ”

    This is when Kara lunges forward and sucker punches him. 

    “Fuck you, you enormous dickwad,” Kara shouts, hovering over him where he’s fallen on the ground, clutching at his nose, which is definitely bleeding. “You are the worst excuse for a brother and human man I have ever met in my life!”

    “Holy shit, Kara,” Alex says, grabbing her sister by the collar of her suit and yanking her away. Down on the ground, Lex is groaning and saying something that Lena really cannot be bothered to hear. Leslie is making a face that belies enormous amusement, one of her hands pressing lightly at Lena’s bicep as though to push her away a little from the mess in front of her.

    “Uh, hey guys!” Nia shouts, arriving at their bubble while Alex tries to corral her sister and some WPGA tools arrive to get Lex off the ground. “Let’s get out of here, huh?”

    -

    They don’t make it very far. 

    “Miss Luthor, Miss Danvers…everyone else,” Doug Weemsdale says, looking quite nervous. They’ve been sat in a dining room in the clubhouse for at least forty minutes now, thanks to a few security guards who had prevented them from leaving. Lena really isn’t shocked; it feels like the whole thing was a trap somehow. 

    Kara has ice for her hand by now, and endured what looked like a very thorough reprimanding from Eliza, Alex, and Kelly in the corner of the room when they got shunted into the space. Nia has been frantically texting on her phone the entire time, and had even called Sam at some point. Lena has been sitting in silence next to Brainy for the whole of their time detained. They haven’t said a word to each other, not even when Kara was released from her haranguing to sit down next to Lena. 

    Doug Weemsdale clears his throat, and then claps his hands together, like an idiot. 

    “After speaking to Mr. Luthor, he does not wish to press charges, nor does he feel the need for an arrest,” Doug says. 

    “Oh, thank fuck,” Alex mutters on the other side of the room. But that isn’t the end of it, judging by the redness of Doug’s face as he twists his fingers and glances at Kara every few seconds.

    “But the WPGA feels that it would be wrong to not address this violent conduct, even if Mr. Luthor doesn’t feel that it is serious,” Doug says. “So we have agreed that Miss Danvers should be banned from the next WPGA tour event that you, Miss Luthor, are registered for, which would be the Canadian Open in Hamilton, Ontario.”

    “Oh, okay, shelter an abuser some more, Doug,” Kara says. Lena sighs, tilting her head into her hand. Doug looks utterly shocked by this, his face bright red. Everyone in the room watches as he literally stomps his foot, his WPGA lanyard swinging back and forth.

    “I’m sorry, I misspoke,” Doug squeaks out. “WPGA officials have banned you for your next two registered events, the Canadian Open in Hamilton, Ontario and the North Star Open in Minneapolis, Minnesota.”

    “Wonderful,” Kara says, shooting up out of her chair so quickly that Doug jerks backwards, colliding with the door of the dining room. “I think - ”

    “Kara,” comes Eliza’s voice. Kara stops short, and Lena watches her injured hand press hard into the tabletop, the ice cracking beneath her knuckles.

    “Okay,” Kara says. “Okay.”

    Lena sighs, and reaches out to wrap her hand around Kara’s forearm. The muscle there relaxes just the slightest.

    -

    That night, Lena wakes up at three in the morning to find Kara sitting on the armchair, and definitively not in bed with her. When Lena sits up, she can see the vague outline of Kara tilt her head.

    “Go back to sleep,” she says. Lena immediately reaches over to turn on the light over her nightstand, prompting an aggravated, “Okay, so you’re awake.”

    “What are you doing?” Lena asks. Kara, in the light, is so clearly upset that Lena very nearly climbs out of bed to begin administering every form of empathy she can muster. Kara’s got her arms crossed, and her eyes are red, and she’s hunched over enough that it seems like she’s trying to fold in on herself.

    “I’m sitting,” Kara huffs, though her voice cracks. And then she starts crying.

    “Kara,” Lena says, now clambering out of bed quickly - but Kara flinches away when she gets close, a small little sound escaping. Lena stops short, a foot away from Kara, unsure what to do. Touch has never exactly been unwelcomed by Kara before. “Darling?”

    “I’m so sorry,” Kara says. “I’m so sorry, Lena, I didn’t mean to ruin everything tonight, I just - I just got so angry, and I haven’t been that angry in so long, and I just snapped.”

    “It’s okay,” Lena says. 

    “And now I can’t - be with you for the next two tourneys, and I feel - I feel like such a jerk,” Kara says. “I feel so stupid, and useless, that I can’t - help you, or control myself. And my fucking hand hurts!”

    Kara very helpfully smacks her injured hand down on the arm of the chair for this. Lena ignores her worries over Kara flinching away to grab for it before she does it again. 

    “Kara, it’s okay,” Lena insists, tracing the bruised knuckles on Kara’s hand and leaning down a little to try to catch Kara’s eyeline.

    “It’s not okay! I assaulted a guy!”

    “Yes, that’s bad,” Lena says. “And I would prefer you not do that again, especially to other people who aren’t my brother. But I’m not upset with you, darling.”

    Kara finally looks up at her, her blue eyes sparkling and bright with the tears and the redness around them. Lena sighs, pushing a little at Kara’s shoulder until she sits back, and climbs into Kara’s lap. It barely takes half a second before Kara wraps her arm around her hip and drops her face on Lena’s arm. 

    “You should be upset,” Kara says. “I should have done better.”

    “You could have,” Lena corrects, plucking at Kara’s fingers and turning to press a kiss into her head.

    “I let you down,” Kara says.

    “Kara,” Lena huffs, and then takes in a breath. Kara probably doesn’t need her snapping. “It’s one thing if you feel like you let yourself down, but I’m not upset with you for being human. And I don’t blame you - Lex was trying to bait us. He didn’t care which one of us he got. That’s just…what he does. I mean, you’ve seen photos of the Maserati post-seven iron.”

    Kara makes a noise that sounds like a laugh or a cry, tucked into Lena’s shoulder, her hands warm on Lena’s hip and in Lena’s hand. She adjusts, digging her face in tighter.

    “This reaction is not what I’ve been expecting,” Kara says, her voice breaking a little. Lena frowns, sliding her hand up Kara’s bare arm and gripping gently at the muscles in her forearm. 

    “That’s…fair,” Lena concedes. “I don’t know, I think it’s just - today was strange. I felt far away from it. Him. The Luthors, in general. I wasn’t worried. I was surprised to see him show up at the dinner at all.”

    “Me too,” Kara says. “I think that’s part of why I punched him. He did it just to fuck with you.”

    “You stood up for me,” Lena says, eventually.

    “Well, yeah,” Kara says. “I love you.”

    Lena laughs a little, and feels tears cloud her throat for the first time in days - and if that’s not proof enough that she’s felt far away from Lex, she’s not sure what would be. 

    “I love you too,” Lena says. “Now, come back to bed.”

    -

    HAMILTON, CANADA - HAMILTON GOLF AND COUNTRY CLUB - CANADIAN OPEN

    It’s unreasonably hot in Canada when they arrive. There are reporters in the hotel lobby who seem to be on the lookout for unsuspecting golfers arriving; Lena is in deep incognito, and Kara isn’t with her - so, no one seems to throw a glance her way even though they’re surely looking for her. 

    “Okay, I have all the stuff,” Nia says, carrying a tote bag of folders, lanyards, and a handful of key cards. “Our first practice time tomorrow is at eleven, press conference tonight at five, we have the clubhouse rehab room scheduled for two. Three rooms, all on the twelfth floor. Wait. Three rooms?”

    Lena watches Nia clearly complete math in her head. On either side of Lena, Jack and Brainy stand with their arms crossed, both of them unnaturally quiet as they have been for hours.

    “Okay. Three rooms is not correct,” Nia says, turning to look back at the hotel desk. Jack laughs just the slightest. “Um. I will fix that.”

    “It’s alright, Nia,” Lena says, glancing toward Brainy, who looks mysteriously upset. “We’re all adults.”

    “Yes,” Brainy says, and then making a noise that sounds like an elephant dying. “Nia, we could share a room. We wouldn’t want to put Jack and Lena in an awkward position, as they have previously been engaged in a relationship.”

    All three of the other members of the conversation stare at Brainy. Lena nearly starts laugh-crying. God, Kara would love this. That thought, along with the heavy pang of missing Kara, distracts her long enough that she doesn’t respond.

    “Oh. Well, yeah, if you’re okay with that,” Nia says, sounding exceedingly nervous. “We do have a double room. Yeah! Yes, okay, here you two go. I have to go talk to the PR rep for the press conference.”

    “I will speak to the Titleist representative who I can see across the way,” Brainy says, taking the keycard from Nia and immediately rushing away. Nia disappears just as promptly, leaving Jack and Lena standing alone in the lobby with their keycards, suitcases, backpacks, purse, and golf bag.

    “There were so many other options,” Jack says. Lena laughs, glancing up at him. He’s sweating through his polo it’s so hot. “So many other paths we could have taken to solve that problem. Adorable. How long has that been going on?”

    “I think that might have been Brainy finally making a move,” Lena says, starting to roll toward the elevator. Jack follows after her.

    “And what a move it was. There’s only one hotel room. Perfect romcom material,” Jack says. “I’ll add that to my repertoire.”

    Lena laughs again.

    “You aren’t gonna believe this,” Kara says, sounding very harried. Lena is in her hotel room, tired after a long practice round on the course. It’s still hot as hell itself, and Jack nearly passed out from heat stroke at some point, in between handing off clubs and frantically consulting his yardage book. “But there is a snake in the pool.”

True to form, Doug Weemsdale and Lex had colluded to prevent the media from finding out about the reasons why Kara was missing - Lex most likely because he didn’t want anyone knowing that he had been well and truly decked by his sister’s girlfriend. She hadn’t seen him around Eve’s camp in the past few days, and she had spent more than a few minutes passing the time by thinking about his probably black eyes. The both of them bruised like peaches - apparently it had been a paternal trait.

    “Oh my God, Kara, call animal control,” Lena says. “I’m pretty sure you aren’t supposed to just rescue possibly deadly animals.”

    “You are a moron,” Lena mutters. The sunset on Hamilton, Ontario was somewhat beautiful, for being Hamilton, Ontario - Lena’s single queen bed and large suite was quite empty, though.

    “We are heading to the treeline, please hold,” Kara says. “Okay, Mr. Snake - you’re outta here. Yep, just - oh jeez, it’s gone. I have no idea where it went. Do snakes have long memories? Will it remember me as a hero or a villain?”

    “Fine,” Lena says. At Kara’s apprehending hum, she flops back onto the bed and stares at the popcorn ceiling. “Things are okay. Maybe good? The course is tough, but working with Jack is surprisingly good. Much better than it’s ever been.”

    “This is why they say to not fuck your coworkers,” Kara says. Lena sighs.

    “And what do you think we’re doing?” Lena asks.

    “I am your soulmate, there are exceptions for that,” Kara says. “Read the rulebook.”

    “Right,” Lena says, feeling warmth slip through her bones. “Well. We’re getting along well. He’s a little reliant on my yardage book and I have to remind him of my clubs sometimes, but overall, it feels good. By the way, did Brainy tell you about him and Nia sharing a room?”

    “God knows where the nearest aquarium is,” Lena says. “How’s the article coming?”

    “Great,” Kara says. “It’s like, way too long. But I got some interviews done and I’m reading this book for research, about like - trauma. And sports. I don’t know, but it was recommended when I talked to the body language expert.”

    “The body language expert,” Lena repeats.

    “Oh, yeah,” Kara says. “Turns out there’s a lot going on there, honey.”

    “Great,” Lena deadpans. “Can’t wait to read it.”

    “Like I keep telling you, you won’t need to read it with the lingerie pictures,” Kara says. “When do you tee off tomorrow for the first round?”

    “Nine,” Lena says. 

    “Awesome, I’ll be watching,” Kara says. “I’m gonna order a pizza and wings. Like my own personal Super Bowl party.”

    “For the Canadian Open opening round?” Lena asks. 

    “Babe. I love looking at you in sleeveless polos. It’s one of my passions,” Kara says. “And - well, I love you.”

    “I love you, too,” Lena says, softly, closing her eyes. For just a second, it feels like Kara could be right there with her.

    The first round does not go well. Lena is out of sorts, for some reason, from the first tee. She isn’t quite sure what the problem is - she’s not overly irritated by anything, or upset, and her warmup had felt good. All the joints in her body are functioning without pain.

    “Can you hand me a fresh glove?” Lena asks, as they walk from ten to eleven. She’s surely well below the cut - operating at about five over par. But she feels a relative spring in her step. She imagines Kara at home, watching the NBC feed and probably inhaling wings - and she wants to keep trying. 

    Jack digs into the bag, unearths a bag of peanuts as well as a glove in its sleeve. He hands it over without a word. It’s been like this the whole round - it’s the only thing that hadn’t felt solid. And she knows that it’s probably her fault, considering she had conditioned Jack to not speak to her on the course, so there were no distractions. 

    “How do you think we should attack this next one?” Lena asks. Jack visibly startles mid-step, her bag rattling on his shoulder as he jerks. The sunglasses he’s wearing obscure his expression, but when he looks over at her, she can imagine the wariness. “The hole position is a little unconventional.”

    “Yeah,” Jack says, his voice ragged. He coughs. “Well. Uh, maybe your nine? And aim further right?”

    “Because I’m slicing,” Lena says. 

    “Because you’re slicing,” Jack agrees, slowly. “So badly.”

    “Rude,” Lena says, watching as Jack’s face transforms into a grin, something fresh blooming between them. It makes her smile back.

    “ So, so badly, Lena,” Jack says. “Is that elbow thing happening again?”

    “Nope,” Lena says, unwrapping her new glove and sliding off the old one. “Nothing is wrong.”

    “Well, Kara isn’t here,” Jack says idly, taking her old glove and jamming it back in with the peanuts that Kara had haphazardly stashed in every pocket of the bag. Lena considers that, coming to a stop behind the teebox. Ahead of her, Cat Grant is setting up at the tee and talking with her son about something - he’s miming a flight motion with his hands and she’s watching him with clear amusement, in Cat’s standards. Lena’s only figured out what that looked like by seeing her talk to Kara.

    “Sure,” Lena says. “But I feel okay. I think I just don’t have it today.”

    Jack looks at her long enough that Lena turns back to look at him, raising an eyebrow. He laughs.

    “Too bad,” Jack says. “I was really looking forward to working in this sauna of an ecosystem for the next three days.”

    Lena snorts, drawing a glare from a nearby tour official. She rolls her eyes but quiets down as Adam steps away. Jack sets the bag down with a rattle. The two of them watch Cat swing a few times, and then the flight of her ball - a towering shot that takes a good few seconds in the air before it lands politely on the edge of the green. She’s probably on for birdie.

    “Do you think Minnesota is any nicer?” Lena asks. Jack laughs loudly again, and the tour official glares harder. Lena finds, for once, that she doesn’t care - about the tour official, about Cat cranking out a perfect hit, about Kara not being here, or about how she’s surely missing the cut on the very first round of play.

    “Are you calling for phone sex?” Kara asks, before Lena can even speak. She’s in the hotel lobby, waiting for the rest of the group to get down so they can make their flight - they’d decided as a group to head to Toronto until Sunday, and then fly to Minnesota for the next tournament. Brainy had repeatedly mentioned the aquarium. 

    “I am in public,” Lena says. 

    “Ah, one of your secret kinks,” Kara says. “I knew it! I knew voyeurism had to be in there somewhere.”

    “I think you are a very confused individual,” Lena says. “How’d I look on TV?”

    “Extremely sexy,” Kara says. “Have you ever thought about having sex on a golf course?”

He catches her purse before it slides right off her. “I have not. You and I both know the amount of chemicals they put on that grass.”

    “Of course,” Lena says, turning away the slightest as Nia and Brainy come closer. “I miss you.”

    “It’s been six days, Kara,” Lena deadpans, rolling her eyes. 

    “Six days is a long time! You literally just asked me to lay around in your hotel room for a week. I think you were aware of the implications,” Kara says, sounding somewhat smug. Lena rolls her eyes again.

    “Goodbye,” Lena says. 

    “Bye, baby,” Kara says. “Love you. And call me when you get to your hotel in Toronto?”

    The implications are clear enough. Lena rolls her eyes again, and her eyes hurt now - and she resents the heavy pull in her stomach that indicates that she’s interested in having phone sex with her girlfriend. 

    “I love you too,” Lena says. “And I’m never calling you ever again.”

    She hangs up before Kara can finish her retort of I’ll believe it when I see it . Brainy and Nia are there when she turns back around, and Brainy is - showing Nia the website for the aquarium. Apparently there is a shark tunnel. Nia is nodding along with visible interest, and even interjects with a gasp of I love eels! that has Brainy looking like he’s about to propose. 

    “I am so sorry I am late,” comes Jack’s harried voice, arriving into their circle. “I just ran into this woman who does statistics for the association, and we started talking about swing speed and carbon fiber shafts, and then she gave me her business card - do you think this is a date business card?”

    He shoves the thing into the circle. It reads Lana Lang, WPGA Statistician, and there’s three phone numbers listed - one is circled. There’s also a note saying see you in MN, and a barely legible LL .

    “Yes,” she and Nia say at the same time. Brainy looks somewhat more contemplative.

    “Do you think it’s odd her initials are the same as Lena’s?” Brainy asks. Nia stares at him. Jack looks down at the business card as though he hadn’t realized. Lena wants to laugh.

    “Brainy. Stop saying weird things,” Nia says. Brainy frowns.

    “Well, it’s only Lena’s initials for now ,” Jack says with a shrug. Lena nearly chokes on her own spit. “Whatever. How long should I wait until I text her?”

    “At least three hours,” Nia says. “And while we’re in the car I will Google date spots in Minnesota. For all of us.”

    “For all of us?” Brainy asks. Lena is still trying to wrangle in her feelings on Jack implying that her initials may at some point change - a thing that happens to many women but was assuredly not something she had ever considered until this very moment - that she is now blindsided by this comment as well. Jack blinks in surprise.

    “Yes, you and me, Jack and Lana Lang, and Lena and Kara. Kara’s coming, right?” Nia asks. Lena nods, while she and Jack look on as Brainy turns a startling color of pink. “I was going to look if they had an aquarium for us, but Jack and Lana should definitely not have a first date there, and I’m pretty sure Kara mentioned she doesn’t like aquariums.”

    “She finds the fish threatening,” Brainy notes, sounding shellshocked.

    “Well, she doesn’t like the vastness of the ocean, more specifically,” Lena says, feeling the need to make Kara sound like not a wimp. “Which is a healthy fear.”

    “The fish are in an acrylic box, Lena, not the actual ocean,” Jack says. Lena reaches over and punches him in the arm.

    “Well, I’m afraid of trampolines,” Nia says. “We all have our things.”

    “We…are going to go on a date?” Brainy asks. Nia looks at him like he’s grown another head.

    “This is so much more fun than watching you and Lex argue over whether nine fifteen was a better start time than nine ten,” Jack says, offhand. Lena hits him again.

    “Yes, Brainy, I like you and want to date you. I really thought we had already discussed this when you asked me to go to the Toronto aquarium,” Nia says. 

    “Oh,” Brainy says. “Yes. I believe Minnesota has a commercial aquarium that is not affiliated with a zoological organization, so I would hesitate to support them.”

    “Maybe just the zoo, then,” Nia says. 

    “Kara would love to go to the zoo,” Lena interjects. Brainy nods in agreement.

    “Right. Okay. So everyone is on the same page here?” Nia asks. 

    “I think so,” Jack says. “You and Brainy are dating, Kara and Lena are three steps away from U-Hauling to a remote cabin with four cats, and I need to text Lana in three hours. And then we also have to get Lena’s swing straight.”

    “Oh, I thought her swing was fine,” Brainy says. “Just unlucky today.”

    Lena laughs, loudly, and feels undeniably happy, even though it’s a Friday and her tournament is over before it ever really began.

    MINNESOTA, UNITED STATES - TPC TWIN CITIES - NORTH STAR OPEN

    Minnesota is about five degrees warmer than Canada and just as humid. Lena feels it as soon as she jogs down the steps of the private jet, and hears Jack groan in unhappiness behind her. This is her first time at this particular tournament - Lex and Lillian had never let Sam schedule it for her, placed in the interim between two majors as it was. Lex had also called it a “backwoods redneck experience.” So, that had been that. 

    It takes about twenty minutes for the four of them to get loaded into the private car waiting for them at the private terminal, and then they wait for another twenty minutes in the valet area for Kara to arrive. When she does come bounding into vision, Lena feels breath literally let out of her lungs. 

    “Heyo!” Kara says, happily, her bright yellow hat askew on her blonde head. It reads, inexplicably, Bad Dad in a cursive font. The minute she’s within grasping distance of Lena, she’s pulled into Kara’s strong, warm arms - pressing her forehead into the solid line of Kara’s collarbone. 

    “Hey,” Lena says, for lack of anything better to say. She’s sure Kara understands just as well, considering the gentle kiss placed against Lena’s temple and the squeeze of her arms. When they draw away, Kara moves on to Brainy, who she does a complex handshake with, and Nia, and then Jack. The two of them clap each other on the back repeatedly in some approximation of the most annoying male greeting. 

    “How was your flight?” Jack asks, as the driver puts Kara’s two small bags into the trunk and they all slide back into the vehicle. Kara has climbed into the back seat with Brainy, leaving the two captain’s chairs to Nia and Lena. 

    “Fun! I’ve never flown first class before,” Kara says. “The guy next to me recognized me, actually. He was flying in for a funeral, which was sad, but he had a lot of thoughts about how women’s golf was inherently superior to men’s golf.”

    “Love that for him,” Nia says. She’s flipping through something on her phone at maximum speed - Lena thinks she sees her own face at some point. 

    “Because women can’t hit the ball as far, it’s not a strength test and it’s more like how the game was originally intended to be played,” Kara continues. 

    “I don’t know enough to know if I love that for him,” Nia says. Next to Kara, Brainy makes a humming noise.

    “While he is correct that the game of golf and most of the traditional courses were designed to be played when the sport’s technology was not as advanced and physical fitness was not as prized, the underlying misogyny of the assumption that women are not as strong does remain problematic,” Brainy says. “He is correct but it is not coming from the correct place.”

    “Then I do not love that for him,” Nia says. 

    Lena settles into her seat and watches what is apparently Minnesota rush by, listening to her friends and her girlfriend chatter on. It feels startlingly perfect.

    -

    Their hotel is some distance away from the actual golf course, and so the tour professionals and their staffs are forced onto buses that take them to the area. This is explained at length when they check-in on Sunday, and yet, somehow, Lena very nearly misses Monday’s bus. 

    The reason, of course, was Kara. Kara, who had pulled Lena back into bed after her morning shower and spent a good portion of an hour teasing Lena to hell and back. By the time she realizes she’s about to miss the bus, she’s come twice. Kara has to help her get dressed, grinning like a cat with a canary, and when she comes sprinting onto the bus with a minute to spare, several golfers and their staff laugh at her. But it’s jocular. Lena doesn’t feel a lick of anxiety, mysteriously.

    She drops into the empty seat next to Jack. He doesn’t say anything until the bus has pulled away from the curb, leaning over and into her shoulder.

    “So, how’s Kara?” he asks. Lena rolls her eyes and elbows him hard enough that he spills his coffee on his pants.

    On Wednesday night, Lena and Kara are walking through the upscale mall attached to their hotel when they run into Lillian Luthor. They’re outside a Peloton store, Kara is insisting that they could get two of them so they could race each other, and Lena is insisting that because the bike records times, two bikes was definitely not necessary, and then, Lillian Luthor is exiting the Coach store with that moron Otis Graves carrying bags behind her. 

    “Lena, how surprising to see you here,” Lillian says, seconds after Kara grips Lena’s hand so hard they both wince. When Lena turns to face her mother, the woman is standing there with a bag on her arm and sunglasses on her head. She blends in so well with the crowd of wealthy white women populating the mall that she could have walked on past without Lena noticing. 

    “You’re surprised to see me at a golf tournament I’m registered to compete in?” Lena asks. Lillian looks to the ceiling as if to indicate her amusement. Next to Lena, Kara shuffles unhappily. Just seconds ago, Kara had been as carefree as ever, listing off her top ten favorite Peloton instructors. Now, she’s tense and clearly angry. Lena can practically feel it wafting off her. 

    “You never wanted to participate in this one! And I’ve only just arrived a few hours ago to support Eve,” Lillian says. “You remember Otis?”

    Lena remembers Otis staring at her chest when she was seventeen; Otis grins and Lena looks away without acknowledging him. 

    “Lovely,” Lena says. “Well, we have dinner reservations, mother, so I’m afraid we can’t stay and talk.”

    “At five? Surely that’s no good for your eating patterns,” Lillian says. “Though, perhaps your barbarous beau is the one with the appetite.”

    Lena feels herself heat up like a firecracker. Next to her, Kara scoffs audibly, tugging on Lena’s hand gently to pull her away, clearly done with the interaction. But Lillian, as is her wont, is not done until someone cries.

    “Miss Danvers, you should really thank my son for not pressing charges against you,” Lillian says airily. “He only held off out of the goodness of his heart - considering your previous employment situation and your life’s course, he thought it best to not add to your miseries.”

    For a moment, Lena feels the distance between Lillian and her yawn as wide as a canyon. Their relationship had never been marked by closeness, and its intimacies were not especially positive. But Lillian had been the woman who raised Lena, for better and for mostly worse. She had learned how to sit in a chair with a dress on without flashing the room from this woman. She had memorized the periodic table with this woman’s help. And yet she feels the distance between them grow so big that it feels like she’s looking at a stranger, and - Lena is angry with this stranger.

    “Don’t talk to her,” Lena snaps, letting go of Kara’s hand and pushing into Lillian’s space. Her hands feel like furnaces, like if she swung on Lillian, she’d brand her. But she doesn’t. She looks up at Lillian Luthor and squares her jaw and chin the same way she had learned watching the same woman. 

    “Oh, Lena, there’s no need to be territorial,” Lillian says. 

    “I’m serious, mother,” Lena says. “Don’t talk to her, or about her. I’d prefer you not talk to me as well, actually.”

    “You sound like a morose teen all over again, darling,” Lillian says. But Lena sees her eyes dart around them, as though gauging the crowd for how much they’re seeing, or what it is exactly they’re seeing. Behind Lillian, Otis looks torn between intervening and running away.

    “I don’t care what I sound like. I am telling you to stay away from me. I’m done with you, and with Lex, and both of your bullshit,” Lena says. “At the end of this season, I’m going to ruin you. Both of you. Because you nearly ruined me, and you don’t deserve to get to do that to Eve, or to anyone else, ever again.”

    “Always so dramatic,” Lillian drawls, rolling her eyes.

    “I’m done,” Lena says, warning on her tone. “Tell Lex I hope his face falls off.”

    “If you’re done,” Lillian says, coldness seeping in. “You’re saying goodbye to your family. Is that really what you want, Lena?”

    “I’d hardly call what you gave me family,” Lena says. “Bye, mother.”

    And Lena turns away, grabbing for Kara’s hand and leaving her mother behind.

Kara doesn’t say anything on the walk through the displays, and doesn’t say anything when Lena finds herself in an alcove showing off every charcuterie board style known to man itself. She doesn’t say anything when Lena feels herself suck in a breath, or when Lena wipes at a stray tear. Kara wraps her arms around Lena, tight, and lets her cry, and doesn’t say a word.

    Two days later, and she’s paired with M’gann for the final round, the two of them tied at -14. It’s been a solid go at it - it didn’t feel like spectacular golf, but it didn’t feel lucky either, like she was hanging on for dear life. Kara had been gracious enough to wake her up with her hand halfway in Lena’s sleep shorts. It’s humid as hell. The par three’s hole location has moved to a back right spot that she finds regretful.

    “I think six,” Kara says, holding Lena’s yardage book out between them. They’re waiting for the pair in front to finish up at the green, hovering at the edge of the tee box. M’gann and J’onn are surely going through the same debate on the opposite side of the box. “On Friday when you hit five it went over.”

    “The wind is against us,” Lena says. 

    “And right,” Kara reminds her.

    “I don’t like the six,” Lena says. “I don’t want to land short and I chipped up fine from the backside Friday, if I even make it that far.”

    “Brainy said the wind was swirling, you could get pushed to the bunker,” Kara says, but she’s jostling through Lena’s clubs anyway, pulling out the shiny length of the five iron and then reaching into her bib pocket for Lena’s glove case.

There’s silence for a few moments from Kara as she pulls it on, flexing her fingers into the fresh leather and pulling the wrist closure on tight before gripping her club. She’s always liked the tackiness of a new glove on a freshly gripped club.

She hadn’t quite noticed, the past few weeks, but now that Kara’s seen fit to point it out, it feels obvious. Even when her game hasn’t been going her way, she’s felt fine, like she can try anything. Lena has studied this hole all week along with all the rest of them and she knows she can make a shot from any given location along its length. She hasn’t felt anything like that for years. Maybe ever.

    She swallows. Kara bumps gently into her hip.

    “You okay?” Kara asks, voice quiet.

    “Yeah,” Lena says, too quickly, and then really stops to contemplate it. But the answer is the same. “Yeah, I’m good.”

    Lena’s shot does end up in the bunker. 

    M’gann’s ends up way up on the sloping green, a tough downhill shot to the flag awaiting her. But Lena is thinking about her ball while Kara wipes off her club head and they walk. When they come up to the deep bunker, piled up against the hill the green sits on, it’s immediately apparent her ball is plugged to high hell.

    “Motherfucker,” Kara mutters. “Well, babe, prepare to get sandy.”

    “Great,” she deadpans, taking her sixty degree wedge when Kara hands it over, walking up the short hill to see how the green slopes from her angle. It looks more manageable than M’gann’s shot on the other end, and Kara hums as she jogs back down the hill.

    “You’re good to go,” Kara says, from somewhere behind her. Lena plants one foot into the sand of the steep wall, checks her angle by glancing at the top of the flag where it gently shifts in the wind. She feels herself smiling, somehow, and that’s another revelation: she’s excited, happy, ready to blast this ball out of the sand and hopefully into the hole. If not - who cares? It’s a Sunday, she’s on a golf course with her girlfriend, and she’s having fun. Holy hell.

    She leans back in her stance, tilts her grip as much as she can, mimes a swing, and then does swing. She takes a metric ton of sand with her shot and a good deal of it lands on her and into her face, but she knows she’s hit the ball up and out as she slips backwards and falls on her ass - it’s just a matter of where the ball is going. 

    She gets her answer when she hears the crowd’s whoa sound interrupted with the plasticky sound of the flag getting struck, followed by an absolute uproar. It makes her laugh, glancing to the side where fans are jumping up and down at the ropes, and then Lena’s wrapped up in a tight, sweaty hug from behind, Kara squeezing her so hard that she ends up pulling her up to her feet. There is sand stuck to every available part of her body, including her face, but Kara still spins her around and kisses her hard anyway.

    At the photocall, Lena gets to hold two trophies, and she smiles obligingly as she does. The bigger, shinier one is ceremonial and stays with the clubhouse, but she does get to keep the smaller, less shiny one. She lets Nia hold it when she and Brainy get through the ropes to come meet her and Kara.

    “That was audacious, Lena Luthor,” Brainy says as he’s striding up, and then he pulls her into a sudden, unexpected hug. “I am proud.”

    “We don’t have to do this,” Kara says. 

    “What do you mean, we don’t have to do this? We’re here,” Lena says, but her hands don’t move and neither does any part of her body. Kara reaches out to grasp Lena’s forearm.

    “We don’t have to do this,” Kara repeats firmly. “Listen to me. Yes, we’re here, but if you can’t do this or you don’t want to, I will get back in the car and I’ll drive anywhere you want me to. I’m the only person who will ever know and we’ll never have to come back here if you don’t want to.”

    “My whole life, I’ve wanted to know - and now - ”

    “Just breathe for a second,” Kara says, and then she reaches over Lena’s lap to grab the bottle of water they’d been sharing on the drive. “Drink some water.”

    Lena does. It calms a little. When she looks up the hill and sees that imposing building, she feels it all come right back.

    “I feel like I remember this place,” Lena says, clutching the water bottle in her fingers. “Or - I remember feeling bad in this place.”

    “An early nineties Irish Catholic church in a small town? Shocking, considering how horny you are for women,” Kara says. It abruptly pulls Lena from her own head, and she smacks at Kara’s shoulder. “Listen, I get it. Being in places that remind you of bad things, where bad things happened, it sucks. Especially stuff this bad. I don’t remember where my parents are buried in their cemetery because it freaks me out too much to remember it in the first place. So really, I get it.”

    “I must have been here for the funeral,” Lena sighs, squeezing at Kara’s hand. Kara hums.

    “Probably,” Kara says. “Do you want me to drive around for a bit so you can think?”

    “No,” Lena says, without thought. It’s firm. Firmer than she had realized she felt. She reaches down to unclip her seat belt, and Kara holds her hand as she hoists herself up and out of the low car. It is…a really nice car, admittedly. “God, why are there so many steps to get up there?”

    “It’s called a metaphor,” Kara says, lacing their fingers together and shutting the car door behind them.

    Kara has a map of the graveyard, somehow. It takes a minute to get up all the steps, but it takes half that to find the well-maintained grave labelled ELIZABETH WALSH, loving mother, 1967-1997. The graves here have small gravel patches overtop, and her mother’s is decorated with flowers and…golf balls. Golf balls with things written on them, by the looks of it - and Lena leans down to pluck one up, seeing without seeing.

    The writing is a bit faded, and it’s small and scrabbled by the dimples of the ball, but still, she can read it: your Lena won! with the year Lena won the low amateur at Bethpage Black inscribed under it.

    “Whoa there,” Kara says, right after Lena pushes the ball into her hand and drops heavily to the ground. She’s not lightheaded or anything, just - gravity feels huge right now. Kara sits next to her, rolling the ball over in her hand and laughing softly when she reads the writing. Lena watches as she leans forward to put the ball back on the gravel before she reaches for two others clustered under a pretty bunch of flowers. When she turns them over in her hands, she makes a sound. 

    “What?” Lena asks. Somehow she knows already before Kara slides them into her hand. 

    Major 2 of 4 down! and the other: THE MASTERS! And a handsome girlfriend to boot!

    “Wow,” Kara says. Lena nods, rolling them back onto the gravel and looking back at the headstone. Loving mother. Thirty years old. 

    “Someone knows me, then,” Lena says. Kara hums and nods in Lena’s peripheral vision.

    “Someone loves you, you mean,” Kara says, reaching for another golf ball, even fresher: US Open win - next The Open. Put a little luck in the ground for her E.

    -

    They go to one of the two pubs in town. Lena orders a dusty scotch from the highest shelf, while Kara gets a Guinness, and they settle at a tiny table in the corner of the creaky old room. Lena can’t help but imagine her mother here, sipping her own drink with a friend. Did she like scotch? Or did Lena inherit that from her father? Had her father been here? Everything she knew of him told her there was no chance of that, but still. What did she know, really? 

    “Ooh, watch,” Kara says, nudging at Lena and drawing her attention to a television in the corner, showing what Lena immediately recognized as her hit out of the bunker just a few weeks ago. The plume of sand and Lena falling on her ass were distinctive. 

    “I’ve seen it plenty,” Lena mutters. Kara laughs at her, slinging her arm over her shoulders and drawing her into her side. Lena lets her. It’s nice. 

    “But you looked so cute with the sand all over your face,” Kara says, followed by a small comfortable silence. “How are you doing?”

    “Fine,” Lena says. “I think I’m just…adjusting. It’s a whole new world suddenly. Do not start singing in this pub right now.”

    Kara huffs at being cut off at the knees, her fingers brushing up and down on the knob of Lena’s shoulder. 

    “I’m really proud of you,” Kara offers. Lena accepts it with a tilt of her head, resting it back against Kara’s shoulder. They sit like that for a while, sipping their drinks and watching television as the commentators debate if Lena can go ahead and finish the in-season sweep. They are plenty dubious, understandably. And Lena - well, she wants to win The Open, now more than ever, and yet, golf is so far from her mind right now. 

    “Decky, have you seen the car parked out front of your pub? If we made off with it, I think we’d fund the lads’ trip to Belfast no question,” says a voice at some point. A man has come ambling in, mousy brown hair tousled on his head, followed closely by another man and a woman.

    “Dara, I’d invite you to use your common sense,” the bartender returns with a laugh. He doesn’t cast a glance in Kara and Lena’s direction, but Lena gets the sense he knows it’s their car. Instead, he turns to grab three pint glasses at once. 

    “The lads’ trip to Belfast is already paid anyway,” the other man says. He’s graying to balding, in a loose jacket.

    “Out of our own pockets!” Dara says, looking aggrieved.

    “That is how most things are paid, you dose,” the woman says. “And anyway, I’m sure you’ve gone and frightened some lost tourist with your talk of making off with their car - ”

    The woman looks around the pub then. It’s not quite busy, and it’s not really empty either, and yet, she somehow seems to look right at Lena and Kara immediately. Her face sparks something in Lena’s brain that makes her sit up from where she’s been lazing against Kara, and it’s obvious the woman recognizes her. 

    “I’ll be damned,” she says.

    “You will, that is true,” Dara says. But the other man has turned to look too - and he also looks familiar, somehow, and he smacks at Dara’s arm hard enough that the man yelps. “Christ! Oscar, you know my elbow is banjaxed, and you go - oh.”

    They’re left with three people staring straight at them looking poleaxed. Lena isn’t really sure what to say. So she’s thankful when Kara speaks.

    “Uh, hi?”

    “Hello,” Dara says. “You - ah, for feck’s sake Aileen, don’t just start crying, you’ll scare the lass!”

    “You’re in a right mood today, you eejit,” Oscar says, shoving at Dara and gently brushing past Aileen, who’s wiping at her face frantically. He takes a step toward them, and smiles gently “Miss…well. Ah.”

    Lena doesn’t really know where it comes from. But she stands up and extends her hand out toward him. 

    “Lena,” she says. He looks at her, and then her hand, and then her again, and now that he’s closer, he really does look familiar. His face is kind and weathered. And his eyes are a fantastically bright shade of blue that reminds Lena of Kara’s immediately. Somewhere in the middle of her, Lena knows she had once adored this man. 

    “Oscar Shanley,” he says, taking her hand. He has a strong grip, just as weathered as his face. “Pleased to see you again, Lena.”

    -

    Lena finds herself in the back garden of Dara and Aileen Kerwin’s home an hour later, plucking through a takeaway bag of chips with Kara. In front of her, Aileen has dumped piles of…stuff. Photos, largely, but also -

    “Wow, look at the font on this,” Kara says, reaching in and plucking up a scorecard. When Lena looks at it, the familiar Portmarnock logo is emblazoned at the top, along with a garish font and design best left to the 1980s.

    “You kept all these?” Lena says, grasping for another one. Her mother’s name is written in the first line, followed by Dara, Aileen and Oscar. 

    “Oh, we had to keep track of wins and losses, or Dara would have eaten our heads off,” Aileen says airily. There’s a huffing sound as her husband comes into view and plops into the seat next to her, sliding two bottles of cider their way before handing her a glass of wine. “Thank you, dear.”

    “Your mother hardly cared to keep score,” Oscar adds. He’d been the one to sit down and explain that they knew Lena, that they had known her mother, that they had golfed up and down the coast with her in a foursome. That they’d been the ones maintaining her grave, leaving the golf balls and flowers. 

    “On account of her being better than all of us,” Dara says. Lena shuffles past the scorecards and pulls a photograph out, then nearly drops it. It’s her, she thinks, small and settled on her mother’s lap with a one-tooth grin and one hand extended toward the camera. Kara wraps an arm around her waist without a word. 

    “Yes, that she was,” Oscar sighs.

    “Was I born here?” Lena asks, abruptly. 

    “Oh, no, you were born in Dublin,” Aileen says. “Elizabeth never much liked to talk about it, but your grandparents ran her out when you appeared on the horizon.”

    “And she ran all the way out here with you. Ach, you were so darling. Look at this little lass,” Dara says, pulling a photo from the pile and handing it over. It’s Lena again, standing victoriously on a low garden wall holding a stuffed bear in one hand and grinning again. Oscar is hovering at her side clearly ready to catch her if she falls, sunglasses perched on his head. 

    “What did she do for work?” Lena asks. 

    “Oh, part-time at a little bookshop up in Balbriggan,” Aileen says. “She used to take you with her on occasion, when the three of us weren’t free. Their golf club is grand.”

    “Grand? For feck’s sake, Aileen, the girl’s played Augusta," Dara says.

    “She’s won Augusta,” Oscar corrects. Kara laughs a little, reaching for a chip from their bag and thumbing at Lena’s hip. 

    “I must have missed that, Oscar, you great dope,” Dara says, glaring at him. He looks unrepentant. 

    “I’ve played lots of places,” Lena says. “I met Kara on what has to be the worst course in Washington.”

    “Okay, rude,” Kara mutters, reaching for her cider. “Says the girl who played it four times in a week.”

    “Couldn’t think of a reason other than the golf she might’ve done that,” Aileen says, teasing. Lena finds herself blushing and grasping for another photograph. This one is her mother, and the three people in front of her, arms wrapped around each other on a course somewhere. 

    “You look so much like her,” Dara says, more softly than he’s said anything before this. “Even if we hadn’t known you’d been picked up by Leo, it’d have been obvious.” 

    Lena finds herself laughing, shaking her head.

    “Leo?” she asks. “You called my father Leo?”

    “It’s how he introduced himself, when he visited,” Aileen says, somewhat carefully. Lena frowns, looking down at the life laid out before her. There was so much to know.

    “How often did he visit?” Lena asks.

    “Every summer. Just a week,” Aileen says. Lena sighs, leaning toward Kara’s warmth. What did that mean? That her father had loved her? That he had loved her mother? That he had hated Lillian, or Lex? 

    “And we were here every day,” Dara says. It sounds like an old argument, one that burns just the slightest. Who might she have been, if she’d stayed here, under the care of these people? 

    “There’s no need to get into that,” Oscar says, a soft reprimand. “So, Lee, why’d you come out here now, after all these years? Move at your own pace, of course, but we…thought you might’ve come sooner.”

    “I didn’t know she was here,” Lena says. “I didn’t even - know her name.”

    The three of them look at her, and then at each other.

    “Ach, I knew that woman was the devil herself, and I told Leo as such, and what did he say - ”

    “Quit giving out, Dara,” Oscar interrupts, sterner than before. 

    “I agree, Lillian is the devil herself,” Kara says cheerfully, looking down at a scorecard she’s plucked off the table.

    “That she is! We should’ve taken that woman up with placing a curse on her, I told you as much," Dara says. Aileen and Oscar both roll their eyes. Lena laughs, shaking her head.

    “I’m not sure a curse could have taken Lillian down,” Lena says.

    “Irish curses are grand powerful,” Dara says, grinning wildly. 

    Lena has to allow that.

    Eventually, she and Kara have to leave. Lena promises to come back after The Open, Dara says you best come back with that trophy, and Aileen hugs her hard. Oscar walks them out, claiming his house is on the way back to the pub where they left the Maserati parked. 

    The town is small. Compact rows of homes swirling around in winding streets. There’s a fine mist in the air as the sun starts to set. Kara holds Lena’s hand, swinging it back and forth as she looks around with interest, but Lena finds herself looking at Oscar. He’s tall, in a sweater and jeans, his hands in his pockets. Somehow, Lena really does feel like she remembers him, in a different way than Dara and Aileen.

    “Did you want to see the house you lived in?” Oscar asks, as they come to a crossroads. He clearly is expecting her answer, because he smiles when she nods. “Sorry to say, it’s not so different from any of the rest of these.”

    “It’s still nice to know,” Lena says. “I don’t remember much of anything about this place, so it’s…nice to re-remember.”

    “There’s not much to remember, in your defense,” Oscar says, his grin a little crooked when he looks over at her. “Has anything seemed familiar?”

    “Oh, well, I had a panic attack when I saw the church,” Lena says. Oscar laughs at that.

    “That’d make sense. You were not in the finest of moods at Elizabeth’s funeral. A right terror, in fact, which was to be expected. I ended up carrying you out during the mass and Leo promised to buy you a pony to get you to calm down,” Oscar says. “Did you ever get that pony, by the way?”

    Lena laughs softly. She can imagine a frantic father trying to reason with his young and hysterical daughter, offering anything he can think of if she’d just stop crying, but - it’s a lot harder to imagine Lionel in that scene. He’d been so…distant. Not just from her, but from all of them.

    “I did, actually,” Lena says. She’d started riding lessons a few years after she’d been adopted. Lillian had called it a waste of time and money, considering Lena was also doing fencing, violin, chess, and golf, but her father had suggested it and Lena had wanted to. Anything to get out from under Lex and Lillian’s thumb had seemed like freedom when she was nine, and neither of them held much interest in horses. “I named him Dickens.”

    “You did dressage on a horse named Dickens?” Kara asks incredulously. “Lena, this is getting embarrassing.”

    “Shut up,” Lena says, using their joined hands to shove at her. Kara stumbles off the curb and onto the street, making an affronted gasp the whole way. “I liked A Christmas Carol!”

    “Oh, your therapist is gonna love that,” Kara says, stepping back up onto the sidewalk and grabbing ahold of Lena by the waist. “The layers there are crazy.”

    “My therapist is going to hear about you, you ass,” Lena mutters. Kara snorts, but hooks her hand on Lena’s hip as they amble along and Lena does nothing to stop her. 

    “Dickens is a bit pomp, isn’t it?” Oscar says. Lena gasps while Kara laughs. She’s trying to come up with a response, but Oscar pulls to a stop, patting at a now familiar looking front garden wall. “This is me. That was you.”

    He points to the house right next door. Nothing about it looks particularly remarkable, and it hardly springs any random memories, but still. It’s nice to see it, especially compared to the pictures. 

    “So we were neighbors?” Lena asks. Oscar nods, tapping at the stone work of his garden wall and humming something. “Were you…”

    She’s not even sure what she’s wanting to ask, but somehow, Oscar seems to find the question anyway.

    “Do you know, your mum was the first person I said I’m gay to?” Oscar says. Lena blinks. “We were sitting at her kitchen table folding up laundry together - she was so harried with you, you were making a big fuss about something - ”

    “Typical,” Kara interjects, which Lena smacks at her for -

    “ - and she had to feed you, but she looked plenty weary to do it in front of me, and I just said, I’m gay, actually, Elizabeth, so I don’t care much for your breasts. And I’d never said that before to anyone. I’d planned to never say it to anyone, to be honest,” Oscar says. “And she said, oh thank goodness, keep folding that, and that was it.”

    Lena does her best not to burst into sobs, but she clearly only half-succeeds. Kara pulls her in tight, humming quietly in a comforting tone as she runs a hand up and down Lena’s back. Oscar smiles at her when she looks back to him, only to find that he’s crying too, which makes her crying worse.

    “Elizabeth was my best friend, and don’t tell the other two, ‘cause Aileen would eat my head off, but I’d bet she’d say the same of me,” Oscar says. “I spent a lot of time looking after you. In fact, I tried to convince Leo to let me adopt you, but he - well, he was a stubborn man.”

    “So it’s genetic,” Kara jokes, prompting a laugh from Oscar and Lena. 

    “Well, Elizabeth was plenty bullheaded as well,” Oscar says, smiling and wiping at his face. “Anyway. My husband is away on business, or else I’d invite you in to meet him.”

    “Some other time,” Lena says. Her voice is hoarse, but she clears her throat. “It’s been…really great to see you again.”

    “It has,” Oscar says, smiling.
